<!-- page 2 -->

In the name of God, the Most Merciful, the Most Compassionate.

This is a book presenting a concise collection of the authentic narrations reported from the righteous Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt — upon Muhammad, the Chosen One, and upon them be the prayers of the Almighty, the All-Forgiving — through the hours of the night and the ends of the day.

Authored by our master, Qadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad ibn Haywan al-Taymi, may God sanctify his soul.

May his intercession and forgiveness be our sustenance. Amen, O Allah, Amen.

<!-- page 3 -->

In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful.

All praise belongs to God for His abundant grace, benevolence, and favor. We give thanks so that He may increase His bounty, and we seek His pleasure and mercy. May God bless Muhammad, the seal of His prophets and messengers, and bless Ali ibn Abi Talib, his successor in his land and his guardian over his family, and the Imams from his pure descendants — the rightly-guided Mahdi Caliphs through whom God established the pillars of religion, guided those who turned to guidance, and illuminated the beacons of Islam for the Muslims. They inherited the Imamate until the Day of Judgment; they are the exemplars and the authorities, the masters of the people, and the leaders appointed for every community in every age. Upon all of them be the prayers of God, and may God have mercy upon their forebears, followers, the earliest believers, and those who came after.

Al-Qadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad said: I had gathered from the sayings of the followers of the pure Imams of the Household of the Messenger of God — may God's peace and blessings be upon him — numerous books encompassing the knowledge of the People of the House. I compiled and arranged them, setting aside narrations in which the transmitters disagreed, and establishing what is authentically confirmed from them by bringing forth its evidence, proofs, and demonstrations. These works grew large in number of volumes, and their contents exceeded the reach of those who sought them. They were further abridged in balanced summaries that, even so, proved too extensive for many students to master in full — a fact that certain individuals exploited in order to circulate shortened versions.

I was then asked — by none other than judges, rulers, guardians, and students of knowledge of the lawful and the unlawful — to compile for them an intermediate work on these matters: one that brings its meanings within reach, suffices in place of its larger sources, is easy to read, can be memorized by the young student, provides benefit to the elder scholar, can be accommodated by the occupied, and is accessible to both the poor and the wealthy. So I wrote this book for them, balanced between prolixity and brevity, and I named it the *Mukhtasar al-Athar* — the Summary of the Narrations.

When I considered the scope of this book, I found it necessary to limit the inclusion of full chains of transmission within it, yet I also saw that it could not be entirely dispensed with. I therefore resolved to confine myself to the nearest available chain of transmission,

<!-- page 4 -->

raising it, verifying it, and establishing it — then drawing from it whatever I wished to confirm — for the guardian and Imam of the time, the master of the age, our lord the Commander of the Faithful, the Imam al-Mu'izz li-Din Allah, may God bless him and grant him peace, and bless his fathers, the pure Imams. I then omitted from the work whatever God caused to be dropped, and God perpetuated His command through that omission. What remained — what he was pleased with and commanded to be preserved — I established in this book. I recited it to him as it stands, and I narrate it on the authority of those who received it from me, on the authority of his pure fathers. For he has drawn illumination from the light of the Commander of the Faithful, and was nourished by water drawn from its very source.

<!-- page 5 -->

In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful.

**On the merits of seeking knowledge and the etiquette thereof:**

God Almighty said: *"Allah will raise those of you who believe and those who have been given knowledge by degrees"* [Surah al-Mujadalah: 11]. The Almighty also said: *"Are those who know equal to those who do not know? Only those possessed of understanding take heed"* [Surah al-Zumar: 9]. He further said: *"Rather, it is clear signs preserved in the breasts of those who have been given knowledge"* [Surah al-Ankabut: 49].

The Messenger of God — may God's peace and blessings be upon him — said: "Whomever God intends good for, He grants understanding of religion." He said: "Four things are obligatory upon every person possessed of reason and intellect: to listen to knowledge, to memorize it —" He said: "Learn from the scholars of my household, for in learning from the scholars of my household you will be saved from the Fire." He said: "There is no rest in life save for one who speaks with knowledge or listens with understanding." He said: "This banner is carried in every generation by those upright in faith who serve as its enemies' adversaries — they repel the distortions of the ignorant, the false interpretations of those led astray, and the misappropriations of the fraudulent." He said: "When a man sets out in search of knowledge, God records good deeds for him; and when he and the scholar meet and the scholar reminds him of what God has commanded, the angels spread their wings over them and call out from above: God has forgiven you."

He said: "The first stage of knowledge is silence; the second is listening; the third is spreading it; and the fourth is acting upon it."

Ali — may God's prayers be upon him — said: "Seeking knowledge is obligatory upon every Muslim man and woman." He said: "Whoever buys and sells without understanding the religion has inevitably fallen into usury." He said: "Four qualities, if present in a person, complete his faith even if what lies between east and west is filled with his sins: the slave places his hope in none but his Lord, fears nothing but his own wrongdoing, the ignorant person is not ashamed to learn, and the scholar, when asked about something he does not know, says, 'I do not know.'"

It is reported from Ja'far ibn Muhammad — may God's prayers be upon him — that he said: "Seek knowledge, and adorn it with forbearance and reverence. Be humble toward those who teach you knowledge, and do not be arrogant scholars, lest your falsehood eclipse your truth." He said: "If a young man from our Shia does not —"

<!-- page 6 -->

— demonstrate sound understanding of religion and good character, then he falls short. He said: "The believer continues to inherit knowledge and refined conduct from his family until he causes all of them to enter Paradise, missing not a single one among them — young or old, servant or neighbor." And conversely, the disobedient slave continues to inherit ignorance and bad character from his family until he causes all of them to enter the Fire, missing not a single one among them — young or old, servant or neighbor.

**On the merits of purity:**

God Almighty said: *"Indeed, God loves those who constantly repent and loves those who purify themselves"* [Surah al-Baqarah: 222]. The Almighty said: *"Within it are men who love to purify themselves, and God loves those who purify themselves"* [Surah al-Tawbah: 108]. He said: *"And He sent down upon you rain from the sky to purify you thereby, and to remove the harm of Satan from you, and to fortify your hearts and make your feet firm thereby"* [Surah al-Anfal: 11].

The Messenger of God — may God's peace and blessings be upon him — said: "God does not accept prayer except with purity." He said: "God will gather my community on the Day of Resurrection among the nations, distinguished by the traces of ablution." He said: "When I was taken up to the heavens, it was said to me while the denizens of the highest heaven were disputing — I said, 'I do not know.' I was then told of the virtue of performing ablution at the ablution basin, of walking to congregational prayers, and of waiting for the next prayer after completing one."

From Ali — may God's prayers be upon him — it is reported that he said: "Purity is half of faith." He said: "Whoever purifies himself and then walks to the mosque is in a state of prayer unless something occurs requiring him to renew it. It is performed before every prayer; if nothing occurs to break it, then the virtue and reward of purity is sought for what has been established."

<!-- page 7 -->

**On the purity and impurity of water:**

God Almighty said: *"And We sent down from the sky pure water"* [Surah al-Furqan: 48]. The Almighty said: *"And He sent down upon you from the sky water to purify you thereby"* [Surah al-Anfal: 11]. The Almighty also said: *"Have you not seen that God sent down water from the sky, then caused it to flow as springs in the earth?"* [Surah al-Zumar: 21] — thereby indicating that the water of the earth originates from the sky, and reports have confirmed its purity.

The Messenger of God — may God's peace and blessings be upon him — said: "Water is a purifier; nothing renders it impure." He said concerning the sea: "Its water is a purifier and its dead are lawful" — meaning what is extracted from it of that which is permissible to eat, that which dies after being extracted from it, not that which dies within it. For as God Almighty said: *"Lawful to you is the game of the sea and its food, as provision for you and for travelers"* [Surah al-Ma'idah: 96] — that is, what is extracted from it and eaten outside, not what dies under the water or at its floor. By "its dead," he means what dies after it has been brought out alive; but what dies within the water before it is hunted is not permissible to eat, according to the position of the Ahl al-Bayt — may God's prayers be upon them all — and that is what floats. We will address this in the chapter on foods, God willing.

As for water that occurs naturally without any human intervention, it is pure. God Almighty stated in His Book and through the speech of His Messenger — may God's peace and blessings be upon him — that if something is cast into water and alters it, that water is referred back to the attribute of what has been cast into it, and the ruling of water is removed from it, replaced by the ruling of whatever has predominated. This is analogous to water into which dates, raisins, or honey have been thrown: if these do not change the water and are not apparent in it, its ruling remains the ruling of water — until what was cast into it becomes evident and manifests, changing the color or taste of the water, at which point it becomes a fermented drink, the name of water ceases to apply to it, and purification with it is no longer permissible. I know of no disagreement among Muslims on this matter, except for the position unique to Abu Hanifah and those among his followers who agreed with him. He said: It is permissible to perform ablution with date wine specifically, but not with other cooked beverages. Abu Yusuf disagreed with him on this, saying: Ablution is not valid except with

<!-- page 8 -->

water. They performed ablution with date wine and then prayed — and this is a baseless position. For God Almighty said: *"Do not approach prayer while you are intoxicated"* [Surah al-Nisa': 43]. If date wine is not water, there is no meaning in using it for purification; and if it is water, there is no meaning in the qualification. Abu Hanifah was misled by the claim that the Messenger of God — may God's peace and blessings be upon him — performed ablution with date wine on the Night of the Jinn. This hadith is not established; and even if it were established, that wine would not be what people know today as wine. Rather, the Prophet — peace be upon him — passed by water from which he drank when no other water was available to him, and he would drink from it and perform ablution with it so long as it had not changed — and we have mentioned that this is acceptable and does not remove the ruling of water from it, except when it has been altered and others have attributed a different character to it.

This is likewise the case with milk that mixes with water: if the milk is not apparent in it, it remains water in its state until the milk prevails, at which point the ruling of milk applies and the name of water is removed from it. The same applies to whatever drinking substances or the like mix with water. For this reason, whatever impurity mixes with water, if it is apparent and changes something of the water's color, taste, or smell, the water becomes impure and takes on the ruling of that impurity. But if the impurity does not appear in it and is not discernible, the water remains in its state and it is permissible to drink from it and to use it for purification.

This is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt — may God's prayers be upon them — that is established from them and agreed upon by those among the companions of al-Shafi'i, the people of hadith, and many others attributed to jurisprudence who claim mastery of this knowledge. It is well established that water whose color, smell, or taste changes without impurity — such as water that stagnates in its course and thereby changes — retains its purity, because it has not changed through impurity.

Ali — may God's prayers be upon him — said: "Nothing defiles water; nothing renders it impure" — meaning so long as the ruling of water applies to it, as we have described. He was asked about running water that passes by a carcass, excrement, and blood: is ablution to be performed from it and drinking from it permissible? He said: "Yes — meaning so long as nothing from those things is apparent in it."

It is reported from a pond that animals urinated in it, drank from it, and bathed in its shallows, and he said: "There is no objection to that." The Messenger of God — may God's peace and blessings be upon him — once descended with his companions on a journey at a pond, and their animals urinated in it and bathed in it, yet they performed ablution from it, drank from it, and the Prophet — peace be upon him — was asked about a small pool nearby —


<!-- page 9 -->
The mosque is where the menstruating woman may reach in her hand, and the boy likewise. He said: "Perform ablution from it, for the water does not defile anything." He was asked about a pool situated near a village where boys and animals urinate in it and bathe in it, and he replied: "If something troubles your heart about it, do this" — and he gestured with his hand, meaning stir the water — "then drink and perform ablution, for the religion is not a narrowness, and Allah Almighty said: 'And He has not made for you any embarrassment' [al-Hajj: 78]." He was also asked about a carcass found in water, and he said: "If the water is abundant and there is no smell to it, then perform ablution from it." He said: "If the water reaches the depth of two arms in two arms, nothing renders it impure except that which prevails over it and changes its color, smell, or taste" — meaning from impurities. And there is much narrated on this subject. As for the ruling on a well in which an animal dies: if the water has changed, it is drawn out until the change is gone and the water prevails again; if nothing has changed, then it is acceptable. Whatever died in it should be removed, and care should be taken that it not change the water. This is the only valid ruling regarding water. As for those who disagree and would apply a fixed measure, this is not tenable, for if we grant them their principle of purity, then when the water of purification touches the face, the impurity present in the vessel would render it impure by contact — and by their own words and measurements, that water would become impure as well. Similarly, if there is excess water in contact with it, it too may become impure, so purification would never be achieved by their measure. The same applies to a garment washed in a vessel that is dyed and carries impurity: the water poured over it mixes with that impurity, and the garment would never be purified by their measure. And if something with blood in it falls into a well, they say a certain number of buckets must be drawn out, yet they also hold that the water equal in quantity to those buckets is impure, and that if any of it falls into another well, that well too becomes impure. If the specified number of buckets is drawn out without using a bucket, they say it is not purified; if drawn out by bucket, they say the bucket itself is purified while the water in the bucket remains impure until a great quantity of it is broken into the well. And I have been immersed in the well water they claimed was purified, while it contained a calamity of impure water — and by their own measure, it would have corrupted it. Such arguments are many, and the full account of what prevents satisfaction with their position is beyond the brevity our work requires.

<!-- page 10 -->
Mentioning what is obligatory for ablution and what is obligatory for ghusl:

Allah Almighty said: "O you who believe, when you stand up to pray, wash your faces and your hands to the elbows, and wipe your heads and your feet to the heels; and if you are in a state of janabah, then purify yourselves. But if you are ill or on a journey, or one of you has come from the place of relieving himself, or you have touched women and you find no water, then wipe your faces and hands with clean earth." [al-Ma'idah 6:6]

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: "If you stand up to pray" means rising from sleep, and "or one of you has come from the place of relieving himself" is a metaphor for the event of impurity — for the excrement-place was the ground to which people would go for their needs. And "or you have touched women" means intercourse, expressed by way of euphemism. If a person is in a state of purity, that purity is not invalidated except by sleep that overwhelms him and prevents him from being aware of what has happened; but one who dozes lightly while still conscious of his surroundings does not thereby invalidate his ablution. Ablution is invalidated by urine, faeces, and wind coming out of the posterior, and by anything that comes from the posterior — whether blood, pus, or otherwise — and this invalidates ablution. What comes from other than these two outlets — blood, vomit, pus, and the like — does not invalidate ablution. Ablution is not invalidated by touching with a stick, or by touching without intercourse, or by kissing, or by direct touching of a male organ, or by food or drink; however, one should wash the part of the body that has become impure. It is recommended to clean oneself after urination and other acts carrying an odor, without this being obligatory for the validity of prayer — rather, it is recommended for purification and cleanliness. As for one who is certain of purity and then doubts whether an invalidating event has occurred but does not know for certain, he need not perform ablution. But one who doubts purity after having been certain of impurity must perform ablution. All of this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them. They hold that ablution is not invalidated except by what comes from the two outlets, by the kiss, and by the preceding; as for al-madhi — the thin fluid that issues from the penis due to desire for intercourse — it requires only ablution,

<!-- page 11 -->
not ghusl. As for al-wudhi — the thin fluid that follows urine — it likewise requires only ablution and not ghusl. If a man is afflicted with a flow from his side, he should urinate before washing, and if after completing the ghusl something comes out of his urethra that is a remnant of janabah, he must repeat the ghusl even if he has already completed it. If urine has cleared out the remnants of janabah and something comes out thereafter, only ablution is required and not ghusl. One who has experienced a congregation or a wet dream should not perform ghusl until he urinates, for the reason that whoever dreams and finds wetness must perform ghusl even if he sees that he stepped in it and finds no moisture on himself. There is nothing required of him if he sees himself descending in sleep but ghusl is required only when semen has actually come out. As for one who bends without entry of the vulva while awake and does not experience emission, ghusl is not required of him unless emission occurs. However, if penetration of the vulva takes place — that is, if the glans disappears — ghusl is obligatory whether or not emission occurs, and the glans is the head of the male organ. All of this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them all.

Mentioning the qualities of ablution, ghusl, and their proper etiquette:

We know from the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that they were commanded to cover the 'awrah when going to relieve themselves, and to rely on whatever concealment is available, and to distance themselves as much as possible from people. They forbade urinating and defecating in stagnant water, on the banks of rivers and wells, on roads, under fruit trees, between graves, and in places where doing so would harm people. One who needs to relieve himself and cannot manage to face away from the qiblah should not do so while facing it, and should take care that nothing of that falls on himself or his garment. They instructed whoever enters a place of relieving himself, or intends to do so, to invoke Allah and seek refuge from the accursed devil, and when he finishes he should praise Allah and give thanks for what He has granted him of food and drink. They permitted urinating and defecating in basins and vessels, and ordered the istibra' from urine to expel what remains in the penis. If water is available, he should wash it; if not,

<!-- page 12 -->
he should wipe his urethra on pure ground, stone, cloth, or the like, or wipe with stones, clods of earth, or rags, or the like, if he has defecated — wiping repeatedly until he is clean and examines what he has wiped. If nothing remains, he ceases; and they forbade wiping with food, bones, dried dung, or fruits — meaning fruits not yet ripe, that is, unripe produce — or charcoal.

They instructed one who intends to perform ablution or ghusl to make the intention to do so; if he washes merely to cool down, or another person pours water over him and he does not intend the act for himself, he is not rewarded for it and must repeat what was obligatory. It is recommended for one who intends ablution to invoke the name of Allah at its beginning. Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, peace be upon him, said: "Whoever names Allah over his ablution purifies his whole body" — meaning it serves as expiation for sins, Allah willing — but if he neglects to do so, his ablution is not thereby spoiled. They ordered that the hands be washed before inserting them into the vessel; if they are already clean there is nothing required of him, but washing is better. One should also wash away any faeces, urine, or matter coming from the outlet before beginning; as for wind from the outlet, washing is not obligatory but is desirable, and some of them used to do it in order to gain its merit. One begins by washing the private parts and then moves downward to the posterior, removing any dirt or trace until it is gone, pouring water with the right hand and washing with the left, then rubbing with water after istibra' until the smell is gone. He should not use his right hand for istinja' except in the case of illness. He then rinses his mouth and inhales water into his nostrils three times, for this is of the Sunnah and should not be omitted. If it is omitted unintentionally, he should wash his face from top to bottom. The man gathers his beard in his hand and runs water through it; if the beard is thin, he washes it three times, and if he washes it two times or once he has washed it, though three is better. However, three is not superior in the sense of being obligatory, and likewise with washing the hands to the elbows — he does this three times,

<!-- page 13 -->
once is acceptable, and three is better, as we mentioned in washing the face; but again, three is not superior in the sense of obligation. If he has a ring on his hand, he must ensure the water reaches beneath it, and he should leave no part of his face or arms unwashed. The face is from the hairline of the forehead to the bottom of the chin, and from the tip of the sideburns to the lobes of the ears. He then wipes his head, facing forward, beginning from above and wiping toward the front of his head, then returning from where he started — wiping the hair cut from the nape — and this is done once; if he wipes three times it is better, though not obligatory. He likewise wipes his ears, both outward and inward. If he takes fresh water for a second wiping, he should then wipe the back of his feet in a wiping motion. This is the meaning of the word of Allah Almighty: "Wash your faces and hands to the elbows, and wipe your heads and your feet to the heels." [al-Ma'idah 6:6] — for the Almighty has made obligatory the washing of the face and hands, and the wiping of the head and legs. Even if he washes them and wipes over them, that is acceptable, and if he runs his fingers between them, that too is good and has reward. He should not perform one organ's act in place of another in ablution; he begins with what Allah began — the face, then the hands, then the head, then the legs — and if he begins with another order, he must go back and redo the first organ so that the sequence matches the original order. If during ablution he interrupts it for a matter that did not deceive him, and then completes the remaining parts, without repeating the ablution from the beginning — that is acceptable, and he need not rely on having interrupted his ablution and left some of it to be completed later. Rather, it is permissible for him to do so if he cut it off for a valid excuse, but not if he did so deliberately. As for the quantity of water used in ablution, there is no fixed limit, and people differ in this: some are content with a little, while others require more than that; and in the adornments of ablution it is preferable


<!-- page 14 -->
The organs of ablution must be thoroughly wetted with water as commanded; otherwise, the water merely passes over them without effect. The place where a hand or leg has been amputated is treated accordingly, and it is recommended to perform ablution before the time of prayer enters, so that one may be in a state of purity and pray at the outset of the appointed time. The scholars disapproved of wasteful use of water in ablution, calling it a stirring of the devils, yet they permitted drying oneself with a garment after the wetness of ablution and ghusl. One should invoke Allah and recite the prescribed supplications while washing each member of ablution, and remember Allah upon completing it. The scholars of the Ahl al-Bayt forbade wiping over socks and were firm in that position, holding that those who wipe over socks have overlooked the relevant hadith, or have accepted a narration that contradicts the Book of Allah the Almighty. For Allah the Almighty commands wiping over the feet, and whoever has a disease in his legs should wipe water over them. As for wiping over slippers, splints, or bandages placed over limbs of ablution — if these are removed or broken, the wiping is rendered void. Wiping over sandals is valid if water reaches the top of the feet, but it is not valid for any other covering such as a veil, turban, or similar headgear.

Whoever intends to perform ghusl from janabah or menstruation begins with ablution as described, then pours water over his head and the rest of his entire body until the hair is thoroughly wetted and every spot is purified. His hands should pass over the rest of his body so that no part remains untouched by water. He is commanded to do what he is able to reach with his hands, and if he does so he is purified. However, if any part of his body remains unwetted, he must bring water to it. There is no prescribed limit for the amount of water used in ghusl, unlike what has been mentioned regarding ablution. It is permissible to perform ghusl under rain at the time of rainfall, or while immersed in water, provided one's hands pass over the body as described so that the water reaches everywhere. Ghusl is obligatory following janabah and menstruation, when a disbeliever embraces Islam, and when a person who has been unconscious regains consciousness after several days or a period during which he does not know what occurred and cannot recall whether janabah took place — ghusl is still required even if the state arose during unconsciousness.

<!-- page 15 -->
What is known and evident requires no ghusl in itself. The deceased must be washed when he dies, and ghusl from janabah requires passing the hands over the limbs of ablution as the foundation of ghusl. If a woman sees intercourse in her sleep, ghusl becomes obligatory upon her just as it is obligatory for a man who experiences the same and finds that a discharge has occurred. One who is required to perform ghusl should do so when able, unless he intends to resume intercourse shortly, in which case it is permissible to delay until he has done so and then perform ghusl. If he washes between acts, that is preferable. Likewise, one who is in a state of janabah and the time of prayer arrives should not delay ghusl if he intends to resume intercourse or if he does not intend to do so. The scholars disliked that the one in a state of janabah eat or drink before washing his body, and disliked that he trim his nails or shave his hair while in that state. He should not sit in the mosque while he is in janabah, though passing through it is permissible if he passes without sitting, as indicated by the words of Allah the Almighty: "or having passed through" [al-Nisa: 43]. If a woman performs ghusl from janabah and then menstruates before she has completed her purity, her ghusl serves her for both states. If a woman washes, she need not undo the braids of her head so long as she is certain the water has reached all of her hair down to the roots; it is permissible for her to wash while her hair is braided, provided the braids are not so tight as to prevent water from penetrating them. If there is some density to the braid through which water can enter by pressing it, she should move it so that water reaches beneath. The one performing ghusl should insert a finger into the navel and pass water inside it. After completing ghusl, one should pray that Allah purify him of sins.

<!-- page 16 -->
**Mentioning the purity of clothes and the two earths:**

The Ahl al-Bayt, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, held that all that is permissible to eat from an animal — its skin, feathers, hair, wool, and bones — is pure if the animal was slaughtered in the prescribed manner. If it died without proper slaughter, then all of that from it is impure. One may use a garment affected by impurity for ordinary purposes, but he may not pray in it. As for what comes from an animal whose flesh is not lawful to eat, all of it is impure and may not be used for prayer. We will mention in the chapter on food what is permissible and what is forbidden to eat. What is purchased from the markets of the Muslims, when one does not know it to be from a dead animal, is considered pure until proven otherwise, just as one may eat meat sold in the markets of the Muslims even if the buyer has not verified how it was slaughtered, for the Muslims in such matters are relied upon. If a pure garment is struck by urine, semen, wine or other intoxicants, blood, pus, blood of defloration, or dung from an animal whose flesh is not lawful to eat, or if that substance reaches the body while it is wet and is not removed until it is washed — one may still wear such a garment for purposes other than prayer, but he may not pray in it until it is washed. As for such minor matters as flea blood, small wounds, the water that splashes during ablution and ghusl, the mud of rain that has not been contaminated by impurity, and flies that land on impurity and then land on clothes — these are slight and hidden matters, and there is nothing wrong with them unless they are clearly visible. If there is a flowing wound, one should protect his body and garment from it as much as possible; what he could not prevent at the time of prayer does not invalidate his prayer, and Allah has excused the servant who is afflicted with it. One who has no garments except those of the polytheists should not pray in them until they are washed, unless he knows them to be free of impurity.

<!-- page 17 -->
If he does not know whether the garment contains impurity, there is nothing wrong with praying in it so long as it appears clean. If one prays in a garment believing it to be pure and then learns after the prayer that it contained impurity, his prayer is valid and nothing further is required of him. If he discovers the impurity while still praying, he removes the garment — provided he has a pure garment beneath it sufficient to cover him — and continues his prayer. If he is praying alone, he removes the impure garment, puts on a pure one, and completes his prayer. There is nothing wrong with the perspiration of a woman in a state of janabah or of a menstruating woman, nor with their saliva, if they are Muslim, for the Muslim is pure. Likewise, if the body of such a person comes into contact with a wet garment, that does not render it impure unless there is a visible stain of impurity on the body that transfers to the garment — in which case the affected area of the garment must be washed. Similarly, if there is impurity in a garment and one sweats while wearing it, or if the garment touches the body while wet at the spot where the impurity is lodged, and the moisture carries the impurity to the body, then that place must be washed. However, if a dry impurity does not transfer its moisture to the garment or the body, it does not cause harm. As for urine of an animal whose flesh is lawful to eat, it is pure with respect to ablution, unless the animal has been eating something impure or habitually eats filth as chickens sometimes do — in which case whatever has been touched by it must be washed if the animal is not confined. If the animal is confined and fed pure fodder, then it is permissible. The mud of rain is pure if it has not been changed by impurity; if water touches impurity and undergoes no change, it remains pure, and if impurity comes into contact with water and changes it, then it becomes impure. If a walker treads lightly on damp impurity and then wipes his foot on the ground so that the moisture is removed, he is pure. If the moisture remains, he must wash his feet. If the ground is touched by impurity and the sun then dries it so that its moisture and smell are gone, the ground is purified and prayer upon it is valid. One should not pray on land known to be unclean, nor in a cemetery. Prayer in sheep pens and in the vicinity of camels is permissible if one does not know of any impurity present.

<!-- page 18 -->
**Mentioning the Siwak:**

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, said: "The siwak is good for the mouth and pleasing to the Lord. Jibril did not come to me but that he commanded me to use the siwak, until I feared it would be made obligatory." He said, may Allah's prayers be upon him: "Three things were given to the Prophets: the siwak, perfume, and marriage." Ja'far ibn Muhammad ibn Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: "The siwak is part of ablution, and ablution is part of faith. The siwak removes phlegm." The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, said: "Were it not that I feared hardship for my community, I would have made the siwak obligatory with every ablution." Whoever is able should not neglect it. It is to be used crosswise and not lengthwise for an extended time. It was said that the thumb and the index finger perform the function of the siwak when it is unavailable. The siwak is forbidden to be made from reeds, basil, or pomegranate wood, as these are said to stir up the moisture of leprosy.

**Mentioning Tayammum:**

Allah the Almighty said: "O you who believe, when you stand up to pray, wash your faces" [al-Ma'idah: 6]. He said: "And if you do not find water, then seek clean earth and wipe your faces and hands with it" [al-Ma'idah: 6]. It is narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, that whoever cannot find water — whether a traveller or a sick person who fears that washing would harm or worsen his illness — should use pure dry earth. He places the palms of his hands flat upon it, shakes them off lightly, then wipes his face with them. He wipes the back of his right hand with the inside of his left palm, and the back of his left hand with the inside of his right palm. This is the extent of the hand from which the thief's hand is cut off, because

<!-- page 19 -->
Allah the Almighty said: "So wipe your faces and your hands from it" [al-Ma'idah: 6], and He the Almighty said: "As for the male thief and the female thief, cut off their hands as a recompense for what they have earned" [al-Ma'idah: 38]. The limit of the tayammum corresponds to the limit of cutting, which is what is apparent according to the Book of Allah the Almighty. The limit of cutting will be discussed in its proper place, if Allah wills. Tayammum begins from the fingertips. The scholars of the Ahl al-Bayt, peace be upon them, said that tayammum should not be performed until the end of the prayer's time, and if one performs tayammum at the end of the time, he prays within that entire time. If he finds water before praying, he must perform ablution if he is able; if the water is such that he cannot purify himself with it, his prayer is not annulled. If he brings a subsequent prayer and still does not find water, he performs tayammum again. If he performs tayammum and then finds water before entering the prayer, he must purify himself with water. If he has already begun the prayer and then water is found, he does not interrupt the prayer and is rewarded for it if this occurs at the end of the time. If he prays at the beginning of the time and then water arrives while time still remains, he is not required to purify himself and pray again by tayammum, for he has already prayed. Tayammum is only valid using dry clean earth. It is permissible to perform tayammum with clay, except that one should not smear it on something until it dries and is then used. If no suitable earth is found, one may shake dust from a garment or cloth and perform tayammum with it, striking the earth once with both hands and wiping the face and hands with that single strike. Tayammum is not permissible in a place of residence unless for a funeral prayer when no water is available and one fears missing it. If one is travelling and has only a small amount of water and fears that using it for purification will cause him to die of thirst, he should preserve his water and not destroy himself. If he cannot reach the water without exposing himself to destruction, he should perform tayammum and pray rather than endanger himself. If water is available only at an exorbitant price, and he has the means to buy it without undue harm to himself, he must purchase it.


<!-- page 20 -->
He exceeds the price except where the price is exaggerated, and whoever has no water with him while on a journey should return to his family and pray.

**Menstruation:**

Allah Almighty said: "And they ask you about menstruation; say, 'It is harm, so keep away from women during menstruation, and do not approach them until they are purified. And when they have purified themselves, then come to them as Allah has ordained, for Allah loves those who turn in repentance and loves those who purify themselves.'" [Al-Baqarah: 222] This indicates that during menstruation one is not in a state of purity. It has been narrated from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that he said: the menstruating woman does not purify herself with water until after the menstruation has ceased.

The prayers of the Ahl al-Bayt, peace be upon them, indicate that the sign of purity for the menstruating woman is that she inserts a clean piece of cotton or cloth, and if it comes out clean, she should then wash herself with water as she would wash from janabah. She then performs the prayers due at that time, and she does not make up what she missed during the days of her menstruation in terms of prayer. As for fasting during the days of menstruation, she makes up the fast but does not make up the prayer. Her husband may approach her if he wishes while she is menstruating, though avoiding that is better. The origin of the term "approach" refers to skin-to-skin contact, and from this the word is derived. It is also said that a man approaches his wife if he enters her. If he has intercourse with her in her vulva while she is menstruating, he has committed a sin and transgressed what Allah Almighty has commanded him. He must seek forgiveness from Allah Almighty, repent to Him, and give charity for that act; it is better that he not do so again. If the bleeding extends beyond what is known of normal menstruation, it is considered istihada.

The blood of istihada is distinguished from menstrual blood in that menstrual blood is thick and has a foul odor, whereas the blood of istihada is thin, clear, and does not smell. If the bleeding ceases from

<!-- page 21 -->
the level of menstruation, the mustahadah purifies herself with water, prays, seeks forgiveness, and resumes her normal way of life, and her husband may approach her.

It has been narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that the mustahadah should perform ghusl for every prayer, by which she seeks the reward of Allah Almighty, and that Allah Almighty will record its merit for her if she is sincere. If a menstruating woman becomes pure and then sees light blood that then ceases, no ghusl is required of her, for that is like the blood of istihada. But if it is thick like menstrual blood, she must wash from it. If she sees blood while pregnant and the bleeding resembles menstruation, then she and her husband must observe what is obligatory during menstruation. She should perform ghusl when her postpartum bleeding ends, just as she would wash from janabah. The menstruating woman and the one in a state of janabah may not recite the Quran, nor may they sit in the mosque. If a menstruating woman becomes pure at the time of a prayer but the ghusl extends beyond that prayer's time, she is obligated to perform that prayer.

**Prayer and Its Times:**

Regarding prayer: Allah Almighty said: "Prayer was for the believers a decree of specified times." [Al-Nisa: 103] And the Almighty said: "Guard strictly the prayers and the middle prayer, and stand up devoutly before Allah." [Al-Baqarah: 238] And He said: "Indeed, prayer prohibits immorality and wrongdoing, and the remembrance of Allah is greater." [Al-Ankabut: 45] And He said: "And seek help through patience and prayer, and indeed it is difficult except for the humbly submissive." [Al-Baqarah: 45]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Guard yourselves and know that the best of your deeds is prayer."

<!-- page 22 -->
He said: "Prayer is the offering of every pious person," and he said: "Prayer is the pillar of faith." He said: "Everything has a face, and the face of your religion is prayer."

He also said: Whoever perfects his ablution and prays well, pays zakat on his wealth, restrains his anger, guards his tongue, does good to others, seeks forgiveness from Allah, and sincerely advises the people of the Prophet's household — he has fulfilled the realities of faith, and the gates of Paradise are open to him. He said: The devil remains watchful over the believer so long as he maintains the five prayers; if he neglects them, the devil dares to lead him into grave sins. It is narrated from Ali, peace be upon him, that prayer is the pillar of religion and the foundation of Islam, and it is the first thing that Allah Almighty examines from the deeds of the son of Adam. If it is sound, the rest of his deeds are examined; if it is not sound, none of his deeds are looked at, and there is no portion in Islam for the one who does not pray. He said: the five prayers are an expiation for what falls between them, so long as the major sins are avoided — this is what Allah Almighty said: "Indeed, good deeds remove evil deeds." [Hud: 114]

He said: Prayer is like a pregnant woman who, if her child is aborted before birth, has no pregnancy and no child. He said: Two rak'ahs performed in the latter part of the night are more precious than anything in the world. Ja'far ibn Muhammad said: "Prayer is the scale; whoever fulfills it will be rewarded fully." I know of nothing after knowledge of Allah and His guardians that is superior to prayer — not zakat, not fasting, not Hajj. He said: "The servant is closest to Allah when he is in a state of prayer." Knowledge of prayer precedes the ability to pray at night or by the river, yet it resembles prayer in that its owner is like a man who has been admitted to a sultan and listened attentively until his need was fulfilled. Likewise, when a Muslim enters into prayer, Allah continually looks upon him until he finishes his prayer, and he is asked about

<!-- page 23 -->
The words of Allah Almighty: "And those who carefully maintain their prayers." [Al-Ma'arij: 34]

He said: "This is an obligation; whoever prays it within its time, knowing its due right, allowing nothing else to distract him — Allah Almighty has written for him an immunity from the Fire and will not punish him. And whoever prays it within its time without knowing its due right, allowing other things to distract him — if Allah wills, He will forgive him; if He wills, He will punish him."

The worshipper has three signs: mercy descends upon him from the heavens to the crown of his head, the angels surround him from the place where he stands up to the heavens, and a caller calls out: "O worshipper, if you knew Whom you are addressing and Who is watching you in this prayer, you would not turn away from it." It is narrated from Ali ibn al-Husayn, peace be upon him, that when he rose to pray his color would change and he would tremble, and when asked about it he said: "I am about to stand before a great King." Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: The five prayers — whoever maintains their times and observes their obligations will meet Allah on the Day of Resurrection with a covenant that will admit him to Paradise. Whoever loses their times and does not observe their obligations will meet Allah with no such covenant; if He wills, He will forgive him, and if He wills, He will punish him. It was said to him: "What of the one who is hasty in his prayer?" He said: "First, let him know that relieving his needs lies in the hands of the One before Whom he stands in prayer."

Prayer is the last commandment of the prophets. What is better for a man than to wash or perform ablution, complete his ablution, then rise in a place where no one sees him — while Allah sees him as he bows and prostrates? When a servant prostrates, Iblis says: "Woe to me! This one obeyed while I disobeyed; this one prostrated while I kept away." The closest a servant is to Allah is when he prostrates. He said: "If a Muslim servant stands in his prayer with sincerity, Allah Almighty turns His full attention to him and entrusts him to an angel who lifts the Quran from him" — and he remains thus so long as he is about to pray. When he offers it, Allah Almighty looks upon him and entrusts him to the angel.

<!-- page 24 -->
**The Prayer Times:**

Allah Almighty said: "So exalt Allah when you reach the evening and when you reach the morning — and to Him is all praise in the heavens and the earth — and in the late afternoon and when you reach the time of noon." [Al-Rum: 17–18] And: "after the 'Isha' prayer." [Al-Nur: 58] And the Almighty said: "Establish prayer at the decline of the sun until the darkness of the night, and the Quran of Fajr — indeed, the Quran of Fajr is ever witnessed." [Al-Isra: 78]

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: "The decline of the sun is its departure from the center of the sky, and that is the first time of Zuhr. The darkness of the night is its midpoint, and these cover four prayers. The Quran of Fajr is the Fajr prayer." He said, regarding the words of Allah Almighty "So exalt Allah when you reach the evening" [Al-Rum: 17]: that refers to the Maghrib and 'Isha' prayers. "And when you reach the morning" [Al-Rum: 17]: the Fajr prayer. "And in the late afternoon" [Al-Rum: 18]: the 'Asr prayer. "And when you reach the time of noon" [Al-Rum: 18]: the Zuhr prayer. These are the times of the prayers in the Book of Allah Almighty, and the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family interpreted them as Allah Almighty commanded him to explain what was revealed to him.

He said: Jibril came to me when the light of dawn spread across the horizon of the east, and he led me to the north, and the dawn is the disapproved dawn — which is the first time of the Fajr prayer, by which the fasting person is prevented from eating and which marks the beginning of the restrictions on the fasting person. Before it there is the first dawn, called the tail of the Sarhan, which is a subtle light that rises from the place of sunrise in a way that does not spread across the horizon as the second dawn does. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said:

<!-- page 25 -->
"I was told: arise, for dawn has broken." And the second day he came to me after some of the people had said to one another: "Arise, dawn has broken," and he waited until noon, when the sun had passed its zenith. For Zuhr: when the shadow of everything equaled its length after the disappearance of the sun's zenith shadow. For 'Asr: when the shadow of everything was twice its length. For Maghrib: when the disc had set — that is, when the sun had gone down — and that time continues until sunset the following day. For 'Isha': when the twilight had vanished. Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, said that this refers to the redness that appears on the horizon at the place of sunset after the sun has set. For the second night: when a third of the night had passed. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) then said that Jibril said to him: "The time is between your prayer yesterday and your prayer today." This is a well-known account from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family. We have mentioned it here in brief summary, and it took place in Makkah according to the report. Jibril, peace be upon him, came to him by way of the ocean, and then the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) established the times thereafter as a Sunnah in Madinah. Many reports have been narrated from the Imams of his family, may Allah's prayers be upon them, as well as from others among the general Muslims, and I have mentioned from that what is sufficient according to what I have stipulated as the scope of this book.

What we have narrated from the Imams of the family of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace and blessings be upon them, is that every prayer has two times, except for the Maghrib prayer, whose time ends at the setting of the twilight without delay. The best times are those other than the 'Isha' prayer — for the 'Isha' prayer, the merit lies in delaying it due to the hardship this entails for those who wish to sleep while waiting for the prayer in congregation. It has been narrated from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family that he said: that one of you who prays and allows the time to pass is a great matter — greater than missing his family and his wealth. He said, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family: "Fulfill the rights of your Lord when they are due; pray at the earliest times." He said: "The first time is better, so hasten to do good as much as you can. The beginning of the time is Allah's pleasure, and the end of the time is Allah's forgiveness — and forgiveness is only for sin or shortcoming."

<!-- page 26 -->
He said — Ja'far ibn Muhammad ibn Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him: "The first time is never anything but better, so hasten toward excellence as much as you can." He said: "The beginning of the time is the time of Allah, which is the better of the two." He said: "The first time is better, and what follows it is wide until the end of the time." It is narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that he said: "No one may take the last of the two times except for a reason or an excuse, but the other time is for those who are busy, forgetful, or asleep. They have made the last time for the sick and those compelled by necessity." He said: "When the sun declines, the doors of heaven are opened, and I do not like anyone to precede me in deeds, for I love that my record be the first record in which good deeds are inscribed." He said: "How is it that one of you believes when, upon the arrival of the time, he does not hasten to it?" It has been narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, peace be upon them, that they were commanded to guard the zawwal — that is, the departure of the sun from the center of the sky toward the west. People have devised many instruments for determining this, the most correct and closest to reliable being a circle drawn on a smooth stone or tile fixed in a place that receives sunlight, in which the equinox has been established. One end of it should not be raised over the other, and this should be tested by pouring water on it; if one side descends, water will run to it until it is level, and then it is fixed. A circle is drawn on it, and in the center a small, moderately-sized nail is fixed at a point where the tip of its shadow reaches the circle line at the time of the sun. He then observes at the beginning of the day: when the tip of the nail's shadow reaches the circle line from the direction of the west, he marks its place on the circle line with a point. At 'Asr time he likewise observes where the shadow of the nail reaches the circle line and marks that place. He then divides the arc between the two points on the southern side and marks the midpoint, and does the same on the northern side, marking that midpoint as well. He then draws a line from the tip of a level arm from the southern mark to the northern mark, passing through the center of the nail, so that this line points toward the center of the sky. When the shadow aligns with it,


<!-- page 27 -->
The shadow indicates the sun's elevation at the center of the ark. If the shadow tilts after that point, the sun has moved away — and that marks the first moment of noon, followed by the Sunnah prayer and then the obligatory prayer. Ali (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said regarding the prayer of the Awabeen: "At that hour the gates of heaven are opened, the prayer is answered, the wind blows, and Allah looks upon His creation." We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, prayers of Allah upon them, that when the sun declines, the time of Dhuhr and Asr enters. Rabi'ah agreed with this, saying that the time of noon and Asr — whether in travel or in residence — begins when the sun declines. Ibn al-Mubarak expressed a view close to this, saying that if a man rises and finds his shadow equal to his own height after the sun's decline, he should pray Dhuhr; another narration concerns the time of Asr. If each of two prayers is prayed at the time prescribed for it, how can one time be shared by two prayers without travel or excuse? Ibn al-Mubarak said this, and what Gabriel brought to the Prophet confirms it; had Gabriel come at the same time for three prayers, that would have been made the time for those prayers. The Asr prayer, according to the Ahl al-Bayt, prayers of Allah upon them, begins after the worshipper finishes the obligatory and Sunnah of Dhuhr. The most they mentioned is approximately two hours of the day, so the Asr prayer should begin at the start of the ninth hour, and it ought not to be delayed beyond that except for a reason or excuse. The time for yellowing of the sun and the Maghrib prayer extends until the sun disappears below the horizon of the west. The Isha and Fajr times are as mentioned above, reckoned by knowledge of the sun's rising — bearing in mind that if a mountain or obstacle obscures it, the sign of its rise is the redness of the western horizon, and the sign of its setting is the darkening of the eastern horizon. The night prayer follows Isha, and Witr follows the night prayer; there is no prayer after Witr until dawn rises, whereupon one prays the two rak'ahs of Fajr sunnah and then Fajr itself.

<!-- page 28 -->
The scholars forbade naafil prayer after sunrise until the sun has fully risen, unless one enters a mosque and prays two rak'ahs before sitting. Naafil prayer is likewise forbidden after Asr until the sun sets, though making up obligatory prayers at these two times is permissible — that is, at sunrise and sunset. If one fears that an obligatory prayer will be missed, one should begin it in preference to the naafil prayer. Delaying the noon prayer during intense heat is permitted, once it has passed its earliest time and the air has cooled somewhat, for those praying in congregation in a mosque. Friday prayer is prayed when the sun declines, and it should not be delayed. Combining Dhuhr and Asr, or Maghrib and Isha, is permitted at the end of the first prayer's time and the beginning of the next, in the case of travel; and in mosques it is permitted when there is an excuse such as rain, danger, or hardship. They are combined with one adhan and two iqamahs: when the imam completes the first prayer, he remains in his place, the muezzin rises and pronounces the iqamah, and the imam then stands and leads the second prayer. Whoever forgets a prayer and does not remember it until the time of the prayer he is currently attending — fearing he will miss what he has at hand — should begin with the prayer at hand. If he knows he can make up the missed prayer and still catch the current one, he begins with the missed prayer. If he has missed many prayers, he begins with whichever he can make up while still realizing the prayer whose time he is in, leaving the remainder to make up afterward, and then prays it. If one forgets a prayer and does not remember it until one has entered another prayer — for example, one forgot Dhuhr and did not remember until entering Asr intentionally — one completes the Asr prayer. If otherwise, one completes what one is engaged in. Abu Abd-Allah Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) was asked about this difference. He explained that the Asr prayer is not followed by another prayer, and whoever misses a naafil prayer may make it up if he wishes — this is a virtue, not an obligation. Whoever prays a prayer before its time has not validly prayed, and must repeat it when the time enters.

<!-- page 29 -->
**Dhikr al-Adhan:**

Muhammad ibn Ali (peace be upon him) was asked about the origin of the call to prayer. He said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family) was taught it on the night of al-Isra', and he recounted the hadith of al-Isra' at length — that the angels called the adhan to the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, and that he prayed with the Prophets (peace be upon them). He was then told that some people claimed the call to prayer arose from a vision that a man of the Ansar saw. The Imam became very angry and said: "To Allah we belong and to Him we shall return — they have made a mockery of their religion, claiming it originated from a vision that a man of the people had, following him in it." The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, was told by Allah Almighty: "Say: I follow only what is revealed to me." He also said: "If my ummah knew what lay in store for those who contend for three things — the call to prayer, the first row on Fridays, and the front rows — they would draw lots for them." People were told: "O Messenger of Allah, are you not afraid that your nation will dispute over this when they hear it?" He said: "They will not be able to overpower the weak on the Day of Resurrection." The longest-necked people will be those who called to the testimony that there is no god but Allah — meaning, peace be upon him and his family, that at that time they were the ones who most ardently sought the mercy of their Lord and security from His punishment. In another interpretation, Allah Almighty described such people with the words: "And if you could see the criminals bowing their heads before their Lord" [al-Sajdah: 12]. This is a well-known usage in the speech of the Arabs, who say of one who faces something he dislikes that "his head descends upon it." It was said: "I would have wished that I had asked the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, regarding al-Hasan and al-Husayn." We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, prayers of Allah upon them, that they were commanded to recite the adhan and to recite the iqamah.

<!-- page 30 -->
The iqamah is to be recited with paired phrases, and the final testimony of the iqamah is singular, similar to the adhan. They said that the call to prayer was established from the time of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, and through the days of Abu Bakr. A phrase was added in the days of Umar; Umar had seen it omitted from the call to prayer. He said: "If the children hear that prayer is better than sleep, they will abandon jihad." This was narrated by many of the general scholars. Allah Almighty has mentioned the virtue of prayer in various places in His Book, and has mentioned in it the virtue of jihad as well. It is not necessary to abandon what Allah Almighty has commanded and what the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, established. Rather, the ignorant should come to know what they did not previously know of the truth and come to see it clearly, for they fall into error due to their ignorance of it. If such reasoning required something to be abandoned, it would require abandoning most of the obligatory duties, since people are ignorant of most of them and harbour doubts about them in general. They said that the muezzin, when performing the adhan, should stand facing the qiblah. When he says "Hayya 'ala al-salah," he should turn his face to the right, and when he says "Hayya 'ala al-falah," he should turn his face to the left, keeping his feet in place. It is permissible for a traveller to call the adhan while mounted, but the iqamah should only be given while standing on the ground. The muezzin should not give the adhan unless he is in a state of purity; if he gives the adhan without purity, it is acceptable, but he must not lead the prayer except in a state of purity. When the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, heard the muezzin, he would repeat what the muezzin said, until reaching "Hayya 'ala al-falah," at which point he would say: "There is no power and no strength except with Allah." Whoever says this seeking the pleasure of Allah has a covenant with Allah that the Fire shall never touch him. When the muezzin says that the prayer has been established, it is obligatory upon those present in the mosque to fall silent and stand for prayer; speaking at that point without necessity is permissible but inadvisable. The muezzin should not interrupt the adhan with speech or anything else; if he is compelled by ignorance or necessity to speak, he may resume the adhan from where he left off, and nothing more is required of him. The same applies to the iqamah. The adhan and iqamah are to be given in every mosque where prayer is performed.

<!-- page 31 -->
It is not permissible to omit the adhan or the iqamah or either of them. Whoever prays alone should still give the adhan and iqamah. Abu Ja'far ibn Muhammad (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said: Whoever gives the adhan and then prays will have two rows of angels praying behind him; whoever does not give the adhan but stands and prays will have one row of angels behind him. Giving both the adhan and the iqamah for every prayer is preferable. Whoever prays without the adhan or iqamah need not repeat the prayer, though he is rewarded with the adhan of the land. The adhan and iqamah are not obligatory for women; if women give the adhan and iqamah, it is good and virtuous, and if they suffice with the adhan of the land, that is also permissible. Whoever forgets the adhan and iqamah, or one of them, until he has entered the prayer, nothing is required of him — he continues his prayer and does not interrupt it, for prayer is obligatory and the adhan is Sunnah, and it is not permissible to interrupt an obligatory prayer for a Sunnah whose obligation is fulfilled without it, as we have mentioned. If one is uncertain about whether one has given the adhan or iqamah, or either of them, before entering the prayer — not knowing whether one has performed them or not — then if one gives them, that is good; if one prays without them, nothing is required. One should not give the adhan before the time of prayer has entered; whoever does so must repeat it when the time enters, though if he does not repeat it, nothing is required — his case is like one who prays without the adhan. They permitted the adhan for Fajr alone to be given before dawn, though the adhan at dawn itself is preferable; the earlier adhan is permitted in order to wake the sleeping. Confusion may arise for the ignorant at dawn if they hear the adhan before the time of prayer, so they should not act on it. The adhan at the proper time is preferable, and it is the practice the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, established — today the imams call to prayer according to this, prayers of Allah upon them all, and the muezzin greets the imam and informs him of the prayer after the adhan. They permitted the adhan to be given by a slave or a boy who has not yet reached the age of dreams, and to lead the prayer in that manner. If the muezzin gives the adhan for the sake of the mosque and remains, then the adhan and iqamah are for each prayer performed in that mosque; those who were in the mosque when the adhan was given are not permitted to leave.

<!-- page 32 -->
The muezzin's call to prayer obliges those present to remain until they have prayed, unless one goes out for a need and intends to return, knowing that he will be aware of the prayer; or unless one is not in a state of ablution and goes out to perform it, striving to return in time for the prayer — even if he does not manage to return, he may pray alone or with another group in another mosque, and no blame falls upon him. It is permissible to give the adhan in the mosque before the imam arrives, even at the very beginning of the time. If the appointed imam of the mosque comes, no one else should lead them in prayer. The imam waits to pray until the end of the time; if he comes, well and good, otherwise those present should pray for themselves. Among the people of the locality, if they dispute who should give the adhan, preference goes to the one with the loudest voice, provided they are equal in virtue. It is permissible for a blind person to give the adhan if he follows the adhan of someone who knows the time. The adhan and iqamah for naafil prayers are not required. A muezzin must not build his minaret so high that he can look out over the private quarters of people and see what is in their homes. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, said: "Whoever has a child born to him, let him call the adhan in the child's right ear and the iqamah in the left, and let him recite the Opening of the Book, the Verse of the Throne, the end of Surat al-Hashr, Surat al-Ikhlas, and the two surahs of seeking refuge." He said: "Whoever does this for his child, the child will be protected from the accursed devil and his curses." If the devil licks the tongue of a newborn, he makes the child loathe good things with his filth; if one performs these acts, they provide protection from him. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, also said: "If the desert demons beset you, give the call to prayer, for the jinn are repelled by it — they are called the magic of the jinn." He said: "The demons are on your path and have cast a shadow over it." The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, therefore commanded that the adhan be given when setting out on a path, so that it would drive them away, by the will of Allah.


<!-- page 33 -->
On the Mention of Mosques:

Allah Almighty said: "The mosques of Allah shall be maintained only by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakat, and fear none but Allah — so it is expected that those will be among the rightly guided." [Al-Tawbah: 18] The Almighty also said: "And the places of worship are for Allah alone, so invoke not anyone along with Allah." [Al-Jinn: 18] He said: "The polytheists are impure, so they shall not approach the Sacred Mosque after this year." [Al-Tawbah: 28]

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: Do not undertake a journey to any mosque except three: the Sacred Mosque, my mosque, and the mosque of Jerusalem. He said that prayer in the Sacred Mosque is equivalent to one hundred thousand prayers, prayer in the mosque of Medina to ten thousand prayers, prayer in the mosque of Jerusalem to one thousand prayers, prayer in the great congregational mosque — meaning the mosque where Friday prayers are gathered throughout any region — to one hundred prayers, prayer in the mosque of one's tribe to twenty-five prayers, prayer in a market mosque to twelve prayers, and a man's prayer alone in his house to one prayer. He said that sitting in the mosque to await prayer is itself an act of worship. He said that whoever takes the Qur'an as his companion and the mosque as his home, Allah will build for him a house in Paradise and raise him a degree below the middle station. He also said that the believer's proper place is his mosque, and his house is his provision. He forbade the uncovering of the private parts — between the navel and the knee — in the mosque. He said that prescribed punishments should not be carried out in mosques — meaning retaliation for killing, stoning, and the penalty for adultery and false accusation — but if the matter is one of discipline and discretionary punishment, it is permissible to address it in the mosque. It is forbidden to raise voices in the mosque, to display weapons in it, to conduct commerce within it, or to engage in singing therein.

It was narrated from 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that he said: "There is no prayer for a neighbour

<!-- page 34 -->
of the mosque except in the mosque." It was also said regarding the neighbour of a mosque: "Whoever heard the call to prayer" — and Allah knows best — that discipline and reproach apply because the prayer of one who lives near a mosque yet prays in his house is deficient, and he must repeat it. It is, however, preferable that he pray in the mosque. This is similar to the saying attributed to him, peace be upon him, that "there is no prayer" — meaning no prayer possesses the merit — like prayer performed in the mosque, which diminishes the reward of prayer performed outside it.

It was narrated from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that a man asked him: "O Messenger of Allah, I go out from my house with my family and my son, give the call to prayer, and lead the prayer." He said: "Yes." The man said: "Our Lord has sent the slave to graze the camels, so I give the call to prayer, stand up, and lead my family and children in prayer." He said: "Yes." The man said: "Perhaps the boy has gone to track the traces of rain, so I give the call to prayer and pray with my family." He said: "Yes." The man said: "Perhaps the parents are occupied with their work, so I pray and stand and pray alone in my congregation." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "The believer praying alone with his family constitutes a congregation."

We have mentioned previously that whoever stands and prays with two rows of angels praying behind him is one who is recommended — namely, the worshipper who wishes to pray in the great congregational mosque, owing to the virtue that has been transmitted regarding prayer therein. We have mentioned this. If he is unable to do so, he should pray in the mosque of his tribe; if that is not possible, he should pray in congregation in a marketplace or in any gathering. If he is unable to do even that, he should pray in his house and gather his family and children with him. This is the virtue of the congregation and the reward for keeping his family steadfast in prayer. Allah Almighty said: "And enjoin prayer upon your family and be patient in observing it." [Taha: 132] If he is unable to do that, he may pray alone, and we have mentioned the virtue of that and the diminishment of reward in the first instance. It was said to him, may Allah's prayers be upon him: "Waiting for prayer after prayer is from the ribat." It is Sunnah, if one sits in the mosque, to face the qibla — that is, if he is able to do so.

<!-- page 35 -->
He said: "If the verses are revealed, leave the people of the mosques." Mosques are a matter of great importance among the Arabs. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Keep your mosques free of selling and buying, your disputes, your quarrels, the raising of voices, the display of weapons, the entry of the insane, and forbid raising voices therein." He said: "They were swept every seven days, and purifying substances were placed in them." Whoever cleans the mosque — as God willed — will meet Allah on the Day of Judgment with a smiling countenance, and his record will be placed in his right hand. As for what falls from the mosque onto its floor, it is to be descended from carefully, just as one is careful descending into fire, twisted carefully as one would handle a hot coal, for it is considered a minor sin, and its expiation is to bury it. If one prays in the mosque, let him pray two rak'ahs.

He said, peace be upon him, regarding the mosque and the prohibition concerning it: this applies to one who casts something onto the wall of the mosque, its floor, or its carpet. If it strikes impurity, he should cover it with his garment, wipe it with the bottom of his shoe, or bury it so that it cannot be seen and will not harm anyone — and there is nothing wrong with that.

From 'Ali, may God's blessings be upon him and his family, he said: "Do not make the mosques a place of blame in hadith." He also said: "A man from the people of Yemen entered the mosque of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, having eaten garlic, and the smell was upon him. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, was discomforted by it and said: 'Whoever eats this plant, let him not approach the mosque.'" He said: Upon entering the mosque, one should say: "In the name of Allah, and by Allah, and peace be upon you, O Prophet, and the mercy and blessings of Allah — peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah." Then he greets the people of the mosque. He said: "It is the right of the mosque that if you enter it, you pray two rak'ahs therein" — during the times when voluntary prayers are permissible, and we have mentioned these above.

'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: the qibla lies between the east and the west for those who do not know the precise direction. The qibla is toward the House of the Sacred Mosque. Allah Almighty said: "Turn your face toward the Sacred Mosque, and wherever you are, turn your faces toward it."

<!-- page 36 -->
[Al-Baqarah: 144] The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: One should not pray except in a garment of clean, dry clothing. He said: "If one of you prays in an open field, let him place something in front of him equivalent to the back of a saddle, if he can find that." From 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, it was related that he disliked a man praying toward a sleeping person positioned in front of him, or toward a woman's shoes, unless something is placed in front of him as a barrier. He said: "If you pray toward a garment, let it be positioned such that there is between you and it the extent needed for your prostration — enough space for you to prostrate." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "If one of you prays, let him pray from behind a barrier and not leave a gap between himself and the qibla, lest the devil take it as a passage." It is forbidden to display images in places of prayer. He said: In the mosque — or in the house of the man where he prays — he does not turn to face the people for their comfort and benefit, and this is permissible.

On the Mention of the Imamate:

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Appoint as your imam in prayer the best among you, for the imam stands in for the people." It was narrated from 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family, that he said: The sick person should not lead the healthy ones in prayer — this was specific to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family — and the restricted should not lead the unrestricted.

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that they disliked the imamate of the leper, the person afflicted with leprosy, the child of fornication, the insane, the possessed, and the blind — except that such a person may lead if he is compelled to do so. They did not consider it permissible for an Arab of full capacity or a sane person to be led by those described above, though they allowed such people to pray with one another, each one of them — being in a similar circumstance — leading. They did not consider prayer permissible behind those who are enemies of the family of Muhammad, peace be upon him, and his family — they hated those who openly display their enmity — except in cases of taqiyyah, seeking refuge with Allah from calamity. Whoever is afflicted with this situation prays behind one whose condition is as described.

<!-- page 37 -->
If he fears for himself, he intends the prayer for himself alone and does not follow the imam. He kneels with the imam's kneeling and prostrates with his prostration, and departs when the imam departs, intending all of that for himself — as if he prayed alone without an imam.
