Regarding the imam of one who performs tayammum: if he is more deserving of the imamate, and he leads a people in prayer, then later learns that he was not in a state of ritual purity, he must repeat the prayer. 'Umar once led the people in prayer, then stumbled and said: "O people, 'Umar led you in prayer while he was in a state of impurity." They said: "What do you think?" He said: 'Ali, peace be upon him, said: "You must repeat it, and so must they."

The people follow their imam in bowing and prostrating. Those most deserving of the imamate are the best of them in recitation and knowledge. If they are equal in that, then the eldest among them takes precedence. If a man is the imam of two other men and stands in front of them, the one who stands to his right is his successor; if another arrives and joins the prayer, the newcomer is given precedence and the original successor stands behind him. It is permissible for the imam to wear a light garment between himself and the congregation. They disliked the imam prolonging the prayer when leading a congregation, because there may be among them the weak and the needy. Whoever prays alone, it is preferable for him to prolong the prayer. They said: A woman should lead other women in prayer and should not stand ahead of them, but rather in the midst of them — she stands in one row with them. If the imam makes an error or recites incorrectly, moving from one chapter to another, or omitting something from the Qur'an, or shifting to something else, there is nothing wrong with that. Those praying behind him are not obliged to correct him; if he continues reciting, that is sufficient. If he hesitates, those behind him prompt him, and if he stops and falls silent, they open the recitation for him from behind. If he then continues, they remain silent. Even if two men stand praying and each of them intends to be leading the other, until their prayers are completed — and they learn this afterward — their prayers are valid, because if the imam stands in prayer, his prayer is not invalidated if no one follows him or if they turn away from him. Even if each of them believes he will come to the end, their prayers are not nullified, because each one of them, believing he is the imam, prayed

<!-- page 38 -->
with his prayer and in front of him. They disliked the imam volunteering additional prayers after the obligatory prayer in the same spot where he led the congregation, and he should move to another place. It is recommended for him, when turning away after the prayer, to turn to his right if he wishes to depart. If he wishes to leave and walk out, he should not break through the rows or walk between them. After giving the salutation, sitting in his place is called upon to allow those who missed part of the prayer to complete what they missed.

On the Mention of the Congregation and the Rows:

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "My nation will remain in goodness until they fall into usury, bribery appears among them, they sever the ties of kinship, they abandon prayer in congregation, and they neglect this house. If they do that, they will not speak with one another." Whoever prays the five prayers in congregation — he said — will be accounted for all good deeds and his testimony will be accepted; that is, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) would not know any ill of him. Abu Ja'far ibn 'Ali was asked about prayer in congregation for obligatory prayers. He said: Prayer is obligatory and congregation is not obligatory in every prayer, but it is Sunnah. Whoever abandons it out of desire to be rid of it and of the community of believers, without excuse and out of disregard for it — there is no prayer for him.

From 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him: he once stayed up all night, and when dawn came he fell asleep and missed the Fajr prayer with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, in the mosque. When the Messenger of Allah saw him — having come to the house of Fatimah after spending the day in prayer — he said: "What is the matter with your cousin? He did not witness the prayer with us." She informed him of what had happened. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "He did not miss the Fajr prayer; rather, he is in a better congregation — he who stays up all night and then sleeps." He then woke him and said: "O 'Ali, the earth cries out to Allah from the sleep of the worldly upon it at sunrise." He, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: "To pray the 'Isha' and the Fajr in congregation is dearer to me than what lies between them," and he said: "On the Day when there is no shade but the shade of the Throne —"


<!-- page 39 -->
A shadow but his shadow is a man who has gone out of his house, purified himself, and walks to one of the houses of Allah. It is one of the duties owed to Allah, and it perishes without it. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "The best prayer rows before Allah are the first row, and the best rows of funeral prayers are the last row, and the best prayer rows for women are the rearmost row." Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: "The best position in the row is the first place to the right of the Imam." Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: "The best position is closest to the Imam." We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that they were commanded to complete the rows and close the gaps. It is said that whoever stands in a row and sees a gap ahead of him, it is permissible for him to walk toward it to fill it, and it is permissible for one who feels crowded in the row to step back if there is a gap behind him. It is also permissible for one who cannot find a place in the row to pray behind it alone, and they permit one who cannot find a place in the first row to move forward and stand in line with the Imam's shoes if no other option is available. The minimum distance between two rows is the amount of space needed for the worshipper to prostrate and rise from prostration. When those in a row cannot reach the place of prostration, their prayer is not valid. They permitted praying between the pillars if no other option is available. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Pray in your ranks, align your lines, and do not differ, lest your hearts differ and the devil slip in among you as a small black sheep slips in among a large flock." The small black sheep refers to sheep that are nearly black and small, entering among the large sheep — meaning the devil enters between the rows when he finds a defect in them, in order to corrupt the prayer of its people and preoccupy their hearts. We seek refuge in Allah from the devil and his works.

<!-- page 40 -->
Regarding the mention of prayer rows: The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Deeds are only by intentions." Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: Whoever prays and his intention is for the prayer alone, with nothing else entering into it, it will be accepted from him if it is sound both outwardly and inwardly. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said that the worshipper must not enter into prayer until he has formed the intention, settled his heart upon it, and determined which prayer it is — whether obligatory, Sunnah, or voluntary. If it is an obligatory prayer, he must intend it as such when he enters it. He then opens the prayer with the takbir, raising his hands with palms facing outward and fingers together, stretched, with his fingertips level with his ears, then letting his hands fall along his sides and thighs. He does not place one hand over the other, nor hold them below the shoulder as some among the common people do, claiming that this is derived from the words of Allah the Almighty: "So pray to your Lord and sacrifice" [Al-Kawthar: 2]. Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) was asked about this and said: "The slaughter is after the Eid prayer, and the sacrifice in prayer refers to the worshipper standing upright, establishing his posture and aligning himself." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) forbade placing one hand over the other in prayer, saying that it was something the Jews did, and he commanded that the hands be let free. Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said that one should place one's hands on one's chest while standing in prayer, covering the chest with them. The worshipper then turns and says: "I have turned my face toward He who created the heavens and the earth, as a hanif and a Muslim, and I am not of the polytheists. My prayer, my devotion, my life and my death are for Allah, Lord of the worlds, who has no partner — thus I was commanded, and I am among the Muslims — upon the religion of Ibrahim, upon the religion of Muhammad, and under the guardianship of Ali ibn Abi Talib, Commander of the Faithful. I disavow before Allah his unjust enemies. I seek refuge in Allah, the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing, from the fire and from the accursed devil." He says this verbally with his tongue with reverence and does not speak it aloud

<!-- page 41 -->
while walking in it, and he does not read it aloud. He then opens the recitation with "In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful," saying it clearly. When he recites aloud he must not omit it, because it is a verse from the Book of Allah the Almighty. Some have claimed that it is not from the Book of Allah, but it has been established that it stands at the beginning of every surah in which Jibril (peace be upon him) revealed it to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him). The Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) and many others agreed upon this, and it has been narrated that it was revealed and that one knows precisely the surah and the beginning of the next when "In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful" is revealed. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: "Piety is my religion and the religion of my fathers, except in three matters: drinking intoxicants, wiping over the socks, and abandoning the recitation of 'In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful.'" Meaning that he does not permit drinking intoxicants, nor wiping over socks, nor leaving out the recitation of "In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful" at the beginning of every surah in prayer and in other contexts. He does not command it for anyone in any circumstance, whether in a state of ritual purity or otherwise. The worshipper should then recite after "In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful" the Opening of the Book and a surah, also opening it with "In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful," in the two rak'ahs of Fajr, the first two of Dhuhr, 'Asr, Maghrib, and Isha'. In the remaining rak'ahs there is nothing specifically established regarding the surahs, and there is no obligatory recitation from the Qur'an in those rak'ahs. However, it is praiseworthy for one who prays alone to prolong his recitation as much as he is able, and if he wishes he may shorten it. It is praiseworthy for one who leads others in prayer to lighten it, so long as he knows that those behind him are capable and that there are no weak persons among them. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) rebuked Mu'adh ibn Jabal for this, when he was told that Mu'adh had prolonged the prayer for the people behind him, and said to him: "Do not cause hardship to the people — pray with a prayer suited to the weakest of your companions." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) used to shorten the prayer when leading people, while perfecting it, and if he prayed alone he would prolong it until his feet swelled from the length of his standing. It was said to him:

<!-- page 42 -->
"O Messenger of Allah, you are causing hardship to yourself, yet Allah has forgiven you your past and future sins." He said: "Should I not then be a thankful servant?" The Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) have ranked the prayers in order of length: the longest is the Fajr prayer, followed by the Isha', which is less lengthy than Fajr, then Dhuhr, then 'Asr, then Maghrib, which has the shortest standing. There is no fixed timing specified. One should not combine two surahs after the Opening of the Book in a single rak'ah of obligatory prayer, though there is nothing wrong with doing so in voluntary prayers. If he begins a surah and then wishes to recite another, he may do so up to the midpoint of what he has started, except for Surah al-Jumu'ah on Friday, Surah al-Ikhlas, and Surah al-Kafirun — if he has begun any of these he must not interrupt them. The worshipper should recite aloud in the Fajr prayer and in the first two rak'ahs of Maghrib and Isha', whether praying alone or as an imam, and he may do so if he chooses. In the remaining rak'ahs — the last two of Dhuhr, 'Asr, and Isha', and the final rak'ah of Maghrib — he recites the Opening of the Book alone in a low voice. It is not permitted to omit the Opening of the Book entirely, as some common people say, nor to recite it behind the imam, for this is forbidden. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and the Imams of his descendants (may Allah's prayers be upon them) all said: "My nation will remain on the right path of its religion unless it makes noise with 'Amin' in its prayers." He mentioned the rest of the hadith. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said that saying "Amin" is something the Jews do, and the worshipper should be fully present in his prayer and clear his mind of all else as much as he can. As the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Of his prayer, Allah the Almighty accepts only what he offers with his full attention and presence. If he offers it in full, Allah the Almighty accepts it in full." Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: "What is accepted from the worshipper's prayer is only what he has been mindful of — perhaps half, or a quarter, or less, or more."

<!-- page 43 -->
Allah the Almighty completes this with voluntary prayers, and this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them). They command the worshipper to be humble in his prayer, to glorify Allah's majesty within it, and to be fully aware of that. Ali ibn al-Husayn (may Allah's prayers be upon him), when he wished to enter into prayer, would change in color and tremble, such that those around him would look at him and ask what had come over him, and he would say: "I am about to stand before a great King." They forbade distraction in prayer and turning the gaze away, and commanded the worshipper to keep his gaze at the place of his prostration and to restrain his limbs as much as he can. It is permissible for him to stand with his feet apart and not need to do anything with his body or clothing, and if something needs adjusting he may do so with his hands, though if he can leave it, that is preferable. It was narrated from Ali ibn al-Husayn (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that while he was praying his robe fell off and he did not reach to put it back. He was asked about it and said: "Do you not know before Whom I was standing and what I was occupying my attention with? I was not aware of what you saw." If a garment falls from a worshipper during prayer and it exposes some of his nakedness, he must hasten to put it back as much as he can and cover what can be covered. The nakedness of a man is from the navel to the knee, while the entire body of a woman is nakedness, except for the face and hands. As for Ali ibn al-Husayn (may Allah's prayers be upon him), whose robe fell and he did not replace it — at that time the robe was worn over the garment as an outer covering, so when it fell he was content with the garment beneath it. The worshipper should not spread his feet apart when standing in prayer beyond what is natural; rather, he should stand in moderation, releasing a reasonable space between them. It is permissible for the worshipper to repeat a verse or a word from the Qur'an in a spirit of exhortation or warning. If the worshipper weeps out of fear of Allah, it does not interrupt his prayer; rather, it is a blessing and a reward. However, he must not force himself to weep if he cannot, for if he does so, it interrupts his prayer and is a form of hypocrisy, which is detestable. They said that the worshipper should not interrupt his recitation and fall silent because of

<!-- page 44 -->
a loud noise or shout or the like that occurs from someone nearby, and he should not do so for any reason. We have narrated that Ibn al-Kawwa' entered the mosque in Kufa while the Commander of the Faithful (may Allah's prayers be upon him) was leading the prayer. As he recited, Ibn al-Kawwa' raised his voice from behind him with the verse: "And it was already revealed to you and to those before you: if you associate others with Allah, your work will surely become worthless, and you will surely be among the losers." So the Commander of the Faithful fell silent, then recited again, and Ibn al-Kawwa' repeated the verse and he fell silent again. This happened three times, then the Commander of the Faithful recited: "So be patient — indeed, the promise of Allah is true — and let not those who have no certainty of faith make you impatient." They commanded that the Qur'an be pondered when it is recited, whether by the reciter in his prayer or outside of it. They said that the minimum by which prayer is valid consists of: opening the prayer with the takbir, standing for those who are able, bowing, prostrating, and sitting for the testimony. Whoever fulfills these five pillars of the prayer, and forgets or omits anything else, his prayer is nonetheless complete. Whoever is ignorant of the remaining pillars of prayer must learn them and act upon them without delay, for there is no excuse from making the effort to learn and memorize them once learning is within his reach. Whoever knows the pillars of prayer is not permitted to deliberately leave any of them, nor to omit the recitation, nor the remaining takbirs, nor the tashahhud, nor the tasbihs, nor to leave the prayer without the salutation, nor to omit any of its obligatory and established elements deliberately. If he does any of that deliberately, his prayer is invalid and he must repeat it. This is the way of the one who prays as an imam and completes the prayer — he should make the takbir when he makes the takbir and listen when he recites aloud what he pleases; he should say "Our Lord, to You be praise" when the imam says "Allah hears those who praise Him," and make the takbir in the same manner throughout all prayers, doing so silently without raising his voice. He should perform the tashahhud when the imam performs it, and give the salutation when the imam gives the salutation, even if he is not satisfied with the imam's situation and does not pray behind him out of taqiyyah. If he opens the prayer for himself intending to pray alone and the imam then begins, he may follow along — bowing and prostrating with him — without intending to follow him. If he leaves, he does so accordingly.


<!-- page 45 -->
All of this he does as he would if praying alone. Whether acting as imam or not, the worshipper raises his hands when he wishes to bow, and raises them again when he lifts his head from bowing, just as he does at the opening takbeer. He does this with the takbeer of bowing and with his saying, "Allah hears those who praise Him." When he bows, he places his knees apart and spreads his fingers, stretching his back to a moderate level so that his shoulders are even, for the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, used to do this — even to the degree that if water were poured upon his back it would rest still and not run off. He should neither raise nor lower his head, but hold his back level, and say in bowing: "Glory be to my Great Lord, and praise be to Him," three times. This applies equally whether he is leading as imam, praying alone, or following an imam. He says in prostration: "Glory be to my Lord, the Most High," likewise three times, unhurriedly and with his heart fully engaged in glorifying Allah the Almighty. If he prays alone without an imam to lead him, he may increase in glorification as he wishes, for there is great virtue in doing so. It has been narrated from a group of the Imams, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that before the three tasbihats in bowing they would say: "O Allah, to You I bowed, to You I humbled myself, in You I believed, to You I submitted Islam, and You are my Lord — I bow my hearing, my sight, my hair, my skin, my flesh, my blood, my nerves, and my bones to You. I have not moved any of my limbs in objection, arrogance, or complaint, but in worship, reverence, and humility before Your obedience." And in prostration: "O Allah, to You I prostrated, to You I feared, in You I believed, to You I submitted Islam. You, my Lord, are the One before Whom my face prostrates — the One who created it, fashioned it, gave it hearing, sight, and power. Blessed be Allah, the Best of Creators. O Allah, Your forgiveness is wider than my sins and Your mercy exceeds my wrongdoings, so forgive me and have mercy on me, O Most Merciful of the merciful. There is no god but You. I truly prostrate to You, O Lord — I worship none but You and I call upon none but You, O Lord." They would also increase the glorification of bowing and prostration to seven times, and this is a virtue for the one who does so when praying alone. As for the one who prays

<!-- page 46 -->
behind an imam, we have already mentioned that the sunnah in that case is to lighten the prayer to its completion. They said: when the worshipper raises his head from bowing and says "Allah hears those who praise Him," he should say quietly, with his tongue: "Our Lord, to You be all praise," whether he is an imam or not. He then stands upright and erect. If he is an imam, he should then prostrate. If he is praying alone, he may add to this what has been narrated from them — the same additions mentioned in connection with bowing and prostration — namely that they would say thereafter: "Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds, Lord of might, pride, greatness, majesty, and power. O Allah, forgive me, have mercy on me, strengthen me, elevate me, and provide for me, for I stand in need of good." Then he would rise and descend into prostration.

When the worshipper goes down to prostrate, he should rest on his palms and spread them on the ground, placing them before his knees. He then places his knees down and prostrates, keeping his elbows away from his sides, stretching his neck, and prostrating on his forehead and nose without pressing his arms to the ground; rather, he rests upon them while they are raised from the ground. He places his fingers and palms in front of his ears, near but not touching them, and proceeds to prostrate without placing them too close together. When he raises his head from the first prostration, he sits until every joint returns to its position. He places his hands on his thighs and spreads his left leg beneath his right thigh without sitting upon it, setting his right leg upright resting on its toe if he is able — otherwise he may let it lie on its side. He then performs the second prostration and sits again, both for the first tashahhud and the final one, saying between the two prostrations: "O Allah, forgive me and have mercy on me." If it is long and he is praying alone, he says: "O Allah, forgive me, have mercy on me, strengthen me, elevate me, guide me, pardon me, and provide for me, for I stand in need of the good that descends to me from You." When he rises from the first rak'ah, he rises from the prostration without returning to a sitting position on the ground. He should not recite the Qur'an while bowing or prostrating. When he concludes the prayer, he says the salaam

<!-- page 47 -->
with two salutations, turning his face to the right and to the left, saying in each salutation: "Peace be upon you, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings," intending by this those around him if he is in a congregation, and the angels who record deeds if he is praying alone. When he has finished the prayer and wishes to rise and depart, he should rise from his right side. If he is the imam, the people move away; he walks between the rows without pushing through them, and does not kiss the hands of the worshippers.

**Mentioning the Tashahhud in Prayer, Supplication, and What Is Desired in Supplication:**

Allah the Almighty said: "And your Lord said, 'Call upon Me — I will answer you. Indeed, those who disdain My worship will enter Hell, humbled'" [Ghafir: 60]. The Almighty said: "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near — I respond to the call of the caller when he calls upon Me. So let them respond to Me and believe in Me" [al-Baqarah: 186]. And we have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that they transmitted guidance from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family. He said: "Whoever sits after the second rak'ah and remembers Allah the Almighty and calls upon Him — Allah will honor him by writing good deeds for him and erasing bad ones, and will elevate him in rank, even until he departs." And he said, may Allah's peace be upon him and his family: "A man who prays after the Fajr prayer until sunrise, then strives to meet the needs of someone who has placed his wealth in the earth" — meaning a poor man. The Prophet, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said: "Shall I not show you a weapon by which Allah will protect you from your enemy and make your provision easy?" The believers' weapon is supplication. And he said, may Allah bless him and his family: "I love the praise of the hadith." It was said: "O Messenger of Allah, and what is the praise of the hadith?" He said: "People are immersed in this world

<!-- page 48 -->
and its falsehoods and amusements, but a man seizes that moment to call upon Allah the Almighty, to remember Him and praise Him." And he said, may Allah bless him and his family: "Allah the Almighty has not opened a door of supplication for a servant except that He has opened for him the door of response; and no door of seeking forgiveness has been opened for him except that the door of acceptance has been opened for him; and no door of gratitude has been opened for him except that a door of increase has been opened for him; and no door of charity has been opened for him except that a door of acceptance has been opened for him." This is attested to by the Book of Allah the Almighty, Who says: "Call upon Me — I will respond to you" [Ghafir: 60]; the Almighty says: "Whoever does evil or wrongs himself, then seeks the forgiveness of Allah, will find Allah Forgiving, Merciful" [al-Nisa': 110]; and He said: "If you are grateful, I will surely increase you" [Ibrahim: 7]; and He said: "Who is it that will lend Allah a goodly loan?"

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said: "Supplication turns back the decree," and he clasped his fingers together, palm to palm, joining them all — thumb against thumb and little finger against little finger — as though showing you something, saying that a scourge descends toward the servant, yet he supplicates to Allah and asks Him for well-being, and it ascends back to the heavens. Supplication meets calamity and they contend until the Day of Resurrection. And the Prophet, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said: "Whoever sees someone afflicted and says, 'Praise be to Allah who has healed me from what has afflicted you and preferred me over you and over many of His creation' — it is the right of Allah not to afflict him with that calamity."

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: "Let him pray for Muhammad in his supplication, for if a servant does not raise his supplication to heaven by praying for the Prophet and his family, it will not reach." He said, may Allah bless him and his family: "Place my name at the beginning, middle, and end of the supplication." And he said, may peace be upon him and his family: "Four intercessors are made in the heavens and the earth — the poplar tree, the eye, the head, and the grave. If the servant says, 'O Allah, I ask You for Paradise,' it says, 'O Allah, make him one of its inhabitants.' And if he says,

<!-- page 49 -->
'O Allah, protect me from the Fire,' the Fire says, 'O Allah, protect him from me.' And if he says, 'O Allah, marry me to the pure-eyed ones,' they say, 'O Allah, marry him from among us.' And if he says, 'O Allah, send prayers upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad,' the angel who is at his head says to Muhammad: 'So-and-so has sent prayers upon you and your family, O Messenger of Allah.' And Allah says, 'O Allah, send prayers upon him as he has sent prayers upon me.'"

He said, may peace be upon him and his family, that the supplication of the believer is answered unless he is hasty. It was asked: "And how is he hasty, O Messenger of Allah?" He said: "He supplicates but sees no sign that he has been answered, and says, 'I have called upon Allah and Allah has not responded to me.'"

Allah the Almighty grants the believing servant whatever He wills of what he calls for, and delays it as He wills, when it is most beneficial for him, and Allah the Almighty is ever true to His promise. "Your Lord said, 'Call upon Me — I will answer you.'" And He will show His believing servant on the Day of Resurrection good deeds that he did not know he had performed, and it will be said to him: "Do you know these good deeds?" He will say: "No." It will be said to him: "You called upon Allah on such and such a day, and in such and such a matter, and He answered you and deferred the reward of that until this day." When the believer sees the great reward of his supplication, he will say: "O Allah, I wish You had not hastened any of my supplication for me, but had deferred it all until this day."

Then Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: "Allah repels affliction by supplication — He knows it will be answered, and were it not for the servant's supplication, he would have been struck by a calamity that would have uprooted him from the earth. No Muslim calls upon Allah except that He either responds to him, or diverts from him a calamity by his supplication greater than what he asked for, or stores it for him until a day when it will be more beneficial to him — unless he calls with oppression or severs family ties." And he said, may peace be upon him: "Supplication turns back the decree and it has been so decreed — so multiply your supplication, for it is the key to all good and the success of every matter. What cannot be attained except through supplication is not to be obtained from any door

<!-- page 50 -->
except through it," and he is never without being on the verge of its being opened for him. Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said:

A man asked him about the saying of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, concerning black seed — al-Shuniz — that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, said: "In it is a cure for every disease except death." He asked: "But can I tell you of what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, did not provide a cure for?" The man said: "And what is that?" He said: "Supplication — it is the answered cure." He clasped his fingers together, joining them; or Ja'far ibn 'Ali, may peace be upon him, placed his fingers and interlaced them between his hands as he spoke, then turned them over, then said: "There is nothing more beloved to Allah the Almighty than to be asked, and the decree is not answered except by supplication."

He was asked about the words of Allah the Almighty: "When you have finished, then stand in worship, and to your Lord direct your longing" [al-Sharh: 7–8]. He said: "That means supplication after the obligatory prayer — do not forsake it, for its virtue over the rest of supplication is like the virtue of the obligatory prayer over the nafl prayer." And he said: "Supplication before prayer, during prayer, and after the salaam." And he said, may peace be upon him: "Supplication after the obligatory prayer is better than nafl prayer." Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may peace be upon him, said: "Supplication is worship," and recited the saying of Allah the Almighty: "Call upon Me — I will respond to you. Indeed, those who disdain My worship will enter Hell, humbled" [Ghafir: 60]. Then he said: "Worship is supplication."

May peace be upon him: if he prays the Fajr rak'ahs, he says after the prayer: "I have clung to Allah's firm handle — the inseparable trustworthiness — and I hold fast to the strong rope of Allah. I seek refuge in Allah from the evil of all devils, humans, and jinn. I seek refuge in Allah from the evil of the Arabs and the non-Arabs. I rely upon Allah; Allah is sufficient for me. I turn to Allah; I ask Allah for my need. There is no power except from Allah. O Allah, make light in my heart, light in my hearing, light in my sight, light in my tongue, and light in my


<!-- page 51 -->
A light in my hair, a light in my being, a light in my flesh, a light in my blood, a light in my bones, a light in my nerves, and a light in my hand, a light behind me, a light on my right, a light from my left, and a light above me, and a light from beneath me, and a light in my grave. O God, make for me a light, a joy, a blessing, and a pleasure. Then he recites five verses from the end of Surah Al-Imran, from "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth" to "You do not break the appointment." Then he says, "Glory be to the Lord of the morning and the dawn, who made the night and the day a resting place, and appointed the sun and the moon for reckoning." Then he says, "O God, make the first part of this day righteous for me, the middle of it prosperous, and the last of it success. O God, whoever has turned his need and his seeking toward one of Your creatures — my need and my request are for You alone, who has no partner." Then he recites the Verse of the Throne and the two Qunuts, then says, "Glory be to my great God, and praise be to Him; I seek God's forgiveness and repent to Him," a hundred times. And if he spoke in the first tashahhud, he said, "In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. In the name of God, and with God, and all the beautiful names belong to God. I bear witness that there is no god but God, alone, with no partner for Him, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and messenger, peace be upon him and his family, the Imams from the People of the House." Then he rises, and if he sits in the second tashahhud in which he gives the salutation and departs, he says, "In the name of God, and with God, and all the beautiful names belong to God. Salutations to God, and pure prayers, and good, kind, blessed, wholesome, and growing salutations belong to God. I bear witness that there is no god but God, alone, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and His messenger — the best of prophets and the best of guardians — and that Paradise is true, and Hell is true, and the Resurrection is true, and that the Hour is coming without doubt, and that God will raise up those who are in the graves. O God, send prayers upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, and bless them and have mercy upon them, as You sent prayers and mercy and blessings upon Ibrahim and upon the family of Ibrahim, in all the worlds; You are Glorious, Praiseworthy. O God, forgive me and my parents and have mercy upon us as

<!-- page 52 -->
they raised me when I was young." He added that one who has Muslim parents says, "And forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and do not place in our hearts any resentment toward those who believe; You are the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful." He loved to supplicate for this world and the hereafter, then he would send prayers upon the Prophet and his family, and say, "Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and the mercy and blessings of God. Peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of God." The entire tashahhud he was cautious about, uttering it with his tongue simply and clearly. Then he would give the salutation, announcing submission if he was an imam, or being cautious if he was behind an imam. If he said somewhat less than the full tashahhud, there was nothing wrong with that, whether he was alone — in which case he could be cautious if he wished and could announce if he wished — or in congregation. When he rose from the first tashahhud he leaned on his hands and said quietly, "O God, by Your aid I rise, by Your aid I sit, and by Your aid I stand." When the worshipper completed the obligatory prayer, he placed his hands upon his thighs — visible at heart — and raised them if he wished, which was better, placing the inner surface of his palms toward his face. He would then recite and say, "O God, I draw near to You through You, and through Your Prophet Muhammad, 'Ali, and Fatima al-Zahra, daughter of Your Prophet, al-Hasan, al-Husayn, 'Ali ibn al-Husayn, Muhammad ibn 'Ali, and Ja'far ibn Muhammad" — naming the Imams one by one down to the Imam of the Age — then saying, "O God, I draw near to You through them and direct them toward You, so grant me through them Your countenance in this world and the hereafter, and make me among those brought near to You, and establish certainty in my heart, and increase me in guidance and light." Then he would supplicate for whatever he wished. Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, peace be upon him, said: "When you rise to pray, or enter the mosque and wish to pray, say: 'In the name of God, and with God, and from God, and to God, and as God wills, and there is no power but from God. O God, make me one of Your visitors and among those who rebuild Your mosques, and open for me the door of Your forgiveness and close the doors of Your disobedience against me. Praise be to God who made me among those He saves.' O God, accept me with Your favor and bless Your name." Then he would begin the prayer. 'Ali, peace be upon him, said: "Whoever wishes to have his scale filled without falling short, let him say, when he has turned away from his prayer: 'Glory be to your Lord, the Lord of Glory, above what they describe, and peace be upon the messengers.

<!-- page 53 -->
Praise be to God, the Lord of the Worlds.'" He also said: "I heard the Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him, say: 'Whoever recites after every prescribed prayer "Say: He is God, One" a hundred times, his path on the Day of Resurrection will be illuminated, and on his right will be eight cubits of light, and on his left eight cubits of light, and Gabriel will take his hand as he looks upon the Fire to the right and to the left, and whoever knew him from among those who entered it by a sin other than shirk will enter Paradise through his intercession.'" For this reason — and God knows best — this intercession is particular and not general, for the intercession is not for all people; rather, intercession belongs only to those whom God Almighty has appointed for it, and "they shall not intercede except for one with whom He is pleased." 'Ali, peace be upon him, said: "Whoever sits in the place of his Fajr prayer after he has prayed it, and recites within it 'Say: He is God' eleven times before the sun rises, no sin will follow him that day, even if the devil strives hard against him." He also said: "The Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said to me: 'O 'Ali, recite the Verse of the Throne after every prayer, for none do so except a prophet, a friend of God, or a martyr.'" Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may God's prayers be upon him, said: "Whoever praises Fatima, peace be upon her, before he folds his legs from the obligatory prayer after the salutation, God will forgive him." He begins with the takbir and says the tasbih of Fatima, peace be upon her: "God is the Greatest, Glory be to God, Praise be to God" — sealing it thirty-three times each, followed by "There is no god but God," so that it reaches a hundred. The praise of Fatima, peace be upon her, came about when she came to the Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, to ask him for a servant. He said: "O Fatima, this is better for you than a servant — better for you than a servant, than a servant, and than all that is in this world. Say it after every prescribed prayer, and a hundred good deeds will be written for you, and they will be multiplied ten times, reaching a thousand." Whoever says a hundred times: "Praise be to God, and praise be to God, and I seek forgiveness from God, and there is no god but God, and God is the Greatest, and what God wills, and there is no power but from God, God is sufficient for me, I rely upon God,

<!-- page 54 -->
God" — none knows what that person has with God except that it exalts His name, and the least of it is ten thousand good deeds, multiplied by God Almighty ten times as He said. He said: "It is recommended to say after leaving the prayer that whoever rises from it performs three takbirs, then says: 'There is no god but God, alone, with no partner; to Him belongs the kingdom, to Him belongs praise, and in His hand is goodness, and He is Able to do all things. There is no god but God alone; He fulfilled His promise, granted victory to His servant, and defeated the parties alone — to Him belongs the kingdom and to Him belongs praise, and to God belongs praise, the Lord of the Worlds. There is no god but God, and God is Great, and Glory be to God, and Praise be to God'" — that being ten times. 'Ali, peace be upon him, used to say after every prescribed prayer: "Then with Your light You guided the Ark of praise, and by the greatness of Your forbearance You encompassed the Ark of praise, and You stretched out Your hands and gave. Praise be to God — Your face is the most generous of faces, Your face is the most noble of prestige, and You have bestowed the most beneficial of gifts, and humbled them. One who obeys God gives thanks, and one who disobeys God is forgiven. You answer the supplications of the needy, heal the sick, relieve the distressed, O our Lord — so accept repentance and forgive sins from whom You will; none can requite Your afflictions." Abu 'Abd Allah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, said: "Because it is a remembrance of God as He deserves to be remembered a thousand times, the one who does so will have a thousand good deeds." It is right and recommended to say after prayer: "O God, I prayed for You, and You appointed the prayer, and I ask You to make my prayers and supplications a blessing for me — so expiate my evil deeds through them, whiten my face through them, honor my station through them, and lift them from me and from my garments. O God, render my garments pure, and make what You have good for me. Praise be to God who has discharged from me a prayer that was incumbent upon the believers as a timed obligation." Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, peace be upon him, said: "The least that is sufficient for you in supplication after the obligatory prayer is to say: 'O God, I ask You for every good encompassed by Your knowledge, and I seek refuge in You from every evil encompassed by Your knowledge. O God, I ask You for Your forgiveness, and I seek refuge in You from the disgrace of this world and the punishment of the Hereafter.'"

<!-- page 55 -->
**Mentioning aspects of supplication that are desirable:**

There is no prescribed time for supplication; whoever wishes to pray may do so, asking God Almighty for his needs for himself and supplicating for whatever he loves, so long as he does not call for injustice or the severing of kinship ties. It is fitting for one who wishes to supplicate that he offer good deeds before his supplication and repent to God Almighty from his sins, disavow whatever stands between him and others of His creation, and devote himself sincerely to God Almighty in his deeds and intentions. The Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him, said: "One who supplicates without action is like one who draws a bow without a string." Abu Dharr, may God have mercy on him, said: "Righteous conduct is sufficient accompaniment for supplication, just as salt is sufficient seasoning for food." Whoever wishes to supplicate should begin by praising God and extolling Him as He deserves, then send prayers upon Muhammad, His prophet, and his family, then supplicate God thereafter, and send prayers upon the prophet and his family in the middle of the supplication and at its end. The Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him, heard a man supplicating without first praising God Almighty, and said: "He has hastened before his Lord." And the Messenger of God, peace be upon him and his family, when he wished to supplicate, would begin by praising God Almighty, then say: "O God, there is none who can withhold what You give, and none who can give what You withhold, and none who can constrain what You expand, and none who can extend what You take back, and none who can advance what You defer, and none who can defer what You advance. O God, You are the Generous — so be not niggardly; You are the Magnanimous — so be not stingy; You are the Mighty — so be not humiliated; You are the Invincible against whom there is no harm; You are the Glorified and the Exalted; there is no power or strength save in God, the Most High, the Almighty." He also said: "Do not make me like the cup of the rider" — for the rider fills his cup and then sets it aside, and when he has completed his journey and his need he looks to see whether there is something left to drink or something with which to perform ablution, or he lets it spill. "Place me at the beginning of the supplication, the middle of the supplication, and the last of the supplication" — meaning that the prayer upon him and his family should not be only at the end of the supplication, but should be at the beginning, the middle, and the end. And when God sent down

<!-- page 56 -->
the verse of blessing upon the believers, peace be upon him, the people said: "O Messenger of God, you have taught us how to greet you, so how do we send prayers upon you?" He said: "Say: 'O God, send prayers upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, as You sent prayers upon Ibrahim and the family of Ibrahim; for You are Glorious, Praiseworthy.'" 'Ali, peace be upon him, was asked about the saying of God Almighty: "They did not humble themselves to their Lord, nor did they supplicate" [al-Mu'minun: 76]. He said: "They were not humble in their supplication to God, nor were they sincere in it." Supplications are more numerous than can be counted or contained in a book, and we have mentioned some of what we wished to convey, narrating from the Ahl al-Bayt, peace be upon them, from what has been related and encouraged. Among what has been said is what was narrated about the Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him, that he said: "Whoever enumerates ninety-nine names will enter Paradise." This hadith has been narrated by many and they differed about these names. Sufyan al-Thawri mentioned this in his compendium and cited names other than those mentioned about the Imams, may God's prayers be upon them, and he mentioned them differently. 'Ali ibn al-Husayn, may God's prayers be upon him, was asked about them, so he described their virtue and the answered supplication for one who prays them and exalts them, in words he set forth at length. He took a covenant upon whoever learned them from him that he would teach them only to one he trusted, or that they not be used to supplicate God for an act of injustice or the severing of His mercy. He said: "They are in a book which he mentions, and in its opening are five names: 'By God, O God, O Merciful, O King.'" Among them in Surah al-Baqarah are twenty-six names: O Ocean, O Mighty, O Knowing, O Wise, O Oft-Returning, O Patient, O Vast, O Subtle, O Aware; O Hearing, O Sufficient, O Forbearing, O Gentle, O Thankful; O God, O One, O Forgiving, O Clement, O Withholder, O Expander; O Living, O Mighty, O Great, O Rich, O Lord, O Moist. And among them in Surah Al 'Imran are fourteen names: O Sustainer, O Bestower, O Swift in Reckoning, O Expert. And among them in Surah al-Nisa' are six names:


<!-- page 57 -->
O Sergeant, O Martyr, O Pardon, O Despicable, O Agent. And from it in Surat Al-An'am, five names, which are O Fater, O Qaher, O Latif, O Ruler. And from it in Surat Al-A'raf, two names, and they are O Mohi, O Dead. And from it in Surat Al-Anfal, there are two names, and they are the Blessing of the Lord and the Blessing of the Supporter. And some of it is in certain suras — seven names, and they are Hafiz, Qarib, Qawi, Matin, Majid, Wadud, and Fa'il. And from them in Surat Al-Ra'd are two names, and they are O Kabir, O Almighty. And from it in Surat Ibrahim, one name, which is O Mannan. And some of it is in Surat Al-Hujurat, a name, and it is O Mannan. And some of it is in Surat Al-Hajj, a name, and it is O Muhit. And of it in Surat Al-Anbiya', two names, which are O Sadiq, O Khayr. And some of them in Surat Al-Mu'minun is a name, and it is Karim. And from it in Surat Al-Nur, three names, and it is O Hadi. And from it in Surat Saba', a name, and it is Fattah. And one of them in Surat Al-Mu'min is four names, and it is O Ghafir, O Maqit, O Rafi'. And of it is Surat Al-Dhariyat, three names, and they are Raziq, Dhu al-Quwwah, al-Matin. And some of them in Surat Al-Tur is a name, and it is Barr. And from it in Surat Al-Najm, a name, and it is O Almighty. And from it in Surat Al-Rahman, three names, and they are Dhu al-Jalal wa al-Ikram. And of them in Surat Al-Hadid, four names, and they are Al-Awwal, Al-Akhir, Al-Zahir, Al-Batin. And from it in Surat Al-Hashr, ten names, which are O Quddus, O Salam, O Mu'min, O Muhaymin, O 'Aziz, O Jabbar, O Mutakabbir, O Khaliq, O Bari', O Musawwir. And some of them in Surat Al-Buruj are two names, and they are O Wali and O Shahid. And some of them in Surat Al-Ikhlas are two names, and they are Ahad and O Samad. These are the names of Allah Almighty in His book, and they are ninety-nine names, and they are one hundred names in Allah — those by which, when Allah Almighty is called upon, He answers; and when asked, He gives. And it was known, and its virtue is narrated, and the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said: O 'Ali, shall I not teach you words to say — for if you had sins like the foam of the sea, Allah would remove them

<!-- page 58 -->
from you? Say: O Allah, establish for me friendship in the hearts of the believers, and make for me a covenant with You. And this is what Allah Almighty sent down: "Verily, those who believed and did righteous deeds — the Most Merciful will appoint for them affection" [Maryam: 96]. And it was among the supplications recited upon him, peace be upon him.

This supplication — the supplication of the greatest Imams — was used to pray for him when he was sent on missions. He says: O Allah, I am inspired to praise You, I give thanks to You, and I am inspired to spread my tongue in Your praise. I laugh toward You and I want You to recompense me when I draw nearer to You, and I seek refuge in You from what calls to other than You, and I seek refuge in You from that which descends upon the people who turn away. O Allah, You began blessing me with Your gifts and singled me out above much of Your creation, so it became mine without a question from me — from Your oath, the arrow of Saad, and fortune and abundance — because You made me among the people of Your knowledge and those who hold fast to the rope of Your unification before the passing of ages, the shifting of eras, the turning of cycles, before the creation of male and female and the multiplication of generations. Then You created me in the finest form and most perfect image, and You made me hearing, seeing, and speaking. You made me aware. Then You perfected my constitution and completed my faculties, and You did not deprive me of any of my limbs but immunized them from ailments by Your power and preserved them. How much incapacity remains under Your management, as You have taken charge — O my God — repelling illness from my body with my health, and guarding my weakness. May Your blessings descend upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You are his family and they are his people. And remove from myself the obstacle of delay, the discouragement of hesitation, and release it from the constriction that grips it, and relieve it of the burdens of what it carries. Grant me to stand at the boundaries of Your sacred limits, and loose my tongue with Your abundance and good grace. Bestow upon me repeated remembrance, and make it through reciting Your book, night and day, and make it effective for truth. Cause me to benefit from it and leave falsehood for fear of its harm, and distance me from the participation of wrongdoers and the symptoms of heedlessness.

<!-- page 59 -->
And silence me from the whispering of the slanderers, and from every insulting alliance. O Allah, grant me resources whose end is spent in Your way, by which I may beseech Your mercy from Your hope, and may I not incline toward that which draws Your indignation, nor be satisfied with reduction when Your satisfaction is within reach. O Allah, and if You have lowered Your great treasures and showered Your gifts with mercy, then do not turn them away disappointed from the bees of Your bounties. I pray that You raise them to You with the descent of Your gifts and the swiftness of Your answer, and restrain them from what the hands of those who tamper with Your sanctity and transgress Your boundaries have long done. And rain down upon my eyes, O Allah, thrusts of tears and streams from Your fear, and anger about the moments You have forbidden. And make them humble before what they attain from the adornments of this world and its flowers. Protect them, O Allah, and shield them from the shrine when the cradle is spread by the heedless. And forbid, O Allah, my hearing from money that pleases sinners, and make it lead to my heart that which draws me closer to You and earns Your satisfaction over me — through making Your halal lawful, forbidding Your haram, observing good conduct at Your doors, and longing for the nafl acts that hasten toward Your satisfaction. And free my heart, O Allah, from every break in which I neglected Your command, and take from it the following of Your indignation, and empty therein a record of Your love, to send it to the intimate conversations of Your devotees and motivate it toward longing for what You have prepared for Your guardians in the abode of Your dignity and honor. Grant salvation from the wreckage of the great religion, the weight of its burdens, O Allah, and grant me firm standing in devotion to Your servants and knocking on the door of Your answer — a door opened only to those who turn sincerely to Your face. I intend thereby the recitation of Your book, night and day. Grant me the strength to draw near to You. Send me to You in the darkness of long nights, between the intervals, to fulfill Your promise. Do not leave me with a wound but that I have prepared for it, nor a purpose except that I have sought its cause, nor a determination but that I have tightened it, nor a thread I intend to twist upon a rope except that I have twisted it, nor an evil gum except that I have suppressed it. O Allah, and whatever has befallen me

<!-- page 60 -->
of failing to realize every good benefit — let me expect nothing except from You. And every hated path is only lifted with horses drawing near to You. Come to me with Your goodness and generosity, for You do whatever You will and judge whatever You will, and You are the most merciful of the merciful. So do with me in all circumstances what befits You, O Allah, in relation to Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, who are sincere — peace and blessings be upon them all — in well-being, O Lord of the Worlds.

From his supplication, peace be upon him: O Allah, You are more worthy of what You are than of Your forgiveness of Your family from Your punishment. O Allah, honor Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You are his family and they are of You, his family. And do not discipline me with Your punishment, and do not scheme against me with Your cunning, and do not lead me astray from where I do not know. O Allah, whoever is better thanks to You wins, and whoever errs by his own mistake perishes. No benefactor dispenses with Your support and aid, and no wrongdoer defeats You, nor does any matter exceed Your ability. Thus, do with me what You are worthy of — a man of all kindness. Then he prays for what he wishes.

And it was from his supplication, peace be upon him: O Lord, a certain enemy has drawn the sword of his aggression against me and sharpened against me the edge of his hostility, and he exposed me to the striking of his grandfather, and he showed me his poisonous troops. His watchful eye does not sleep, and I fear that he may strike me with his hatred and overwhelm me with his bitterness against my weakness and the possibility of casualties, and his gloating over the victory of those whom I sought to fight. He cornered me in many of my intentions. So extend me with Your victory over him, and let his gathering be scattered after its assembly, and exalt my standing against him, and make what I have shot at him from arrows reach their mark, and strangle him with his own throat, and strike him with his own sword. Let him be humbled after his manhood and submit, and after his arrogance let him be servile, imprisoned in the bind of his rope — that very bind by which he believed he would see me on the day of his power. Praise be to You, as You are his family, beyond imitation and beyond defeat. And this is not to be hastened. This is a prayer taught by 'Ali, peace be upon him, to al-Husayn, peace be upon him, and mentioned to him. His bounty is that which Allah Almighty has enjoined upon the reward for the one who supplicates with it at length and takes it upon himself.

<!-- page 61 -->
He promised not to teach it to anyone except his family, his Shi'a, and his loyalists. So he made that promise to him and said: If you wish to ask Allah Almighty for anything, or call upon Him for anything in this world and the hereafter, say: Glory be to Allah, praise be to Allah, there is no god but Allah, Allah is the greatest, and there is no power except from Allah. Glory be to Allah in the night and the ends of the day. Glory be to Allah in the morning and in the origin. Glory be to Allah when you enter the evening and when you enter the morning. And praise be to Him in the heavens and the earth, and in the evening, and when you appear at midday. The living comes out from the dead, and the dead comes out from the living, and He revives the earth after its death, and thus you will be brought out. Glory be to your Lord, the Lord of Glory, above what they describe, and peace be upon the Messengers, and praise be to Allah, the Lord of the Worlds. Glory be to the King of kings, glory be to the Owner of Glory, Greatness, and Honor. Glory be to the Living Who never dies. Glory be to the Holy King. Glory be to the Everlasting Existent. Glory be to the Living, the Self-Sustaining. Glory be to the Most High, the Almighty. Praise the Holy of our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit. Glory be to our Lord, the Most High, the Almighty. O Allah, I have entered the evening and the morning in Your grace and well-being, so complete Your grace and wellness upon me, and grant me to perform Your thanks. O Allah, by Your light I was guided, and by You I seek help, and in Your grace I entered the evening and the morning as a witness. O Allah, I bear witness to You — and You suffice as a witness — and I bear witness to Your angels and the bearers of Your throne and all of Your creation, that You are Allah, there is no god but You alone, with no partner for You, and that Muhammad is Your servant and prophet, peace be upon him and his family. O Allah, record for me this testimony with You until You receive it on the Day of Resurrection, and You are pleased with me, for You are capable of all things. O Allah, praise be to You — a praise to which the sky lifts its cover, the earth and those upon it give praise. O Allah, praise be to You — a praise that ascends first and does not run out. O Allah, praise be to You, always and eternally, never ceasing, and I thank You with me and my son, whether asleep or awake, whether alive or dead, while You remain. O my Lord, praise be to You with all Your praises for all Your blessings. O Allah, for You

<!-- page 62 -->
is praise for every still and pulsing thing, and thanks for every food and drink, and for every place of rest. O Allah, I thank You for Your forbearance after Your knowledge, and I thank You for Your forgiveness after Your decree. You are the source of praise, You are the heir of praise, You are the praise itself, You are the worthy of praise, You are the originator of praise. Praise be to You, O Bestower of thanks — praise ancient and of high degree, a house of blessings from above the seven heavens, the One who brings out darkness into light, and who changes evil into good and elevates good deeds to higher degrees. O Allah, I thank You, O Forgiver of sins, O Accepter of repentance, O Severe in punishment — there is no god but He, to whom all destiny belongs. O Allah, praise be to You in the night when it recedes, and to You is praise in the daytime when it manifests, and praise be to You in the hereafter and the first. O Allah, praise be to You in the number of riches and pebbles, seeds, humans, jinn, birds, beasts, and seven. And I thank You in the number of what Your book has counted, and praise be to You for what Your knowledge has encompassed — abundant praise, good and blessed. Then he says: There is no god but Allah alone, with no partner. To Him belongs the dominion and to Him belongs the praise. He gives life and causes death, and He is alive and does not die. In His hand is goodness, and He is capable of all things — ten times. Then he says: I seek forgiveness from Allah, there is no god but the Living, the Ever-Reviving, for all my sins, and I repent to Him — ten times. Then he says: O Allah, the Most Merciful — ten times. Then he says: O Hannan, O Mannan — ten times. Then he says: O Creator of the heavens and the earth, O Lord of Majesty and Honor — ten times. Then he says: In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful — ten times. Then he says: Amin, Amin — ten times. Then ask your need and pray for the Prophet and his family.

And 'Ali ibn al-Husayn, may Allah's prayers be upon him, in the dead of night, after every eye had calmed down, would say in his supplication: My God, the stars of Your heavens have set, and the eyes of Your creation have slept, and the voices of Your servants have quieted, and the kings of Banu Umayyah have closed their doors and surrounded them with their guards, withholding anyone who asked them for anything or sought benefit from them. But You, my God, are the Living and the Strongest — neither slumber nor sleep overtakes You, and nothing diverts You from anything. Your doors


<!-- page 63 -->
Your heavens are open to those who call upon You, and Your treasures are not withheld from those who ask of You — they are bestowed freely.
My God, You are the Most Gracious; You do not turn away a believing soul who asks of You, nor do You conceal Yourself from one who seeks You.
None who want of You are diminished by their need, and nothing can suffice them except You. O God,
You see where I stand and You know my resting place, and You see what lies in my heart and what is good for me in my worldly life and in my hereafter.
So come to me with Your goodness and generosity, my God. You have seen that I am anticipating death, and He is the One who knows and who stands.
Between me and my food and my drink, my wakefulness and my rest, my anxiety and my calm.
He has prevented me from restful sleep, so that the one who fears the sudden arrival of the King of death in the watches of the night may sleep —
And the watches of the day — but how can the wise man sleep when the King of death does not sleep in his vigil over him, seeking to take his soul
In the morning and in whatever hour it may be. Peace be upon him — he wept at these words and wept
So deeply that his family and devoted companions were frightened by his weeping. They approached him and found that he had pressed his cheek to the dust while
Saying: O God, I ask You for ease of soul at the time of death, and passage to the Abode of Decision. And from the supplication of my father
Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may God's prayers be upon him: O God, pray upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad,
And have mercy on my lowliness and my helplessness before You. I pray to You and I have kept apart from people for Your sake, and I forget all else for Your sake. O
My God, do not expose me, for You are fully aware of what is in me. Do not punish me, for You are capable. O God, I seek refuge in You from
Humiliation in life, an ill appearance at death, evil in the grave, and regret
On the Day of Resurrection. O God, I ask You for a life of contentment, a death of happiness, and a generous return — not shameful or
Disgraceful. O God, Your forgiveness is wider than my sins and Your mercy exceeds my deeds, so forgive me
And have mercy on me. O God, I thank You for granting me obedience to You, for Yours is the argument — that disobedience brings no loss to You,
Whether from me or from others in charity, and there is no excuse for me or for others in wrongdoing. O God, strengthen me in my religion, in my worldly affairs,
And in my hereafter through my piety. O God, protect me in what has passed, and do not leave me to myself in what I have

<!-- page 64 -->
Prepared for. O You who are never lacking in forgiveness — and forgiveness does not diminish You, and sins do not harm You — pray upon
Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, and forgive me what does not harm You, and grant me what You do not lack. O God, I ask You for
Ample sustenance, acceptance of supplication, forgiveness, mercy, health, piety, pardon, well-being, richness, and contentment,
And intention and righteousness and gratitude and success in all my affairs, in this world and the hereafter, making all difficult
Things easy in the best of ways. O Generous One, bestow generously. O God, upon my son and my family and relatives and my brothers from among
The believing men and believing women, the Muslim men and Muslim women, the living and the dead, those whom You have loved and who have come close to You,
And those whom You gave birth to and those who gave birth to me. O God, I ask You for patience in obeying You, patience against disobedience to You, and contentment
With Your decree, honesty in every land, the realities of faith, and gratitude for Your countless blessings. O God,
Pray upon Your Prophet Muhammad and all his family.

— Mentioning what is permissible and what is not permissible in words and actions during prayer:

It is narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, the prayers of God be upon them, that speech, events, and departures
In general, from the direction of the Qiblah, and whatever is obscene in action, interrupts prayer — but not supplication, praise,
Takbir, recitation of the Qur'an, or whatever a servant says to his Lord in prayer, even if it be in words
That would otherwise interrupt prayer. It is related that Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali, may God's prayers be upon him, said:
"I spoke to God in prayer with words" — meaning words that do not interrupt prayer. They said that one who is greeted with peace
May return the greeting silently or with a hidden gesture, and this does not interrupt his prayer.
They did not hold that a gesture or an inward thought interrupts prayer, though they preferred to leave such things. It is
Better for one who is greeted to continue his prayer than to leave it, with the greeting returned
After he has completed it if he wishes — that is preferable. They gave permission to one who sees something reprehensible or fears harm, or

<!-- page 65 -->
Who fears he will miss something, to say "Glory be to God" while praying, intending it as Qur'anic recitation —
Since he may intend it as praise of God, for that is permissible in prayer, even if the matter is not of great consequence.
Thus his prayer remains sound, though leaving such things is better. Making a gesture to indicate what a worshipper wishes is also, in the understanding of this tradition,
Permissible — meaning that if he points to someone who wishes to address him without turning his face away from the Qiblah, there is
No harm in it, though if he refrains and continues in his prayer, that is better. It is held that laughter
During prayer nullifies it, whereas smiling does not nullify it, though it is disliked for the worshipper. They also forbade
Blowing during prayer — such as a worshipper blowing on the place where he is to prostrate if he sees something on it
That he dislikes prostrating upon — for it is not permissible to prostrate upon it, and they did not view blowing as acceptable.
If there is something unclean in the place of prayer, it should be moved even if it is on
The ground — moved under his feet — and if he removes it with a garment, that is better. The worshipper should not spit toward
His face or his right; he may spit to his left without turning his face away from the Qiblah. They also forbade interlacing
The fingers during prayer, which is when the worshipper bends them together as if to clap. This is among the forms of heedlessness and negligence in
Prayer. If he interrupts it, it is because the one who does so has no need for it; if it is nullified by an action he did not intend
Or by something he did not find necessary, there is no harm in that. They permitted making contact in prayer for one to whom
It was offered, if he could bear it patiently and it did not preoccupy him from his prayer. They also gave permission to the worshipper
To kill a snake or scorpion he encounters during prayer, and every frightening and harmful creature, and to remove lice,
Fleas, and insects found on his body or garment while he is praying. They forbade deliberately tampering
With the beard or other things by hand during prayer, but permitted it if done unintentionally.
They gave permission to pray while holding a Qur'an and forbade stretching and yawning during prayer.
Neither of these nullifies his prayer, nor does sneezing nullify

<!-- page 66 -->
Prayer — though the worshipper should lighten it if he is able, cover his mouth with his hand, and praise God. They also
Forbade the worshipper from wiping his forehead of dust or anything else from what he prostrates upon while
He is in prayer. It is permissible once he has left the prayer, though not while still in it. They forbade the Turk position
During prayer, which is when the worshipper places his hand on his hip. They also forbade a man from praying while his face is veiled, except
From an excuse or a cause.

— Mentioning dress in prayer:

The Messenger of God, may God's prayers and peace be upon him, said: "Let every one of you fear God in his garment during his prayers."
For this reason, one should pray in the finest garments he can find, for prayer is
The best occasion for a person to adorn himself. We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, the prayers of God be upon them, that they permitted
Praying in a single garment, provided it is covering — so that the 'awrah of the worshipper is not visible — if it is lightweight
And would otherwise reveal him, and they did not consider it sufficient for the worshipper to wear it alone unless it covers his 'awrah.
For a man, the 'awrah is between the navel and the knees; for a woman, her entire body is 'awrah and she must cover it in prayer,
Except for her face. A free woman is required, if she wishes to pray, to cover her face as well. A slave woman may pray without a veil, but
She must cover her head and not leave her hair exposed. It is rewarding for a man to pray wearing a garment that covers his eyes, even if
The rest of his body remains exposed if he has nothing else. He should not deliberately do so if
He has something else available; rather, he must wear it if it is present. The worshipper should dress in a robe or gown, placing the garment
Across his middle from its length upon his shoulders, throwing the left end over to his right and the right
End over it to his left. They forbade the wrapping known as ishtimal al-samma' in prayer — which is
When one gathers the side of the garment while it is on his shoulder and throws it from his right

<!-- page 67 -->
Over to his left and winds himself in it as the people of the valleys do, leaving his hands inside it — so he should not
Pray in such a way. They are permitted to pray in this manner, but they forbade the prayer of the prince style.
They said that it is the way of the Jews. The saddle position is to place the robe from the middle of its length
Upon his shoulders and to let both sides hang freely, his hands free from beneath without anything pulling them from his shoulders.
They permitted prayer while carrying a sword and that the worshipper should wear it as a mark of distinction; they commended this for the Friday prayer,
The two 'Eids, for Jihad and Ribat, and wherever the worshipper fears for himself in travel and otherwise. As for
Praying in leather socks, they disliked it, though they permitted prayer in sandals if the worshipper is not impeded by them and was
Able to do so. They forbade praying in the garments of polytheists that they had worn, until such garments had been purified.
It is commended for a woman to pray wearing her ornaments if she has them, without this disturbing her, even if she is wearing around
Her neck a necklace or something worn to adorn herself before men. They disliked women going out
With henna on their hands and preferred that they change the appearance of their palms and not imitate men. Whatever is permissible to wear, upon which one may prostrate,
Is permissible to prostrate upon — on the ground and other materials such as wool, hair, wells, and the like.
The worshipper should stand in his prayer and not prostrate upon any foodstuff. They also disliked
The worshipper prostrating on part of his own clothing — not upon his sleeve or on the wrapped part of his turban — and held that
He must press his forehead down until it touches what he prostrates upon, or whatever part of it he is able to prostrate with,
Including his nose.

— Mentioning the Friday prayer:

The Messenger of God, may God's prayers and peace be upon him, said that four categories of people have what preceded them forgiven:
The sick person when he recovers, the polytheist when he embraces Islam, the pilgrim when he completes his Hajj, and the one who comes to Jumu'ah

<!-- page 68 -->
Out of faith and in anticipation of reward. He said, may God bless him and his family: pray abundantly upon me on Friday,
For it is a day on which deeds are multiplied. The Prophet, may God's prayers and peace be upon him, said: "Indeed God Almighty sends down
Angels with pens of gold and silver and scrolls on Friday night, and when dawn breaks,
They record nothing but prayers upon me and my family until the sun sets. Angels stand at the doors of
The mosques recording those who come, in order of their arrival, from the time of dawn until
The Imam ascends the pulpit. When the Imam ascends the pulpit, they fold their scrolls and enter to listen to the remembrance." He also said,
May God bless him and grant him peace: there are three things that, if only my nation knew the full merit of them, they would compete for them even at the cost of striking one another with arrows — the call to prayer, and tomorrow, meaning Friday,
And the first row in prayer. He said: "Whoever proceeds to the mosque on Friday and recites Surah Al
Imran until its completion, seventy thousand angels accompany him, and seventy thousand receive him, and he is forgiven between
The two Fridays. And if he recites Surah al-Kahf, seventy thousand angels accompany him and seventy thousand receive him, and he will be gathered
On the Day of Resurrection with a gathering spanning the distance between Mecca and Medina." He also said, may God's prayers be upon him, regarding the words of God Almighty:
"Guard strictly the prayers and the middle prayer" — he said the middle prayer is the
Friday prayer, which on other days is the noon prayer. He urged that it be kept and maintained, saying about it:
Peace be upon you — and he forbade abandoning the Friday congregations, saying: "Let each of you be mindful, for some of you are about to begin neglecting the mosque —
Coming only on Friday, then being absent, and then missing another Friday, and then being absent, until God seals
His heart." Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali ibn al-Hussein said, may God's prayers be upon them:
"I have doubled my prayers on Friday, for I love to multiply prayer and charity on that day." And Ali said,
Peace be upon him: "Friday night is a resplendent night and its day is radiant. Whoever dies on Friday night, God Almighty writes
Whoever dies on Friday, God will protect him from the Fire, and Hell


<!-- page 69 -->
It is assessed every day except Friday, on which no such assessment applies, and it is permissible to pray throughout the whole of Friday.

Ali (peace be upon him) said that the Friday prayer is obligatory, as is attendance upon the just Imam — an obligation such that if a man abandons it without any excuse, he has abandoned three obligatory duties, and he should not abandon any of them.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said that on the night of Friday, Allah Almighty is as a King: He calls from the beginning of the night until its end, just as He calls every other night during the final third, saying: "Is there anyone who asks, that I may give him? Is there anyone who repents, that I may accept his repentance? Is there anyone who seeks forgiveness, that I may forgive him? O seeker of good, draw near; O seeker of evil, desist." When the morning of Friday arrives, Allah Almighty distributes sustenance among His servants until the sun rises. It has been narrated from the People of the House (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that they encouraged ghusl on Friday and disliked taking medicine on Friday and Thursday, lest one's strength be weakened before the witnesses of Friday. They also enjoined kindness and fine dress — the best one can find — when intending to go out to witness the Friday prayer. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "On Friday there is an hour in which Allah does not allow a believing slave to ask for anything good except that He grants it."
