Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him), Abu Ja'far, and Abu Abdullah (may Allah's prayers be upon them all) said that this hour is from the setting of the sun until an hour has passed. They also said (peace be upon them) that the Friday prayer is obligatory except for the traveler, the woman, the child, the sick, the slave, those with a valid excuse, and those who have witnessed it among those for whom it substitutes the noon prayer. They said (peace be upon them) that whoever is within two farsakhs of the congregational mosque is obliged to attend the Friday prayer. The farsakh is three miles, and the mile is three thousand arm-lengths. If there are three miles between two congregational gatherings, then the Friday prayer may be held in each of them even if they are in the same city. They said that the minimum number required for the Friday prayer is five men, one of whom is the Imam; if fewer than five are present, they do not gather for it and instead pray the noon prayer, for the Friday prayer offered with an Imam is equivalent to seventy prayers.

<!-- page 70 -->
O you who believe — this applies to those who are in a city. Regarding the words of Allah Almighty: "When the call is made for prayer on Friday, hasten to the remembrance of Allah" [al-Jumu'ah: 9], Allah's command does not mean running or haste; rather, what counts is the merit of setting out before the call. Those who are led to prayer should not delay to the point that they cannot reach it in time, and one walks to it — neither running nor lagging — until one arrives. Regarding His saying: "When the prayer has been concluded, disperse through the land and seek the bounty of Allah" [al-Jumu'ah: 10], this is a permissive command: one who is commanded to seek the bounty of Allah when the call is given is permitted to leave after the prayer if one wishes. Whoever remains in the mosque, praying and remembering Allah and sitting, receives the virtue and reward of that. Whoever leaves after the prayer has been completed, there is no blame upon him, for Allah has permitted it.

If one enters the mosque on Friday before the prayer, one prays voluntary prayers. It is encouraged to pray before the Friday prayer as well as to offer voluntary prayers after it as one wishes. It is disliked for one who has arrived and for those already in the mosque to climb over one another's necks. It was said to me (may Allah's prayers be upon him): to sit away from the Friday gathering is something I dislike, even if the Imam is already seated, if it means stepping over people's necks. Whoever comes to the mosque should sit where he finds space without climbing over people's necks. If he finds them tightly packed, he may make his way carefully among them to his place, and if he finds space at the front of the mosque it is permissible to walk toward it. Speaking is forbidden once the Imam rises to the pulpit. The people are instructed to face toward him and to listen attentively to the sermon. Once the Imam finishes the sermon, speaking between the two prayers is permissible.

When the Imam enters the mosque on Friday, he ascends the two steps of the pulpit, turns his face toward the people, greets them, and sits. The muezzins then stand and give the call to prayer before him. When they finish the adhan, they sit down. The Imam rises, praises and glorifies Allah, and sends prayers upon the Prophet and his family.

<!-- page 71 -->
He then delivers the sermon to the people as fully as he is able. He then sits, rises again, praises and glorifies Allah, sends prayers upon the Prophet and his family, and supplicates for the Imam and for the believers as much as he is able. The muezzins then call the iqama, the Imam descends, and he leads the Friday prayer of two rak'ahs. In the first rak'ah, after the opening chapter of the Book, he recites Surah al-Jumu'ah; in the second, after the opening chapter, he recites Surah al-Munafiqun. He recites aloud. After the recitation is completed in the second rak'ah he performs the tashahhud, gives the salam, and departs. He should deliver the sermon holding a sword or staff, imitating that practice if possible. Whoever catches the second rak'ah of the Friday prayer with the Imam before the Imam raises his head from bowing has caught that rak'ah; he adds a second rak'ah to it. If he did not catch it before the Imam raised his head, he has not caught the Friday prayer and should pray four rak'ahs.

The people do not pray the Friday prayer if they miss it; rather, they pray the noon prayer alone. If the Imam is absent and there is no one to lead the prayer in the mosque, they pray the noon prayer of four rak'ahs on their own that afternoon.

Mention of the two Eid prayers:

Allah Almighty said: "The month of Ramadan in which the Quran was revealed" through to His saying: "That you may complete the prescribed period and glorify Allah for having guided you, and perhaps you will be grateful" [al-Baqarah: 185]. He also said: "And a proclamation from Allah and His Messenger to the people on the day of the Greater Pilgrimage" [al-Tawbah: 3]. It has been narrated from the Imams of the family of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and his family), all of them, that Friday is the greatest day, and that within it fall the Visit, the Tawaf al-Ifadah, the slaughter, the throat-cutting, the casting of the pebbles, and the sacrifice. They said (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that the takbeer begins after the completion of the fast on the night of Eid al-Fitr and the day of Eid al-Fitr, as well as on the day of Eid al-Adha, and we shall mention that in its proper place, God willing.

<!-- page 72 -->
He said to me (may Allah's prayers be upon him): I encourage a person to devote himself — four nights in the year — to prayer and the remembrance of Allah Almighty: the night of Eid al-Fitr, the night of Eid al-Adha, the night of the fifteenth of Sha'ban, and the first night of Rajab. He also said to me (may Allah's prayers be upon him): I heard the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) addressing the people on the day of Eid al-Adha and saying: "On this day, no blood is shed before the believer — if his intention was sincere — but that the first drop of it is an expiation for every sin. So hasten to Allah in supplication, for by Him in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, none emerge from this gathering except the forgiven, save the one who has persisted in one of the major sins."

It has been narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that it is recommended to perform ghusl on the day of Eid al-Fitr and the day of Eid al-Adha before going out to the prayer ground, to break the fast on the day of Eid al-Fitr before going out to the prayer ground as much as possible, and not to eat on the day of Eid al-Adha until after the prayer. One should wear the finest clothes one possesses for both Eids, rejoice at whatever good one has prepared, and the Imam should be sheltered whether in winter or summer.

Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali — if he wished to go out for the two Eids or for Friday — would say: "O Allah, whoever has prepared, exerted himself, and made ready, and that preparation is beneficial to a creature — grant it to him; and You are permitted to prepare me. Prepare me, and make my readiness please You and earn Your reward and Your gifts. I have gone out to You not to display any righteous deed I have done, nor to seek the intercession of any creature You have been beseeched by, save the intercession of Muhammad and his family. I have come out to You burdened with sins and having wronged myself. O Magnificent One, O Magnificent One — forgive the great sin, for there is none to forgive the great sin save You. There is no god but You." It is also said (may Allah's prayers be upon him) that showing weapons, equipment, and banners on the day of Eid to take pride and to awe the enemy is permissible. The pulpit is brought out and placed on level ground; if it is near the cemetery, that is preferable. The Imam goes out

<!-- page 73 -->
and the people go out in their finest clothes, with dignity, proclaiming the takbeer, glorifying, and praising Allah as they go and return. The Commander of the Believers (may Allah's prayers be upon him) used to go out to the two Eids in Kufa proclaiming the takbeer, with those around him — a thousand of the city's elders and those of mature years — also proclaiming the takbeer. When the people reached the prayer ground, they sat without praying any voluntary prayer before or after the Eid prayer. They sat in awe and reverence, remembering Allah, without crowding; each person sat where he had arrived. The rows should be spaced such that each worshipper has room to prostrate and bow. If this is not possible, the prayer is not valid for him.

When the Imam arrives, he begins with the prayer before the sermon — this is the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family). After him, matters changed under 'Uthman: he prayed the Eid prayer with the people, and when he finished, most of the people dispersed and did not remain to hear the sermon. So he placed the prayer after the sermon — after the sermon — so that they would not leave him as they did on Friday. But the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) should not be altered or changed or diverted from what he established. Allah Almighty said: "Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it; and whatever he forbids you, refrain from it" [al-Hashr: 7]. He also said: "So let those beware who oppose his command, lest fitnah befall them or a painful punishment befall them" [al-Nur: 63].

The Imam opens the prayer without adhan or iqama, for there is neither adhan nor iqama for the two Eids. He begins with the takbir al-ihram and recites the opening chapter of the Book and Surah al-Shams, reciting aloud. He then recites five additional takbeers, and between each two takbeers he recites a light qunut, which is to say: "O Allah, forgive me, have mercy on me, heal me, and pardon me in this world and the hereafter — You are capable of all things." He then pronounces the sixth takbir, raises his hands, and performs two prostrations. He rises for the second rak'ah and recites the opening chapter of the Book and Surah al-Ghashiyah.

<!-- page 74 -->
He then recites four additional takbeers, and between each two takbeers he recites a light qunut. He then pronounces the fifth takbir, raises his hands, and performs two prostrations. He then performs the tashahhud and gives the salam.

He departs and ascends the pulpit. The people face toward him attentively, seated in their places. He delivers two sermons as we described for Friday. In the sermon of Eid al-Fitr he mentions the Sunnah of Zakat al-Fitr and enjoins it, and in the sermon of Eid al-Adha he mentions the sacrifice and its rulings for those who are able to perform it. He then descends and departs, and the people depart. It is recommended for them to return by a different route from the one by which they came out, if they are able to do so. Whoever misses the Eid prayer with the Imam prays it alone at home as four rak'ahs. Women are permitted to go out for the two Eids, but the people of the open countryside have no obligation to gather or go out for the two Eids; rather, the Eid is observed at the times appointed, and the Friday prayer is also observed there. The two Eid prayers require five men, one of whom is the Imam, just as the Friday prayer does. If there are fewer than five, they do not hold the Eid prayer. The traveler has no obligation to observe the feast, though if the people of the valleys attend, whoever among the travelers chooses to attend does so as a virtuous act and earns reward from it. Those from the countryside who are within two leagues of a city are obliged to witness the Eid prayer; those closer than that are likewise obliged.

If the day of Eid coincides with a Friday, the Eid prayer is held at its time and the Friday prayer at its time. The people of the countryside may return to their settlements after the Eid prayer, and this is regarded with approval. If the people do not learn of the Eid until after midday, they go out and perform the Eid prayer and break their fast — whether it is Eid al-Fitr. If they do not learn of it until after midday, they break their fast and then go out the following day at the time of the prayer, praying the Eid prayer in the manner they would have prayed on the day of Eid itself. The time for going out to the prayer is when the sun has risen to some height; going out on the day of Eid al-Fitr is delayed slightly compared to the day of Eid al-Adha. The takbeer on the days of Tashreeq runs from the Fajr prayer on the day of Arafah through to the Asr prayer on the last


<!-- page 75 -->
The days of Tashreeq conclude after the Asr prayer, at which point the Takbeer is cut off. The Takbeer on the days of Tashreeq is obligatory upon men and women alike, whether traveling or resident, and is to be recited after every prescribed prayer — not after supererogatory prayers. The Takbeer on the days of Tashreeq is: "Allahu Akbar, Allahu Akbar, la ilaha illallah, Allahu Akbar, Allahu Akbar, wa lillahi al-hamd. Allahu Akbar, alhamdulillah ala ma hadana, wa Allahu Akbar ala ma razaqana min baha'im al-an'am." The Takbeer recited when going out to the prayer ground on the day of sacrifice and the day of al-Fitr is similar, except that in the case of al-Fitr, one does not say "for our sustenance of the cattle." The days of Tashreeq are the three days following the day of sacrifice. They are so named because the meat is spread out and dried in the sun during those days. The day of Arafah is the day before the slaughter. The day of slaughter is the day of Eid — that is, the day of Arafah is the day before it, and the day of sacrifice is the Eid itself. The day is called thus because people settle in Mina. Then comes the first day of Tashreeq, followed by the second, followed by the third — the last day of Tashreeq — on which the Takbeer is recited after Asr and then cut off. If people pray in congregation during the days of Tashreeq, the imam recites the Takbeer aloud; when he finishes, those praying behind him recite it aloud as well. If the imam preceded some worshippers in a portion of the prayer and then stood to make up what he missed, he recites the Takbeer after completing his prayer and offering the salutation.

- **Mentioning the prostrations of omission in prayer:**

It has been narrated from the imams of the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that they said: whoever forgets to recite the opening Takbeer at the beginning of his prayer must repeat the prayer. Whoever doubts whether he completed a bow in any rak'ah should bow and then perform two prostrations of omission. If one has no doubt that he prayed one or two rak'ahs, and it becomes established that he prayed one, he builds upon that and performs the prostrations of omission. If it is not established, he begins the prayer anew, treating the rak'ah he is in as the first of his prayer.

<!-- page 76 -->
He then performs the two prostrations of omission. If he is in doubt — not knowing whether he has prayed two or three rak'ahs — he builds upon certainty, which is two, and performs the prostrations of omission. If he doubts whether he has prayed three or four rak'ahs, he performs two rak'ahs in a sitting position and then prostrates the prostrations of omission, for in doing so he has completed four rak'ahs — and if he has already prayed four, the additional rak'ahs count as supererogatory. This is because the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family said that the prayer of one who sits without a valid reason is half the prayer of one who stands. If one doubts without knowing whether he has prayed two or four rak'ahs, he sits for the testimony and the salutation, then prays two rak'ahs in which he recites the opening of the Book, sits for the testimony, gives the salutation, and then performs the two prostrations of omission. If it turns out he had only prayed two rak'ahs, his prayer is now complete; if he had already prayed four, then those last two rak'ahs are supererogatory for him — but he should treat them in his heart as though they were obligatory.

Whoever omits a bow must repeat the prayer. Whoever is obligated to perform a prostration and remembers it while still seated must perform it and then sit for the testimony and prostrate the prostration of omission. Whoever forgets a prostration must perform the prostrations of omission, sit for the testimony after them, and then offer the salutation. If one forgets and leaves the prayer without giving the salutation, the testimony itself is sufficient as a substitute for the salutation, since the testimony contains the phrase "peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah." Whoever forgets to recite the Fatiha but completes the bowing and prostration need not repeat the prayer; he performs the two prostrations of omission. If he omits the Fatiha deliberately, however, he must repeat the prayer. If one forgets to sit for the first tashahhud and rises for the third rak'ah without sitting, but remembers before bowing, he should sit and then perform the prostration of omission after completing the prayer. The prostrations of omission apply to all the cases we have mentioned. If one remembers after the salutation but before standing and before speaking, he should prostrate, then sit for the testimony of the Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him), and before departing from the prayer, he completes it and sits for the testimony and performs the two prostrations of omission. Likewise, if one departs from the prayer and speaks, he must complete what remains of his prayer and perform the prostrations of omission.

<!-- page 77 -->
The two prostrations of omission — and so it was with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family. He prayed only two rak'ahs of a prescribed prayer; someone asked him, "Has the prayer been shortened, or did you forget, O Messenger of Allah?" He said that he had prayed only two rak'ahs. He then stood before the people and prayed two more rak'ahs, gave the salutation, and then prostrated the two prostrations of omission, then gave the salutation and departed.

Whoever is correct in his prayer yet sits for the testimony at the end of his prayer and then stands up, having thereby added an extra element, what he has added becomes supererogatory for him, and he performs the two prostrations of omission. If he does not sit for the testimony and adds an extra bow, he must repeat the prayer. If he adds an extra prostration, he completes the prayer and performs the prostrations of omission.

Regarding one who is in doubt — not knowing whether he has increased or decreased in prayer — he sits for the testimony and the salutation, then prays two rak'ahs in which he recites the opening of the Book, sits for the testimony, gives the salutation, and then performs the prostrations of omission. As for doubt about a bow that arises after one has already prostrated and proceeded with the prayer — that doubt is dismissed. Similarly, doubt about a prostration that arises after one has already risen is dismissed. And doubt that arises about any part of the prayer after the prayer has been completed is also dismissed, until certainty is established. Deliberate omission, if remembered afterward, requires the prayer to be repeated. Whoever experiences frequent doubts should proceed on the basis of certainty, build upon it, and perform the two prostrations of omission. If an excess occurs in his prayer, he should proceed with it unless he is certain of the excess. Whoever follows the imam while the imam is praying on his behalf bears no responsibility.

- **Mentioning what interrupts the prayer:**

If something arises that poses a threat to oneself, one's wealth, or others, and it is possible to address it while in prayer, it is permissible to interrupt the prayer; one then begins again and resumes.

<!-- page 78 -->
The prayer. An occurrence of ritual impurity during the prayer interrupts it; if a worshipper loses his purity while praying, he should leave, perform ablution, and resume the prayer. Whoever vomits or suffers a nosebleed during prayer may remove the vomit and wash away the blood without turning away from the qibla; he may then continue with his prayer. If, however, he turns away from the qibla, he must begin the prayer again. Whoever speaks during the prayer has interrupted it and must repeat it. Whoever, while praying, remembers that he is in a state of ritual impurity or has not attained purity, must cut off the prayer, purify himself, and then begin the prayer again. If he remembers this after completing the prayer, he purifies himself and repeats the prayer — even if he was the imam; those praying behind him must also repeat their prayers. A worshipper does not interrupt the prayer on account of something passing in front of him, but should ward it off as much as possible. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said one day: a woman passed in front of him, then a donkey, then a dog passed by — and those present looked on. When he finished, he said, "I saw what you saw, and nothing interrupts the prayer of a believer." Ward off what you can.

- **Mentioning the preceding prayer:**

If a man arrives while the imam is leading the people in prayer, he should enter the prayer with the opening Takbeer before the imam bows, or before the imam raises his head from bowing; he then bows with the imam and that rak'ah is counted for him. If he did not enter before the imam raised his head from the bow, he enters with the imam in the prayer and prostrates with him in his prostration, but that rak'ah is not counted for him, nor are the ones that follow it — rather, he treats what comes next as the beginning of his prayer. If the imam sat for the first tashahhud, the latecomer does not sit with him, but waits; when the imam rises for the third rak'ah, it counts as the latecomer's second. If the imam prostrates, the latecomer prostrates with him, and when the imam rises for the third rak'ah, the latecomer returns to the ground briefly and witnesses.

<!-- page 79 -->
He recites the testimony lightly, then rises. When the imam sits for the final tashahhud, the latecomer sits as the imam sat — fully — but does not recite the testimony himself. When the imam gives the salutation, the latecomer does not give the salutation; he rises and prays a rak'ah, then sits in a composed manner, recites the testimony, gives the salutation, and departs.

If the imam preceded the latecomer by two rak'ahs, the latecomer treats the rak'ah he joined as the first of his prayer. When the imam gives the salutation, the latecomer does not give it; he rises and prays two rak'ahs to complete his prayer, then sits for the testimony and the salutation and departs. The testimony he performs when the imam departs from the prayer serves as his first tashahhud. If he joins the prayer with only one rak'ah remaining, he builds upon it and treats it as the first of his prayer, following the posture of the imam in sitting and rising as we have described.

If he misses a rak'ah — even in the Maghrib prayer, where the imam preceded him by one rak'ah — he enters with the imam in the second, also treating it as his first. He sits when the imam sits for the first tashahhud, though he treats it as his own second. When the imam rises for the second rak'ah, the latecomer treats it as his third. When the imam sits for the second tashahhud, the latecomer sits with him and recites the first tashahhud for himself. When the imam gives the salutation, the latecomer rises, prays a rak'ah, sits, recites the testimony, gives the salutation, and departs. If he does not catch a rak'ah with the imam, he treats what he has as the first of his prayer. If the imam sits for the testimony, the latecomer sits as we have described and cannot proceed otherwise. He prays a rak'ah in which he recites the opening of the Book and a surah, then sits for the first tashahhud, then rises and completes his prayer.

If the imam preceded the latecomer by one rak'ah in the Fajr prayer, and the imam sat for the testimony, the latecomer also sits as we described — but does not recite the testimony himself. When the imam gives the salutation, the latecomer prays a rak'ah, sits, recites the testimony, gives the salutation, and departs. If the latecomer forgot that the imam had given the salutation and then remembered, he makes up what he missed and performs the prostrations of omission. This is what the imams of the household of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said.

<!-- page 80 -->
- **Mentioning the time at which boys are commanded to pray:**

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: the pen is lifted from three — the child until he reaches puberty, the sleeper until he wakes, and the insane until he recovers. Boys are not obligated to pray in the same sense as adults — prayer does not become truly obligatory for them until they reach puberty. However, it has been narrated from Ali and the imams of his lineage, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that they instructed that boys should be encouraged toward prayer once they are able to understand it, and toward fasting if they are able to bear it — not as a strict obligation, but to train them, accustom them, and ensure that they do not neglect it. When they reach puberty, they will have already come to know the prayer and will have been maintaining it, and it will not come upon them suddenly as a burden, causing them to become bored and negligent. For when habits are formed gradually, they become like second nature.

It has also been narrated from them, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that whoever performs a good deed — whether prayer, fasting, or otherwise — and that deed is one of the gates of righteousness that is sustained without interruption, it should not be abandoned; rather, people should be led to goodness gradually and made to find it easy. If one is told at the outset that something will weigh upon him forever, he may feel overwhelmed — but if he is told to begin with a sunnah, that is easier for him, and if he has a sunnah established, the transition to full obligation is easier still, and he will generally be unable to abandon what he has grown familiar with and trained himself to do easily.

They also commanded that boys be instructed in prayer and fasting before puberty. They said: children of seven years of age should be commanded to pray, and they should be disciplined if they abandon it by the age of nine. They should be separated in their beds when they reach the age of ten, and distinctions made between them in sleeping arrangements at that stage — as a precaution, given their proximity to one another in bed and their closeness to the age of puberty and the stirrings of desire for intercourse, lest proximity in bed lead any of them into something to be feared. This is the discipline of the guardians of Allah's servants whom Allah has protected and over whose affairs — both religious and worldly — He has taken care.


<!-- page 81 -->
- Mentioning the Traveler's Prayer:

The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Allah has given my nation a gift that has not been given to any other nation" — a blessing from Allah Almighty. It was said: "O Messenger of Allah, what is this gift?" He said: "Breaking the fast and shortening the prayers during travel. Whoever does not do so has returned his gift to Allah." He also said: "Whoever shortens the prayer in a journey has accepted the relief of Allah Almighty, and whoever completes it has rejected it." And he said: "Allah forbids that the full prayer be performed while travelling." Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: "I am innocent of those who pray four rak'ahs during a journey." He further said that whoever completes the prayer while travelling and knows that the Sunnah requires shortening it must repeat the prayer, but if he does not know this, then he need not repeat it.

The Imams (may Allah's prayers be upon them) held that it is obligatory during travel to shorten every prayer that consists of more than two rak'ahs, while Maghrib is not shortened whether in travel or at home. They also said that the traveler should pray the Sunnah and nafl prayers: if he is walking during the day, he may do so while walking, and he prays the obligatory prayer as two rak'ahs. As for the end of his journey by night, he spends it in the night prayer — they considered this praiseworthy and disliked leaving it. The traveler is not absolved of the obligation to shorten prayers whether in travel or at home.

They stated (may peace be upon them) that the distance of travel at which shortening and breaking the fast become obligatory is two hundred parasangs — the farsakhs being reckoned as four leagues, each farsakh being three miles, and each mile being three thousand cubits, which equals one thousand moderate steps taken with one leg from where the right foot is raised to where it is placed — even if it is a destination that a traveler intends to reach and return from in less than ten days. Such a traveler shortens and breaks his fast until he departs. The traveler breaks his fast and shortens his prayers from the moment he leaves his house and begins his journey.

<!-- page 82 -->
He should return and enter his house only when he is resident again. If a traveler joins a congregational prayer led by a resident, he prays two rak'ahs and then departs. If a resident prays behind a traveling imam, the imam prays two rak'ahs and then commands them to complete the prayer — both situations are disliked, as has been mentioned, because the traveler's ruling in prayer differs from that of the resident. Whoever missed a prayer while resident and remembers it while travelling makes it up in full as it was originally upon him. Whoever missed a prayer while travelling and remembers it while resident makes it up as it was, namely shortened.

The traveler may perform voluntary prayers while riding, without facing the Qibla. This is supported by the words of Allah Almighty: "Wherever you turn, there is the face of Allah" [al-Baqarah: 115]. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family used to perform voluntary prayers while riding, not facing the Qibla, as attested during the journey back from the Farewell Pilgrimage with the Ka'bah behind him. However, this applies only to nafl prayers; the obligatory prayer may not be prayed except on the ground, facing the Qibla. It is also forbidden to pray on a thoroughfare — that is, the path along which people walk — or between two roads.

The traveler may shorten prayers at sea and pray on a ship. If the ship is in motion, he faces the Qibla; if he cannot stand, he may pray sitting. In cases of drowning, nakedness, or rough waves, he prays by gesturing, making his prostration lower than his bowing. A naked person should similarly pray sitting if he finds nothing with which to cover his nakedness.

Shortening is not applicable to one who leaves his hometown looking for a runaway slave, or a prince searching for his subject, or a collector pursuing debts, or sailors whose way of life is travel — for such travel is not considered a journey in the legal sense. Similarly, one who alights during a journey at the home of his family without intending to stay does not complete the prayer. But whoever alights at a place during his journey intending to stay for ten days must complete the prayer; if he is uncertain about his length of stay, he shortens it. If his stay is prolonged unintentionally and he intends to depart each day, he shortens the prayer for one month, then completes it after that month until he departs.

<!-- page 83 -->
- Mentioning the Prayer of the Sick:

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that if a sick person cannot pray standing, he prays lying on his back with the Qibla in front of him, in the direction of his feet, or lying on his right side facing the Qibla, gesturing in a way that makes his prostration lower than his bowing. Likewise, one who is sitting and cannot kneel or prostrate does the same. One who has a nosebleed that cannot be stopped, or has a wound that continues to discharge and cannot be bandaged, performs ablution and prays in his condition, for Allah excuses His servant when he is thus afflicted.

If a sick person cannot perform any prayer beyond the obligatory, then when he recovers, if he wishes he may make up what he left of Sunnah and nafl prayers, for that brings virtue and reward — though making them up is not obligatory as it is for the obligatory prayers. If a sick person is able to pray sitting with his legs bent, he does so as he would bend them when standing; if he cannot, he prays in whatever manner he is able. He is rewarded for reciting the Opening of the Book alone if that is all he can manage. If he cannot bow and prostrate except by a gesture of praise, then he does so; if he can prostrate but not more, then he prostrates; and if he cannot, he nods his head, making his prostration lower than his bowing. If he cannot nod his head, he uses his eyes or whatever he is able to use, for Allah does not burden a soul beyond what it can bear.

If a sick person is able to pray standing, he must do so; but if standing aggravates his condition and increases his illness, he may pray in the manner that has been described, according to his ability. Whoever faints must make up the prayers he missed when he regains consciousness, whether that is one prayer or more.

<!-- page 84 -->
- Mentioning the Prayer of Fear:

Allah Almighty said: "There is no blame upon you if you shorten the prayer if you fear that those who disbelieved may harm you. Indeed, the disbelievers are a clear enemy to you. And when you are among them and lead them in prayer, let a group of them stand with you…" [al-Nisa': 101–102], up to His saying: "a humiliating punishment." Allah Almighty has thus permitted shortening the prayer in a state of fear without excluding those who are resident from those who are travelling. Abu 'Abd-Allah Ja'far ibn Muhammad ibn 'Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) was asked about the prayer of fear and the prayer of travel. He was asked whether both involve shortening, and he replied: "Yes, and the prayer of fear is more deserving of shortening than the prayer of travel, for in the prayer of travel there is no fear."

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) prayed the prayer of fear in the Battle of Dhat al-Riqa'. His companions divided into two groups: one division he stationed to face the enemy, while the other stood behind him. He recited aloud, they listened, and then he bowed, and they bowed and prostrated. Then the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) stood, and they prayed another rak'ah on their own, then they gave the greeting of peace, turned to face their companions, and stood opposite the enemy. The second group came and stood behind the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family. He recited aloud, they listened, then he bowed, they bowed, and they prostrated. Then the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) sat while they stood and prayed another rak'ah on their own, then they sat and bore witness, and gave the greeting of peace together.

The Imams (may Allah's prayers be upon them) said: "This is how the prayer of fear is prayed, when it is possible, in all prayers except Maghrib, which is not shortened." — as may have been mentioned earlier. In such a prayer, the congregation prays the first rak'ah with the imam, then prays the remaining two rak'ahs on their own as described in the prayer of

<!-- page 85 -->
the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family. The second group prays with him two rak'ahs, so that all of them have heard the recitation in the first two rak'ahs in which he recited aloud.

The Imams (may Allah's prayers be upon them) said: if circumstances do not allow this manner of prayer and fear intensifies, they may pray on horseback or on foot, nodding their heads and making prostration lower than bowing, or they may dismount if unable to do so otherwise. If they cannot face the Qibla, they face it for the opening takbir and then turn to face the enemy. This accords with the saying of Allah Almighty: "Whether you are on foot or riding" [al-Baqarah: 239]. If the fighting becomes so fierce and the battle so intense and relentless that they cannot even nod their heads, they pray with the takbir, making a takbir for each rak'ah with the intention of prayer.

Similarly, the Commander of the Believers (may Allah's prayers be upon him) and his companions prayed in this way on the night and day of al-Harir.

- Mentioning the Eclipse Prayer:

It has been narrated that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "I was startled by the eclipse of the moon, and Jibril (peace be upon him) came to me. I said to him: 'What is this, Jibril?' He said: 'These are angels who have been obedient to Allah since He created them and have never disobeyed Him. But this is a sign and a lesson.' I said: 'What should be done?' He said: 'Prayer and recitation of the Qur'an.'" The Prophet (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) and his family, when the sun or moon underwent an eclipse, would say to the people: "Hasten to your mosques."

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that the prayer for the solar eclipse and the lunar eclipse is the same, except that the solar eclipse prayer is somewhat longer. When an eclipse occurs, the people should be called to prayer without the adhan, and they should gather at the Grand Mosque if they are in a city. The imam comes forward and recites in an audible voice, opening with the Fatiha and the longest

<!-- page 86 -->
surah he can recite. He then makes a second takbir and bows, and they bow and remain in the bowing position as long as he remains, praising Allah and saying what is said in bowing. Then he raises his head with a takbir and they rise, and he recites the Opening of the Book and a long surah similar to what he read the first time, reciting aloud before bowing. He then makes a takbir and bows — and so it continues for five bowings after which, following the second set of rak'ahs, he raises his head from the fifth bowing and says: "Allah hears those who praise Him," saying this only before the tenth prostration. For the remaining bowings, when he raises his head he says "Allahu akbar" and then prostrates.

He prolongs the prostration as he prolongs the bowing, praising Allah and saying what is said in prostration. Then he raises his head, sits between the two prostrations, says what is said in that position in the most complete manner, then performs the second prostration in the same way, and rises to pray another five bowings in the

same manner as the first. He then prostrates and sits for a long tashahhud in which he calls upon Allah, expresses hope in Him, and supplicates for what he wishes of the affairs of this world and the Hereafter. He then gives the greeting of peace if the eclipse has cleared, or else he remains seated facing the Qibla and instructs the people to sit, recite the Qur'an, pray, and remember Allah until the eclipse clears or passes.

The eclipse prayer is not to be performed until the eclipse is actually seen and confirmed. One should not rely merely on someone saying that the sun has eclipsed without having seen it, or on a claim made at night that the moon has eclipsed during the day, or during the day that the sun has eclipsed at night. The solar eclipse prayer is not prayed except during the day, nor is the lunar eclipse prayer prayed except at night after the eclipse has been seen.

The qunut in the eclipse prayer is performed in every other rak'ah before the second, fourth, sixth, eighth, and tenth bowings. It is also permissible to recite in every rak'ah of every prayer, or more than that, before bowing. In that case, the Opening of the Book is recited only at the beginning of each surah — that is, he makes it the first opening of the surah — and likewise when he rises from the prostration in the second eclipse prayer.


<!-- page 87 -->
These two rak'ahs with four prostrations — five rak'ahs in each rak'ah — were performed by the Commander of the Faithful. The prayers of Allah be upon him: he led the people in the eclipse prayer at any time, whether night or day, whether travelling or resident in a city. If the eclipse coincided with the time of an obligatory prayer, he would begin with the obligatory prayer. If, however, he had already entered the eclipse prayer and feared that the time for the obligatory prayer would expire, he would interrupt the eclipse prayer, perform the obligatory prayer, and then, upon finishing it, resume and build upon what he had already completed of the eclipse prayer. He likewise prayed in this manner at every sign that occurred — whether wind, darkness, tremors, earthquakes, or any other portent — just as the eclipse prayer is prayed. Whoever misses the eclipse prayer or the prayer of the signs does not make it up. It is recommended that the eclipse prayer be performed in an open, cleared space; if the imam departs, those who see the eclipse should be informed, and if he does not depart, they should remain in their places. The people are instructed to remain seated, as we have mentioned.

**Mentioning the Istisqa Prayer:**

Allah Almighty said: *"And when Moses sought water for his people, We said: Strike the rock with your staff."* [Al-Baqarah: 60]

It is narrated that when Moses' people were in need of water and rain fell upon Madinah, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, went out, so concerned for his people because of the intensity of the rain. We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, that the prayer of al-Istisqa is like the two Eid prayers: the imam goes out and the people go out to the prayer ground in humility before the imam and in fear. They humble themselves and do not carry weapons as they do for Eid. The imam prays two rak'ahs without the call to prayer or the iqamah, and recites in them as he recites for the two festival prayers. Then he rises to the pulpit and sits briefly before standing. He then turns his garment, placing what was on his right side to his left and what was on his left to his right, and did likewise.

<!-- page 88 -->
The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, began his first sermon with takbeer and tahleel, seeking forgiveness, and as much praise as he was able and enabled to offer. He then praises Allah and invokes blessings upon the Prophet and calls on the people to seek forgiveness, repentance, and righteous deeds, and makes them aware that rain is withheld on account of their sins, urging them to repent to Allah Almighty, to ask Him, and to implore Him for water and mercy to the extent that He sees fit. It is recommended that this prayer be performed on a Monday.

**Mentioning the Prayers of the Shaf', the Witr, and the Two Rak'ahs of Fajr:**

Allah Almighty said: *"By the even and the odd."* [Al-Fajr: 3]

He swore by all that is praiseworthy. Al-shaf' in the language of the Arabs means the paired or even, and al-witr means the odd. In prayer, witr consists of three rak'ahs, performed as a sunnah of the night prayer after Isha', and no prayer is prayed after it even if the dawn has not yet broken. The name witr applies to these three rak'ahs; the witr may also refer to the final single rak'ah and the two rak'ahs before it, and this is what is preferred in the narrations from the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them. The worshipper gives the salam after the two rak'ahs, then prays a single rak'ah. What is narrated regarding the witr is that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, commanded it and said that Allah is witr and loves the witr, so pray the witr, O people of the Qur'an. He strongly emphasised it and did not permit its abandonment. Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: the witr must not be abandoned, yet it is not among the written obligatory prayers — it is mustahabb. Whoever misses the witr may make it up, though it is not required of him since it is not obligatory. Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, peace be upon him, held that the witr is not obligatory and may be performed while travelling on a mount, as with the supererogatory prayers. They preferred that the witr be prayed at the end of the night. Whoever prays it at the beginning of the night should recite in its two rak'ahs the Opening of the Book.

<!-- page 89 -->
In the first rak'ah he recites the Opening of the Book and *"Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High"* [Al-A'la], and in the second the Opening of the Book and *"Say: O disbelievers"* [Al-Kafirun], and in the third the Opening of the Book and *"Say: He is Allah, the One"* [Al-Ikhlas]. In the witr, after bowing, he raises his hands in qunut and supplicates as much as he is able. He also supplicates in the Fajr prayer before bowing, after the recitation. The two rak'ahs of Fajr are prayed before the Fajr prayer: he hears the call to Fajr and then prays two rak'ahs after the dawn has broken. It is preferred to keep them brief. In the first rak'ah he recites the Opening of the Book and *"Say: O disbelievers"*, and in the second the Opening of the Book and *"Say: He is Allah, the One"*. He should remain seated in his prayer place and continue supplicating until he prays Fajr. They said there is no supplication in the mosque that is a temporary matter that earns a reward through prayer or otherwise; whoever wishes to supplicate may call upon Allah Almighty with whatever he wills.

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, supplications for qunut in many forms, all indicating that there is nothing temporary in it as we have mentioned. The least of what is indicated is that one immerses oneself in it with three glorifications. From them, may Allah's prayers be upon them, have come long, short, and medium supplications for the qunut of witr and the qunut of Fajr. Among what has been narrated from them of the medium length is the following:

The narrations report that they, peace be upon them, instructed that one say in the witr qunut: *"O Allah, You see and are not seen, for nothing is hidden from You. You raised Your eyes, moved Your feet, stretched out necks, spread out hands, and are called upon by tongues, and deeds are submitted to You. O You to whom hands are stretched out and hearts have turned, and before whom eyes are cast down and necks are bowed: I submit to You, complaining of the severity of the times, the dominance of enemies, the fewness of numbers, and the division of hearts. We thank You for Your blessings upon us and upon our Imam, the son of the daughter of our Prophet, who is called the Imam of his time — he is our guide to You and we are those guided by him to You. We ask You to send blessings upon him and his forefathers, and to support him with the mighty victory of his era and his world."*

<!-- page 90 -->
*"Support Your guardians through him, unite hearts in obedience to You and to him, make religion firm through his Imamate, and support him against his treacherous enemies. O Creator of creation, Lord of the worlds. O Allah, make certainty firm in my heart, increase my light, guide me, and forgive me and lead me to Your straight path — Ameen, Ameen. I ask You, O Allah, for good in this world and good in the Hereafter, and I ask You to save me from the torment of the Fire."* Whoever wishes to extend the supplication may add what he wishes.

For the Fajr qunut: *"O Allah, we praise You, seek Your help, ask Your forgiveness, and thank You with good thanks. We do not disbelieve You, we obey You and submit to You, and we distance ourselves from those who oppose and disbelieve You. O Allah, You alone we worship, to You we pray and prostrate, to You we strive, we seek Your mercy and fear Your severe punishment. Surely Your punishment against the disbelievers is impending. O Allah, punish the disbelievers, the hypocrites, the liars, and those who are ungrateful to Your chosen Imams from the pure family of Your Prophet. Descend upon them Your wrath, Your anger, and Your torment. O Allah, punish the disbelievers among the People of the Book, the polytheists, and the associators. Forgive me and the believing men and believing women and the Muslim men and women, and set right what is between them and unite their word in obedience to You and obedience to Your guardians. Establish faith and wisdom in their hearts and make them steadfast in the religion of Your Prophet. Support them over Your enemy and their enemy. O Allah, guide me among those You have guided, take me among those You have taken, heal me among those You have healed, bless me in what You have given, and protect me from the evil of what You have decreed — for You decree and none decrees against You. None is humiliated whom You befriend and none is honoured whom You oppose. Blessed are You, there is no god but You. I ask You for forgiveness and repent to You, and I ask You, O Allah, for good in this world and good in the Hereafter, and I ask You to protect me from the torment of the Fire."* And in the Friday qunut and in the eclipse qunut: *"There is no god but Allah, the Wise and Generous. There is no god but Allah, the Mighty. Glory be to Allah, Lord of the seven heavens and Lord of the seven earths and all that is in them and between them, and Lord of the Great Throne. Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. Send blessings upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad. Make my heart firm upon Your religion and the religion of Your Prophet, and do not cause my heart to deviate."*

<!-- page 91 -->
*"After You have guided me, grant me mercy from You — You are the Most Giving. O Allah, make me one of those You have chosen from Your creation, those whom You have selected for Your religion, and who have repented — for You are capable of all things and You are the Accepting of Repentance, the Most Merciful. May blessings be upon Muhammad and his family, as they are indeed his family, may Allah's prayers be upon them all."*

**Mentioning the Obligatory Prayer, the Sunnah Prayers, and the Supererogatory Prayers:**

As for the obligatory prayer, it is seventeen rak'ahs every day and night. The Dhuhr prayer is the first prayer, called so because it was the first prayer prayed by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, at the time when the obligatory prayer was revealed to him. It consists of four rak'ahs; in the first two he recites the Opening of the Book and a surah and performs the tashahhud after them, and in the last two he recites the Opening of the Book alone, then performs the tashahhud and the tasleem. The Asr prayer is likewise four rak'ahs with the same recitation as Dhuhr. The Maghrib prayer is three rak'ahs: he performs the tashahhud after two rak'ahs in which he recites aloud, then rises and prays a third rak'ah in which he recites the Opening of the Book silently, then sits, performs the tashahhud, and gives the tasleem. The Isha prayer is the last, consisting of four rak'ahs like Dhuhr, except that in the first two rak'ahs he recites aloud. That makes seventeen rak'ahs. The sunnah prayer is performed like the obligatory prayer with all recitation done silently, though if one wishes one may recite quietly in the night prayer. All of it is in sets of two rak'ahs, and in each rak'ah one recites the Opening of the Book and a surah. These consist of six rak'ahs before Dhuhr after the sun has passed its zenith, four rak'ahs after it, four rak'ahs before Asr or after until the sun sets, six rak'ahs after Maghrib before the last Isha prayer, and four rak'ahs before it and four rak'ahs after it. The witr is three rak'ahs: two rak'ahs, then he rises and prays the third rak'ah, then prays two rak'ahs sitting, and two rak'ahs

<!-- page 92 -->
before the Fajr prayer — making thirty-five rak'ahs in total. However, since the two rak'ahs are performed while sitting, they are counted as one rak'ah of kneeling, making the total thirty-four. This is the equivalent of the obligatory prayer in number. It is called the sunnah prayer because the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, used to pray it and adhered to it himself, and likewise the Imams from his descendants, may Allah's prayers be upon them, adhered to it themselves and commanded their guardians and Shi'a to perform it, encouraging them and mentioning its merits and reward to their followers. As for the supererogatory prayers, they have no limit; one volunteers to pray them however one wishes at any permissible time of night or day. It is narrated from 'Ali ibn al-Husayn, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that he used to pray a thousand rak'ahs every day and night, in the best and most excellent manner — praying at night and praying after sleeping.

Allah Almighty said to His Prophet Muhammad, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family: *"And from the night, prostrate to Him as an additional prayer for you; perhaps your Lord will raise you to a praised station."* [Al-Isra: 79] Al-tahajjud is when one wakes from sleep to pray by command — he intends it, then wakes for it, and then prays with it, which is the supererogatory for him, meaning in the Qur'an. He said, O Muzzammil: *"Rise at night, except for a little — half of it or a little less."* [Al-Muzzammil: 2–3]

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, used to stand at night until his feet would swell. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, forbade a man from sleeping through the entire night, likening him to a discarded carcass. It is mustahabb to pray at length at night, and one should not deprive himself of it. Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: if a servant hates to recite the Qur'an and wakes in the night but does not rise, even if he manages only a little, he should rise in the morning and begin his prayers. Whoever does not recite the entire Qur'an should recite as much as he is able. Allah Almighty said: *"Recite what is easy for you of the Qur'an."* [Al-Muzzammil: 20] And whoever wishes may rise for the whole night.


<!-- page 93 -->
With one surah that he repeats, or with some of them, or with two or more surahs, and there is nothing wrong with praying nafl while reciting a portion of a surah in the rak'ah alongside the opening of the book, or pausing between two surahs within it, or between many surahs. Some of them would spend the whole night, or most of it, reciting a single verse if they could rest with it, reflecting upon it, and repeating it so as to understand it and submit to its meaning. They preferred to open the night prayer with two light rak'ahs, then pray afterward as many as they wished. The best form of voluntary devotion — charity and all acts of righteousness — is what is performed privately and in secret. The best of all obligatory acts — prayer, zakat, and others — are performed openly. All nafl prayers are not to be prayed in congregation, whether in Ramadan or at any other time; people pray them alone, and this is preferable when performed at home, where privacy and secrecy are among the virtues. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) prayed voluntarily during the nights of Ramadan in the mosque, and people lined up behind him to pray alongside him. He then withdrew into his room and prayed there. It was 'Umar ibn al-Khattab who was the first to enact the congregational standing in Ramadan. Had there been merit in that practice, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) would not have forbidden it and would have been the first to do so before 'Umar and others. Yet the people narrated this from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and then deliberately followed a path in opposition to him, contradicting his prohibition. Allah Almighty says: "Let those who disagree with his command beware lest a trial befall them or a painful punishment be inflicted upon them" [al-Nur: 63], and He says: "Whatever the Messenger has given you, take it; and whatever he has forbidden you, refrain from it, and fear Allah" [al-Hashr: 7]. Most of what they said in this regard is that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) disliked and forbade it so that it would not become an obligation upon the people, and he said to them: you are close to the Messenger of Allah — may peace be upon him —

<!-- page 94 -->
and you do not claim that he granted a license to others; had there been a license in it, he would have spoken of it as a prophet. May Allah bless him and his family — when he forbade it, it was because Allah Almighty had made the ruling clear. You must refrain from it, as Allah Almighty commanded you to do what you claim is permissible. They claim it was permissible, yet the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) neither did it during the days of his life, nor during the days of Abu Bakr, nor in the early days of 'Umar — only in 'Umar's later period. They themselves say it is bid'ah, and some of them acknowledge it while calling it a good bid'ah — but this contradicts the statement of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him): "Every innovation is misguidance, and every misguidance leads to the Fire. Follow and do not innovate." How can a bid'ah be good when Allah Almighty forbade it on the tongue of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family)? They also invented the Duha prayer, which the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) had forbidden. A man from the Ansar asked Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him), and he replied: "Was he not the first among the Ansar to invent it?" The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) had forbidden it, yet people would come from their homes and enter the mosque while the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) had forbidden them from doing so. Yazid ibn Mu'awiyah revived it at Ras al-Husayn (may Allah's prayers be upon him) — he offered a sacrifice and prayed two rak'ahs, making it a regular practice for himself and for his people from what he had earned, calling it a thanksgiving, according to his claim. As for what Allah's prayers are upon 'Ali ibn al-Husayn (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), he held that one must make up any nafl prayers missed due to being occupied or sleeping, and this is not an obligation but is beloved to Allah — he should not abandon what Allah Almighty had made a part of his devotion or let it go to waste. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Whoever intends to rise at night and then lies down on his bed and says: 'O Allah, do not leave me to my own cunning, do not make me forget Your remembrance, and do not make me among the heedless — I intend to rise, Allah willing,

<!-- page 95 -->
at such and such an hour' — then Allah Almighty entrusts him with an angel, who watches over that hour and awakens him." It is said: "Whoever intends something of the night prayer, and sleep overcomes his eyes, then his sleep becomes a charity from Allah, and Allah is obliged to reward him for his intention, and his night prayer is complete." The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Allah has an angel; when half the night has passed, he declares: 'Praise be to Allah, our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful — there is no god but Him. Let those who strive rise up.' Then after a pause — as Allah wills — he says: 'Glory be to Allah, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful — there is no god but Him. Let those who remember rise up.' Then after another pause — as Allah wills — when dawn comes, he says: 'Praise be to our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful — there is no god but Him,' so as to rouse the heedless." Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said he calls out when a third of the night has passed: "O seekers of good, draw near; O seekers of evil, be few. Is there one who repents, that repentance be accepted? Is there one who seeks forgiveness, that he be forgiven? Is there one who asks, that his question be answered? O Allah, give every one who spends a successor, and give every one who withholds loss." This call goes out every night from the time a third of the night has passed until dawn, and on Friday night from the very beginning of the night until its end. He said: when a third of the night passes, the gates of heaven are opened and an angel calls out until the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) — the people of the highest ranks ride in heaven upon horses with golden bridles, saddles of pearl and ruby, and they pass by those beneath them, who say: "Our Lord, what dignity have You bestowed upon Your servants!" And it is said to them: "They used to rise at night while you slept; they used to fast during the day while you ate; they fought while you were cowards; and they spent in the way of Allah while you were miserly."

<!-- page 96 -->
**Mentioning Prostration in the Qur'an**

Allah Almighty said: "And when the Qur'an is recited to them, they do not prostrate" [al-Inshiqaq: 21], meaning at the places of prostration within it. We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that there are fifteen prostrations in the Qur'an; whoever recites them or hears them recited must prostrate, and some of them are obligatory such that one cannot omit the prostration at that point. We will mention these in their proper place.

The first prostration in the Qur'an is at the end of Surah al-A'raf; then in Surah al-Ra'd, "and their shadows in the mornings and evenings"; in Surah al-Nahl, "and they do what they are commanded"; in Surah al-Isra', "and increase them in reverence"; in Surah Maryam, "they fall prostrate and weeping"; in Surah al-Hajj, "Allah does what He wills," and in it also, "do good that you may succeed"; in Surah al-Furqan, "and it increases their aversion"; in Surah al-Naml, "the Lord of the great throne"; in Surah al-Sajdah, "and they are arrogant," which is one of the obligatory ones; in Surah Sad, "and he bowed and repented"; in Surah Fussilat, "prostrate to Him if you worship Him," which is one of the obligatory ones; at the end of Surah al-Najm, which is also obligatory; in Surah al-Inshiqaq, "and when the Qur'an is recited to them, they do not prostrate"; and in Surah al-'Alaq, "Read in the name of your Lord who created."

Whoever is in prayer and hears a reciter reciting a prostration verse, or has it recited before him, and does not prostrate for it, should incline his head in a nod; or he may intend to prostrate if it is one of the obligatory ones. They disliked reciting in prayer a surah containing one of the obligatory prostrations — for if he recites it he must prostrate, even if it falls at the end of the surah; he then prostrates, rises, recites al-Fatihah, performs ruku', and prostrates. If he is in the middle of an obligatory prostration verse, he has the option: he may prostrate or not. Whoever recites a prostration verse while riding prostrates wherever he is facing, nodding with his head. There is no takbir or witnessing in the Qur'anic prostration, and no taslim. Whoever prostrates upon hearing it — whether he was listening to the Qur'an or reciting it — should do so even if he is not in a state of ablution, praising Allah and supplicating while in prostration, asking Allah Almighty and calling upon Him as he wills.

<!-- page 97 -->
**The Book of Funerals**

**Mentioning the Sick, Visiting the Ill, and Its Rewards**

We have narrated from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that Allah Almighty says: "If My servant is afflicted with a disease and he is patient three times — he does not complain — and Allah knows best for his condition, I will replace his flesh with better flesh and his blood with better blood. If he dies, he dies in My mercy; if I heal him, I heal him without any sin upon him." He said (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family): they are purified by a Forgiving Lord. He said (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family): the sick person who groans has good deeds written for him equal to those of one who is patient; and if he panics, only panic is recorded for him and he has no reward. The Commander of the Faithful, 'Ali ibn Abi Talib (may Allah's prayers be upon him), said: the sick person remains in the custody of Allah — unless he complains to other than his Lord — and his misdeeds are erased from him. He said (may Allah's prayers be upon him): if a servant is afflicted, his sins fall from him in proportion to his illness, and from your illness comes a reward you cannot count. He said (may peace be upon him): there is no need to visit the sick until after three days, and women are not to be visited in illness. He said (may Allah's prayers be upon him): whoever visits the sick seeking the face of Allah and fulfilling his appointment will be in the gardens of Paradise; he will not sit with the sick person and then depart without Allah sending seventy thousand angels that day to pray for him until nightfall. If the visit is at night, then until morning. Visiting should therefore be hastened early in the day. Allah Almighty said: "So Allah has made the people prosper" [al-Rum: 30], meaning He guided them to success. He said (may peace be upon him): it is from the natural disposition (fitrah) that the dying person be directed toward the Qiblah. We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (may Allah's prayers be upon them) that they ordered this, and that he should be laid on his back and received with his face and the sole of his foot directed toward the Qiblah if possible. If not, then he should be placed on his side, turned to face the Qiblah. They also ordered that the testimony of "There is no god but Allah and that

<!-- page 98 -->
Muhammad is His servant and His messenger" be repeated aloud at his head; the Verse of the Throne and the two verses that follow it are to be recited, then: "Your Lord is Allah, Who created the heavens and the earth in six days" [al-A'raf: 54] and so on to the end of the verse; and the final third of Surah al-Baqarah: "To Him belongs all that is in the heavens and the earth; and whether you reveal what is in yourselves or conceal it" [al-Baqarah: 284] and onwards. He is called to, and it is said to him that his appointed term has come, so his matter should be made easy for him and his soul drawn forth until he is content with his Lord and his Lord is content with him. Al-Bushra — may Allah forgive him and have mercy upon him. Ja'far ibn Muhammad (may Allah's prayers be upon him) said: if a believer among you dies and is prevented from speaking, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family), 'Ali, Fatimah, al-Hasan, and al-Husayn come to him, and it is said to him: "The Messenger of Allah, may peace be upon him and his family, is here. As for what you feared, you have been saved from it; as for what you were hoping for, it is before you." Then a door is opened for him to heaven, and he sees his dwelling from it, and it is said to him: "If you wish to return to this world, it will be returned to you with all its gold and silver." He says: "I have no need of this world or anything in it," and follows the word of Allah Almighty: "For them are glad tidings in the life of this world and in the Hereafter" [Yunus: 64]. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family) was asked about the death of the believer — how it may be easy for him — and also about the death of the disbeliever. He said: the believing servant has descended upon him from heaven what no calamity can compare to, until death comes to him and he has not yet reached that degree; and death presses upon him so that he may reach it. Perhaps Allah commands the Angel of Death to return the soul of the believer by drawing it out from its lowest point, so that people may see death pressing hard upon him, as a mercy and relief from Allah Almighty upon His servant. Or perhaps He commands him to draw out the soul of the disbeliever in one gentle stroke — like pulling a hair from wet dough — so that people see it as easy for him, though he has met with great punishment. He said (may Allah's prayers be upon him): death is a blessing for the believer. He said (may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family): the believer


<!-- page 99 -->
He is rested from it, and the one who is rested has been relieved from the sorrow and gloom of this world and has come to the mercy of God. As for the disbeliever, the servants of God are relieved of him. He said, peace be upon him, "Increase the remembrance of the destroyer of pleasures." It was asked, "O Messenger of Allah, what is the destroyer of pleasures?" He said, "Death." And, peace be upon him, are not the believers the most frequent in mentioning and preparing for death? The Commander of the Believers preached, prayers of Allah upon him, and said: "I have never seen certainty of faith more resembling doubt than this matter concerning man and his own soul." Every day he says farewell, and to the graves he goes and returns, yet he does not take heed of the vanity of this world or turn away from its desires and pleasures. Woe to the poor son of Adam — he has no escape from death, and no account of the dead can be settled. It scatters his gathering, separates his Friday, and orphans his children; he ought to be deeply cautious about his state. Yet people have neglected death because of the heedlessness of those to whom death has not yet come, and they have turned to this world and its desires in comfort — a people who hope for reckoning but do not fear punishment.

**Mentioning the grief at the time of death and condolences:**

When Ibrahim, the son of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, died, the Prophet ordered him to be prepared. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, shrouded him and carried him, and he said, "O Ali, carry him." So he carried him until they reached al-Baqi', and the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, prayed over him. He said, "O Ali, I will carry him," and he carried him until they brought him to the grave, and he said, "O Ali, go down into the grave," and he went down into it, and he handed him to him. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, when he saw him in position, wept, and the people wept with him until the voices of the men rose louder than the voices of the women. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, then forbade them from wailing and said, "Tears in the eyes and sadness in the heart — but do not say what angers the Lord. I am with you, O Ibrahim." He said, prayers of Allah be upon him and his family,

<!-- page 100 -->
"It is not the crying that is prohibited — what is prohibited is wailing and lamentation. This crying is a mercy that God Almighty places in the heart of whomever He wills among His creation, and God has mercy on whomever He wills from among His merciful servants." He said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, "Whoever is afflicted with a calamity, let him remember his calamity in me, for it is a consolation from every disaster." Peace be upon him, he said, "Patience is at the first shock" — meaning at the moment of bereavement. He said, peace be upon him, "Whoever loses three children, they will be a barrier shielding him from the Fire." It was said, "And two, O Messenger of Allah?" He said, "And two." When the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, was near death, his family heard his words but could not see his person, and he said, "Peace be upon you, O Ahl al-Bayt, and the mercy and blessings of God. Every soul is dying, but anticipate your reward on the Day of Judgment. Whoever is saved from the Fire and admitted into Paradise has truly won — and what is the life of this world?" May Allah Almighty grant solace from every calamity and reward those who are patient. All things that perish return to Allah, so place your hope in Him, worship Him, and know that the one who laments in anguish is deprived of reward. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, used to take the oath of allegiance from women except regarding lamentation, saying, "One third of the deeds of the pre-Islamic era is not to wail." People engage in it until the Day of Resurrection — bewailing through stars, and stabbing at lineage, and wailing. Ja'far ibn Muhammad, prayers of Allah upon him, instructed upon his death, saying, "Do not strike your cheeks, and do not tear your garments." The Commander of the Believers, prayers of Allah upon him, wrote to his governor over Ahwaz: "Do not allow wailing over the dead in any land under your authority."

<!-- page 101 -->
**Mentioning the washing of the deceased:**

The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, instructed those who would perform his ghusl and said: "Gabriel will be with you to turn me over. Pass by al-Fadl ibn Abbas so that he may close his eyes and pour the water for you, for he does not see my nakedness." And another who was blind washed him, peace be upon him. He, peace be upon him, said, "I was assisting him, and I felt a hand joining mine, though I could not see it." When they began washing him, one of them wished to remove his shirt, and a voice said, "Do not remove the shirt." So they washed him in his shirt. When they wished to pour water over his face, they were told not to do so, and instead turned him to his side and washed him. He was washed three times — with water mixed with lote-tree leaves, then with water mixed with camphor, and finally with pure water. Thus the deceased should be washed according to the words of the Ahl al-Bayt, prayers of Allah upon them: three washings — a washing with water and lote-tree leaves, a washing with water and camphor, and a third washing with pure water. The last of them is pure water. He is turned to his side, and water is not poured directly over his face, nor is he sat upright as the common people do with the dead. The washing of the deceased is like the washing from major ritual impurity — one begins by washing the hands, then the private parts. The washer wraps a cloth around his hands when washing the private parts and gently wipes the stomach as he pours water over the private parts to allow whatever may come out to exit. If he then washes the face, it is done three times; the hands are washed to the elbows; the head is wiped along with the ears; and water flows over the rest of the body. He is turned so that water reaches what is beneath, and he is washed unclothed with the area between the navel and knees covered with a cloth. Even if washed in a garment, that which is stained or soiled must be purified. The soles of his feet are rubbed if they have dirt on them, using pottery or stone. If there is something beneath his nails, it is removed. The head and beard are washed with lote-tree leaves, and care is taken in their cleanliness and in removing all stains and dirt. If it is necessary to heat the water

<!-- page 102 -->
to wash him, this is permissible. He is also washed three times as described. The washer must cover the private parts of the deceased and attend to what he is doing without mentioning anything unsightly that he may observe. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, said: "No Muslim who washes his Muslim brother without defaming him, without looking at his nakedness, and without mentioning any evil from him — then spreads him, prays over him, and sits until he is placed in his grave — except that he emerges purified of his sins."

We have narrated from the Imams of the family of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, that they commanded the washing of all Muslims — those of sins and others alike. It is recommended that a man wash men and a woman wash women. A woman may wash her husband and a man may wash his wife, but this is done from behind a garment when necessary. If a woman dies among men or a man among women with no mahram present, none of them shall wash the deceased, and the deceased is buried without washing. The martyr is buried in the clothes in which he was wounded and is not washed; his weapons, skin, and fur are removed. This applies if he dies on the battlefield. If he is removed from the battlefield and dies elsewhere, he is washed and shrouded. The drowned, the burned, and those whose remains are found — if it is known that leaving them would mean they could not have survived — are washed and buried. Whatever falls from the deceased is washed and placed with him. If wounds or parts are removed from the body, each is washed and placed in its proper position, and a shroud is arranged accordingly. It is also recommended to hasten the burial of the deceased, except in the cases of the drowned and those who have fainted, where one waits until death is confirmed. If a man dies in a state of major ritual impurity, or a woman who is menstruating, or one whose parts require ghusl, they are washed the same as any other deceased person.

<!-- page 103 -->
**Mention of embalming and shrouding:**

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt, prayers of Allah upon them, that it is permissible, upon finishing the washing of the deceased, to dry him with a garment and then apply camphor to the extent of what is available, spreading it over him. The cloth used to cover his private parts during washing is removed and replaced with a clean covering. The camphor is applied to his throat, and cotton is placed on his private parts and in his seat. He is then dressed in a good shirt, whether that is his shroud or one of his garments. If neither is available, he is washed in his clothes. The shrouds are then spread out, his limbs are arranged, and he is placed in them.
