Embalming is applied to his places of prostration — his forehead, nose, the palms of his hands, the fronts of his knees, and his feet. His hands are folded over his chest, and a turban is placed on his head with its tail under his chin and brought around to his chest beneath the shrouds. The camphor is applied to his head, his ears, his eyes, his nose, his beard, and his chest. Embalming is placed therein, and whatever is good is used, though it is not made as a perfume. Men are not to be embalmed with red or saffron, though these may be used for women. The Commander of the Believers, Ali, prayers of Allah upon him, prepared the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, and commanded that camphor be placed in his embalming. Camphor is placed in the embalming and shrouds of the deceased, and at the place of washing, and incense is burned. No mahram is brought close to the deceased with perfume, just as it is not brought close to the living. It is recommended that an odd number of shrouds be used — one, three, five, or seven — with no fixed limit. One garment should be removed from the shroud in the same manner as is required for prayer. It is disliked for a man to be shrouded in pure silk. The shroud should be modest in cost, and the shroud is taken from the estate left by the deceased.

<!-- page 104 -->
**Mention of the funeral procession and the prayer over the deceased:**

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt, prayers of Allah upon them, that they ordered the deceased, after being shrouded, to be placed on a bier if one is available, and to be carried on the shoulders of men if possible. If not, he may be carried on an animal or whatever is available, and his shroud may be covered with a garment if one is found. A coffin is placed over the woman's bier in the form of a dome and covered with a garment, tied down if there is fear that the deceased may fall. The procession walks at a moderate pace — not so fast as to cause disturbance, and not as slowly as the Jews did. Whoever wishes to carry him begins by taking the front right side of the bier on his right shoulder, then circles around if he wishes, and others take turns carrying. It is disliked to decorate coffins. Walking in front of the funeral procession is disliked, and walking behind it is commanded. When the procession arrives at the graveyard, the bier is placed crosswise, with the deceased lying on his back and his right side facing the qibla and his left facing the worshipper. The prayer leader stands at the funeral and pronounces five takbirs, raising his hands with each takbir, and says the salutation to his right and left as he does in the prayer. In the first takbir, he praises Allah and glorifies Him as He is worthy. In the second, he prays upon the Prophet and his family. In the third, he supplicates for the deceased and strives in his supplication if he knows him, saying: "O Allah, this is Your servant and the son of Your servant. He has departed from this world and come to You. We know of him only good, and I have come as an intercessor for him." Then he prays as much as he is able. If he does not know the deceased, he says: "O Allah, You know his secret and his intention. If he was good, increase his good and have mercy on him, and if he was sinful, forgive him and pardon him." He strives in supplication and says: "O Allah, You have encompassed all things in mercy and knowledge, so forgive those who repent and follow Your way" — to the end of the verse — and this is the great victory. If he wishes, he may say: "O Allah, You know this servant of ours, so I entrust him to one I love and who has authority over him," and so on. Even if the deceased is a child, he says: "O Allah, make him an intercessor and a reward in advance for his parents."


<!-- page 105 -->
And reward and provision, benefiting them and granting them what they ask — they call upon such things as they are able, and they call at the fourth.
For the believers, and pray at the fifth for the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and his family.
He departs and recites all of this quietly except the takbir, which he announces aloud, and if he prays over the deceased at night, he recites aloud as he does in the night prayers. There is a second narration from Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him.
He said: praise Allah at the funeral and praise Him at the first takbir, pray for the Prophet and his family at the second, call upon the Imam at the third and upon the believers at the fourth, and pray for the deceased.
He knew this at the age of five. He prayed for the Prophet and his family and said yes, and so did the Prophet grow up —
May Allah bless him and his family — and he prayed for some of those who prayed for him. Four of those who did not deserve to be prayed over
were among the hypocrites, so they abstained from the fourth takbir and did not pray for them, leaving their remains without funeral prayer. Praying over the deceased
is permissible at any time of the day or night, except at the time of sunrise and sunset. It is preferable
to pray for the deceased during the day, and funerals should not be prayed over at night unless one is compelled to do so.
It was narrated from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) that he said: "Bury your dead during the day.
The angels of the day are more merciful than the angels of the night." If the sultan or a governor is present
for the funeral prayer of the congregation, he comes forward first to lead the prayer, then the guardian if the sultan is absent.
He is the sultan of the nearest guardians, the closest of them, and he prays for the martyr and the people of grave sins
and transgressions, and the one executed by hadd or retaliation, and the child of adultery, and the severed limb, if it is known
that without it the person cannot survive; if that is not known, the prayer is not offered over it.
The rest of the body is recorded, and if multiple funerals are combined, the imam prays over them together, arranged in rows if they are all men or all women, and stands in the middle of the funerals even if there is
only one. He stands at the chest of the man and at the head of the woman, and if someone has already prayed over one of them,

<!-- page 106 -->
it is still permissible. If the funerals of men and women are gathered together, the women are placed closest
to the qibla and the men beyond them; likewise, if a man and a woman are brought together, the woman is placed closest to the qibla and the man
beyond her. If the imam began the takbir at the funeral before the latecomer arrived, the latecomer joins and begins praying with him.
He should begin with it and say the takbir with the imam's takbir, and if the imam completes the prayer before him, the latecomer makes up what he missed, then
says the salutation and departs, as he would if he had prayed before. Whoever does not catch up with the imam before he says the salutation has missed the
funeral prayer and may pray for the deceased individually if he wishes, or he may forgo it.

— Mention of Burials and Graves —

We narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that they passed by graves being dug for the deceased
and forbade making them deeper than three cubits. Then one may perform the lahd within it or simply pray over the deceased if he wishes, and the one who prepares the lahd
places the deceased lying in a position facing the qibla and removes whatever remains behind from what was brought along,
such as stones. The one who prepares the lahd, when he does so, and the shroud stands over it — a hollow is made in the middle of
the grave, and it has supports in front and behind — that is, this is the action, and it is permissible to do so well.
They are permitted to line the grave if it is necessary to do so. When the deceased is brought to his grave and placed at one of
its sides, facing the qibla, his guardians or those who brought him down enter first — the man who is in front of him goes first
and the woman whose back is toward him goes first — and they descend into the grave and receive him
taking him with their own hands. If it is a woman, a garment is spread over the tomb to cover her as she is brought down,
until they are concealed from view. They lay her on her right side, moderately, neither on her face nor on her back, and untie
what has been prepared for her of the burial garments, and they pray for her.
They place a brick tablet or a folded cloth if they wish, and if they place dust over her then that is good, and they press the dust upon her

<!-- page 107 -->
from above, and those who are outside ascend, and the earth of the grave is returned over it. It remains over it, and water is sprinkled
upon it from above, and the people withdraw from it. If two or a group need to be buried in one grave,
they are buried in it, and the best of them is placed closest to the qibla, even if they are men and women — the men are placed in front of the women.
A small amount of dust is placed between the two to separate them. They regarded it as disliked to move the deceased from place to place, and they were strict
in this regard. They instructed that the grave should be leveled and not be slept upon, and they permitted that the grave be marked with a sign so that it may be recognized,
and to plaster, whitewash, and roof it with a covering. They permitted visiting the grave from time to time,
at any time, to have mercy upon the deceased and seek forgiveness for him, and they instructed whoever enters the cemetery to greet
the people of the graves and pray for them all. They forbade walking over the graves and laughing near them, and
prayer between them, and urinating and defecating among them. If a polytheist woman dies and in her womb is a child from
a Muslim, and the child was not yet separated from her, her people attend to her and bury her in their graves. If a woman dies and
in her belly is a living boy that is moving, it is permissible for the midwife to insert her hand and deliver the boy without cutting him open,
if she is able to do so; if she cannot, she splits the belly and takes the boy out. If she cannot do that,
those closest to her among the men may do so, but only if they are certain of the child's viability.
The child must be at least six months old, based on what is known about survival if delivered — otherwise they wait until
death occurs in the womb, and she is not buried while the child is still moving inside her. If the boy dies in the woman's womb and
she is endangered by him, the midwife inserts her hand and cuts it and removes it. If the child is cut
but then emerges and survives, the belly of the deceased is split before the child is killed in her womb. They considered it proper that the family of the
deceased be sent food by their neighbors and their own relatives for as long as they are in their grief,
since grief preoccupies them and distracts them from preparing food for themselves. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) commanded

<!-- page 108 -->
his family to prepare food for the family of Ja'far ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon him) when the news of his death came.
Al-Nu'man said: "He provided them with what would distract them from thinking about it themselves."

— Transition to the Chapters of Zakat: The Command to Give Zakat and What Has Been Encouraged in That Regard —

The command to give zakat and what is encouraged in that regard is mentioned,
along with the severity of withholding it. Allah the Almighty said: "Successful indeed is he who purifies himself, and remembers the name of his Lord, and prays" [al-A'la], and He said: "Blessed be the believers
who are humble in their prayers, and who turn away from ill speech,
and who are active in giving zakat" [al-Mu'minun], and He said: "Establish the prayer and pay the zakat."
The Almighty said: "When the sacred months have passed, kill the polytheists wherever you
find them, and take them, and confine them, and wait for them at every lookout. But if they repent, establish
prayer, and pay zakat, then let them go their way" [al-Tawbah: 5]. Allah the Almighty does not accept the Islam of
a polytheist who withholds zakat. He said: "Woe to the polytheists who do not pay
zakat" [al-Fussilat: 6–7]. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) was asked about
this verse — Allah the Almighty said: "Woe to the polytheists who do not pay zakat" —
have you not heard him say: "Woe to the worshippers who are heedless of their prayers, who are heedless of what they see
and who withhold the ma'un" — for the ma'un is zakat. Then he said: By the One in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, Allah the Almighty does not accept
any zakat from his wealth except from a believer, and the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said: "If Allah wills good for a servant,
Allah sends an angel from the treasures of Paradise who wipes his chest and softens his heart for zakat." The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said,
upon him and his family: "Whoever gives zakat on his wealth, let him give it willingly, for he gives a portion of what

<!-- page 109 -->
Allah has given him while He saves the rest for him. Whoever gives zakat on his wealth, his evil is removed
from him." Upon him and his family: "No wealth is destroyed on land or sea except through the withholding of zakat from it, so purify your wealth
with zakat, treat your sick with charity, and ward off affliction with supplication." The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said:
"No one withholds zakat on his wealth but that his wealth is diminished, nor does he hoard it but that it increases on him, and wealth does not decrease
through spending. Do not withhold charity." The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said: "Allah the Almighty has a refuge of
angels who are sent against those who withhold what is rightfully due from their wealth, so that they lack it in what they need." And he said, upon him
and his family: "Whoever has a camel or a cow or a sheep and does not pay its zakat will be laid flat on the Day of Resurrection in a vast plain,
and it will trample him with its hooves and gore him with its horns and bite him with its mouths; whenever the first of them passes
it returns to him, until the reckoning of all creation is finished." And from the Commander of the Believers, may the prayers of
Allah be upon him, he said to the servant: "Three signs — prayer, zakat, and fasting." And he said,
upon him and his family, in his bequest to al-Husayn (peace be upon him): "I recommend to you al-Hasan, and all my sons, my family,
and whoever among the believers to whom my Book reaches, that you fear Allah your Lord. And Allah, and Allah — regarding zakat: it extinguishes the anger of
Allah from you." And he, peace be upon him, said: "Allah the Almighty has made obligatory upon the wealthy, from their wealth, a portion
that is sufficient for the poor among them. If the poor are hungry or naked while the wealthy withhold their wealth, then Allah the Almighty
will hold them accountable for that on the Day of Resurrection and punish them severely."
"Whoever does not pay what is due from his wealth — either a serpent will consume him on the Day of Resurrection." And he said, peace be upon him: "What is forbidden
is zakat: the one who consumes riba, and any Muslim who withholds zakat." And he said, regarding the words of Allah the Almighty
— the Almighty said: "They will be encircled by what they were stingy with on the Day of Resurrection" [Al 'Imran: 180] —
"What prevented zakat in a remarkable parable: it is bald and coils around his neck and devours him until the people are freed from
the reckoning. He says: 'I am the owner whose zakat was withheld.'" And he said, peace be upon him, regarding the words of Allah the Almighty:

<!-- page 110 -->
"And those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend it in the way of Allah —
give them tidings of a painful punishment, on the day when it will be heated in the fire of Hell, and with it will be branded
their foreheads, their sides, and their backs — this is what you hoarded for yourselves, so taste what you used to
hoard" [al-Tawbah: 34–35] — treasure is wealth on which zakat has not been paid; if zakat has been paid on it,
it is not treasure, as the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said. On the Day of Resurrection, whoever withheld zakat on his wealth will have
plates brought, then heated in the fire of Hell, and then his forehead, sides, and back will be branded with them; whenever
they cool, they are heated again and the branding resumed, until the reckoning of all creation is finished." And he said: "Whoever
pays zakat on his wealth, and fulfills what is owed from it" — and a man asked Abu 'Abd Allah Ja'far ibn Muhammad ibn
Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, about a man who complained to him that he was terrified in his dreams as though a woman was demanding something of him.
He said: "This is a man who does not pay zakat on his wealth."
The man acknowledged this before Abu 'Abd Allah, may Allah's prayers be upon him, and Abu 'Abd Allah said to him: "If that is the case, then he is not
placing it in its proper place." The man said: "But this is indeed so."

— Mentioning the Payment of Zakat to Its Rightful Recipients and the Prohibition of Misplacing It —

Allah the Almighty said to Muhammad, His Prophet, peace be upon him and his family: "Take from their wealth
a charity that will purify them and cleanse them by it" [al-Tawbah: 103], then He praised the places where
it is to be given and said: "Charity is for the poor and the destitute, and those who administer it,
and those whose hearts are to be reconciled, and for freeing captives, and for those in debt, and in the cause of Allah, and for the wayfarer —
an obligation from Allah, and Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise" [al-Tawbah: 60]. Ja'far ibn Muhammad said, may Allah's prayers


<!-- page 111 -->
The poor, according to Ja'far ibn Muhammad, is the needy person who does not beg from people, while the destitute is one in even greater need — the one who does beg. Those who work on zakat are the collectors who take it from the people with their own hands. Those whose hearts are to be won over are those who harbor enmity or occupy positions of influence among their people, and who are given from zakat to attract and incline them toward obedience, as the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) used to give to tribal chiefs such as 'Uyayna ibn Husn, al-Aqra' ibn Habis, and others. This was the practice of the imams throughout the past: distributing zakat in its prescribed categories. These include freeing those in bondage, relieving those burdened by debt, supporting jihad in the way of Allah, and aiding the stranded traveler — eight categories in all upon whom Allah Almighty has made charity obligatory. No class is to receive it exclusively without the others, since there is no distinction among Muslims in this regard. If a man were named as recipient by name and one of those named rose against the others, claiming it all for himself without passing it to his companions, he would be told that this is not permissible for him. How, then, can one who has heard the words of Allah Almighty — "Charity is only for the poor and the needy, and those who work on it" [al-Tawbah: 60] — and who has heard all eight categories, consider it permissible to give it only to those whom Allah has commanded otherwise? Allah Almighty has obligated that it be placed in its proper positions and has entrusted those responsible to know the situation of each category, how much is to be given at all times, and according to what is appropriate for Muslims and for Islam. This is something that none can fulfill except the Imams whom Allah Almighty has appointed over His servants, establishing them over His land and His dominion. The people do not dispute that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) was the one who received charity, that he sent his agents to collect it from those who could not bring it to him directly, and that he divided it accordingly and was well pleased with its distribution — as is made clear and evident by the words of Allah Almighty, who mentioned those who work on it in His Book.

<!-- page 112 -->
Allah made it a share of theirs in recognition of their labor in collecting it, as He said to His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him and his family): "Take from their wealth charity" [al-Tawbah: 103]. The question then arose among the people as to whether this obligation would continue after the passing of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family). They were unanimous that whoever stood in his place would receive it as he used to do, and would spend it on the aforementioned categories. They agreed with Abu Bakr in fighting those who withheld zakat, calling them apostates and taking action against them. Abu Bakr, 'Umar, 'Uthman, and the Commander of the Believers (prayers of Allah be upon him) each collected it in their respective times, and none disputed this with them. When authority passed to the Umayyad rulers, however, and the people saw that they were diverting it for themselves, some stopped paying zakat to them. Those who remained among the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) were asked about this, and they commanded that it be paid to the rulers. They said: upon you is to give it, and upon them is to place it properly. They narrated this from 'A'isha, Abu Hurayrah, Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas, Abu Sa'id al-Khudri, and 'Abdullah ibn 'Umar, and none of the Companions narrated otherwise, nor did they relate any disagreement on the matter. When Ibn 'Umar was pressed further on the question, he said: "What would you say if I sealed the hands of thieves? You would cut them off and leave some as if they were in the right?" They said no. He said: "If you bring them before the Sultan, and he does that, is it upon you?" They said no. He said: "Then let things proceed; pay them their due, even if they devour it as serpents devour flesh." This is the prevailing opinion and what has been transmitted from the predecessors. The full exposition differs from this endpoint, and those who claimed that withholding zakat from the rulers amounted to apostasy did not thereby prevent their own princes from paying zakat on their wealth — rather, they were the first to pay it and to uphold its proper distribution. This topic requires lengthy explanation.

<!-- page 113 -->
As for the Ahl al-Bayt (prayers of Allah be upon them), zakat is not to be paid except to the Guiding Imams from the family of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) — those whom Allah Almighty has commanded His servants to obey in His Book and whom He has entrusted with the governance of His servants. Whoever pays the zakat of his wealth in obedience to others has not fulfilled his obligation, and he must pay it to the Imam of his time from among them. Even if the Imams of injustice overpower him and take it from him by force, that is permissible for him under compulsion. The Commander of the Believers (prayers of Allah be upon him) said to some of his agents in a certain region: whoever pays the zakat of his wealth to Mu'awiya and his agents who forcibly protected him — our horses and men will offer him no reward for that, since it did not reach him and he was not thereby protected. He noted that where they overcame him and took it from him, or less than his due, he said: let it be shown that he acted under duress. He said (peace be upon him) that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) forbade a person from concealing his zakat from his imam, calling that an act of hypocrisy. Allah Almighty has made clear to His servants, through the tongue of His Messenger Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him and his family), that those who pay their charity to the Imams are entrusted to place it properly — for He forbade it to the Imams themselves, and to no one else. The nation is agreed that it was not permissible for the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family), nor for his family, to eat from charity. Indeed, al-Hasan ibn 'Ali (prayers of Allah be upon him) once, as a small child, placed a date from the dates of charity in his mouth, whereupon the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) saw him, quickly reached him, and drew it out of his mouth with its saliva, throwing it back among the charity dates, saying: "The people of the house have no share in charity." This signified that they are trustees over it, placing it in its proper positions — it is not for anyone else, nor for them personally. And as a mark of their trustworthiness and their status equivalent to that of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family), Allah also assigned to them the fifth, just as He assigned it to the Messenger. This applies to the Imams (prayers of Allah be upon them) from among them, and not to the rest of the family, for the rest of them are obligated, like the rest of the nation, to pay their charity to

<!-- page 114 -->
the Imam, just as the rest of the people do — as was the case with the household of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) during his lifetime. This is a dignity that moves from one to the next in succession, and a virtue that is continuous. Whoever has denied their right and is ignorant of their trustworthiness — the least such a person needs is the words of the Imams and his own predecessors, through whom Allah has revealed their affairs and their authority. Whoever denies that zakat on his wealth must be paid to them, despite all the arguments we have presented, has cut himself off from the guidance available to one who is blessed with understanding. And Allah guides whom He wills to the right path.

- Mentioning zakat on gold, silver, and mineral wealth:

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (prayers of Allah be upon them) that zakat is not due on gold until it reaches twenty mithqals, with a full year having passed on it in the possession of one who has benefited from it, nor on silver until it reaches two hundred dirhams, with a full year similarly having passed. The due zakat on twenty mithqals is half a mithqal, and on two hundred dirhams it is five dirhams. Once the nisab has been reached, if it exceeds that amount, zakat is due at one quarter of a tenth. It should be paid at the completion of the year; if at the end of the year the amount falls short of the nisab, no zakat is due on it. Whatever exceeds the nisab is subject to zakat in proportion, and the two — gold and silver — are not combined with one another. Zakat is not obligatory on gold or silver until each separately reaches the amount we have mentioned. If a man has one hundred dirhams and ten dinars equivalent in value to one hundred dirhams or more, no zakat is due on him in this case, since the nisab for dinars is twenty dinars and for dirhams it is two hundred dirhams, and a full year must have passed on each. However, if he spends some of it before the year's end in order to escape zakat, having had two hundred dirhams and then spent some as the zakat became due —

<!-- page 115 -->
converting some into dinars to avoid zakat on them, or spending some of the dinars similarly — that is not permissible. As for mineral wealth — pearls and amber — when extracted from their source and place, the fifth is due on the one who extracts it, and no zakat applies to what he retains. If he sells it and the buyer takes possession, it becomes like the rest of his wealth, and zakat applies at the completion of the year if it is held for trade. There is no zakat on jewelry or mineral goods if they are taken for personal use, unless one takes them as a stratagem to escape zakat — in which case that intent renders the stratagem invalid. Metals such as gold, silver, lead, iron, and stone minerals are subject to the fifth when extracted, and if sold or exchanged they are treated like the rest of one's wealth. Whoever possesses alloyed coinage must pay zakat on it in proportion to the pure metal it contains, even if the remainder is copper, iron, lead, or other base metals with a surface coating — the value reverts to what it truly is in origin and is combined with other holdings accordingly. There is no zakat on clothing, animals, vessels, and furnishings if they are not held for trade and are not acquired as a means of fleeing from zakat. If they are held for trade, or if they were purchased with funds on which zakat was due and retained specifically to avoid paying it, then zakat applies to them. Whoever purchases trade goods at the start of the year and pays zakat on them — whether the goods are in his presence or in a place from which he is not prevented from accessing them — must similarly pay zakat on debts owed to him, provided he is not prevented from collecting them. When the year is complete on such a debt, it is treated as if it were in his hands, even if he cannot obtain it except through legal action. If the debtor refuses to pay, the creditor does not owe zakat on it until he receives it. There is no zakat on a child's wealth, even if the child has reached the age of discernment and a full year has passed on it — unless it is in the hands of an adult acting on his behalf, in which case zakat is due, charged to whoever manages it, along with its profit for the child, in the manner of a dowry obligation for a woman or a debt obligation. We have already addressed this matter. Whoever pays zakat on his wealth and loses it before delivering it to its rightful place must pay it again, for he remains a guarantor of it until it is properly placed. Whoever dies with zakat still owed, not yet fulfilled, it is to be taken from his estate, whether or not he made a bequest to that effect, if it is known and established.

<!-- page 116 -->
- Mentioning the zakat on camels, cattle, and sheep:

We have narrated from the Imams of the family of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) that there is no zakat on working camels — those used for carrying loads or for irrigation — regardless of their number. If, however, they are grazing camels kept for their increase in value and their milk production, then no zakat is due on fewer than five. When they reach five and a full year has passed, if they do not exceed five, there is nothing due. When they reach ten, two sheep are due; at fifteen, three sheep; at twenty, four sheep; at twenty-five, a bint makhad — a she-camel that has completed one year, entered the second, and whose mother has since become pregnant again — is due. If no bint makhad is available, then an ibn labun — a male camel that has completed two years, entered the third, and whose mother has similarly since become pregnant and given birth — is acceptable, and this applies up to thirty-five. At thirty-six, a bint labun is due; at forty-six, a hiqqah — a she-camel of three years, entering the fourth, suitable for pregnancy and mounting — is due, and this applies up to sixty. At sixty-one, a jadha'a — a she-camel of four years, entering the fifth — is due, and this applies up to seventy-five. At seventy-six, two daughters of labun are due; at ninety-one, two hiqqas are due; and at one hundred and twenty-one, the scale resets: for every forty camels a bint labun is due, and for every fifty a hiqqah. If a believer cannot find a camel of the required age, he may give one below that age along with two sheep or twenty dirhams, or one above that age and receive back two sheep or twenty dirhams in return. As for cattle, working animals used for plowing and irrigation are exempt, and no zakat is due on them. Grazing cattle — those kept for increase in value and milk production — are not subject to zakat until they reach thirty, at which point, with a full year having passed on them in one's possession, the due amount is a tabi' or tabi'a — a calf of one year whose horns have begun to level —


<!-- page 117 -->
There is nothing more between thirty and forty; at forty there is one old cow. From forty to sixty, there is one old cow; from sixty to seventy, one old cow plus one calf; from seventy to eighty, one old cow plus one calf; from eighty to ninety, two old cows; from ninety to one hundred, three calves; at one hundred, two old cows and one calf — and so on, with one calf due for every thirty and one old cow for every forty, and nothing in between.

There is no zakat on poultry of sheep. Poultry refers to those raised in houses that do not graze; as for the sa'imah — those that are pastured — zakat is due on them once they reach forty. When she reaches forty, one sheep is taken from her owner. There is nothing additional until she reaches one hundred and twenty. If she exceeds that by one or more, then two sheep are due, until she reaches two hundred. If she exceeds two hundred by one or more, then three sheep are due; and when the flock is large, one sheep is due for every hundred.

- Mentioning what the workers of charity do in their duties and what they take from people:

Regarding the funds of the mixed property: It is narrated from the Commander of the Believers, 'Ali ibn Abi Talib, may Allah's blessings be upon him, that he appointed Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym al-Azwi over the zakat of Bakr ibn Wa'il, and wrote a covenant for him.

This is what Ibn Abi Talib entrusted to Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym when he sent him to collect charity. He commanded him to fear Allah in all his affairs and in the hidden aspects of his deeds when there is no witness over him except Allah — the Guardian — and commanded him not to act in disobedience to Allah, letting what appears outwardly differ from what he conceals inwardly. For if he does that, he is in a state of treachery, is negligent before Allah, and exposes himself to Allah's wrath. He commanded him to travel among those under his charge with leniency and to deal with them fairly under his oath, to meet them with a broad face and gentle manner, to do justice to the oppressed among them against the unjust, and to establish justice among them — in his council, his countenance, his region, and his permission — so that none of them would see any favoritism from him toward another. He commanded him not to insult them, not to reproach them, and not to covet what they possess.

<!-- page 118 -->
Nor should he prefer himself over them in authority, for they are his brothers in religion and his supporters against enemies, and his helpers in extracting rights. He said — peace be upon him — that Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym had in this charity a share and an imposed right, along with partners: the poor, the people of his household who are weak. And if they do not receive their right, they will understand that right, otherwise he would be among the most hostile and wretched of people on the Day of Judgment — and his opponents would be the poor, the destitute, the exhausted, and the son of the wayfarer. And may Allah's blessings be upon him.

The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, forbade people from being made to swear oaths over their charity. He said that they are trusted regarding what is captured from the animals and what is in their bellies; but what appears openly is taken by the assessor in charity — meaning it is forbidden to press people with oaths in the collection of charity. And the Commander of the Believers, 'Ali — peace be upon him — said: Peace be upon the people of the valleys; the charity of the people of the valleys is collected in their own places, and they are not driven from place to place. They delay the practice of al-jadab until the land becomes fertile.

It is narrated from him that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said: Do not combine separate holdings or separate combined holdings for the purpose of charity — meaning that if a man has one hundred and twenty sheep and possesses only a single animal that would bring him to the threshold, the assessor is forbidden to divide it into groups of forty and take three from it. Likewise, if the flock belongs to forty sheep, the owner should not be told to divide it into two groups of twenty in order to avoid paying zakat on it. As for the combination of two or three men's flocks — if each of them has forty sheep and they are not joint owners — then the assessor is only obligated to take one sheep total; whereas if the animals were held separately, three sheep would be obligatory. Similarly, the assessor should not combine the flocks of two men when zakat is not individually obligatory on each of them, so that by combining them zakat becomes obligatory. Nor should partners in livestock be assessed zakat unless each of them individually holds what makes zakat due. If that is the case, then it is assessed equitably among them; and if their shares are unequal, each one recoups in proportion from the other, as between two partners who together have

<!-- page 119 -->
sheep on which nothing is due until they reach eighty; when they reach eighty, two sheep are due. Each receives one sheep if the split is equal halves; but if one holds one-third and the other two-thirds, then only the one who holds two-thirds has the nisab until they reach one hundred and twenty. If the combined flock exceeds that by one, then two sheep are due, to be apportioned between the partners in proportion to their shares — because the assessor took from the combined flock two sheep: one for the forty and one for the eighty. He therefore takes from the owner of two-thirds one sheep and a third — though only one sheep is actually due from him — and from the owner of forty, a third of a sheep, though a full sheep is due from him. So each one makes a proportional adjustment with the other by way of the share taken.

This same principle applies throughout: in all forms of wealth — camels, cows, sheep, grains, and everything on which zakat is obligatory — if the property is held in partnership, each partner owes on his share what he would owe if he were not a partner, because the partnership does not transfer the property from one to another; rather, each partner owns what he owns, and whatever was or was not due before the partnership remains so after it. If people of wealth combine and mix their wealth without forming a legal partnership, then from each of them is taken what he owes individually, and no account is taken of the mixing of their wealth.

Whoever holds a nisab of wealth on which zakat has become obligatory, and then adds to it at the start of the second year, the additional amount is added and zakat is levied on the whole together — so that it is not treated as a separate tenth of ten. What comes from livestock is counted in the total number even if a mother gives birth and the assessor is present; he does not count it, nor does he take it, nor take its equivalent in charity. He does not take from camels and cows except the specific teeth described, nor from sheep except the adult animals. He does not take a defective animal, nor a one-eyed one, nor a male sheep, nor the best of the flock, nor the most valued one; rather, he takes from the middle. If there is a dispute

<!-- page 120 -->
between the assessor and the owners of the wealth, the owners choose one-third of the flock, set it apart, and the assessor then chooses from the remaining two-thirds what is due, and takes it.

- Mentioning zakat on grains and fruits:

Allah Almighty said: "And it is He who causes gardens to grow, both trellised and untrellised, and date palms and crops of different flavors, and olives and pomegranates, similar and dissimilar. Eat of its fruit when it yields and give its due on the day of its harvest." [Al-An'am: 141]

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said regarding the words of Allah Almighty — that they shall come to him on the day of its harvest — that this means: after the obligation of charity is fulfilled, it is given at the time of harvest. The one who asks stands with a handful — meaning a fistful — and the thing is given in charity alongside the obligatory zakat imposed upon it.

It is narrated from his forefathers that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, explained that the tithe is due on all crops watered by rain or rivers, and half a tithe on those watered by wells. It has come from the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that the tithe is obligatory on all grains once they are mature and husked — that is, when they appear and are separated from their owners. Then a tenth is taken from what has been harvested, and the one who undertakes the harvest is instructed not to investigate the husking closely but to let it proceed. They said: the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, sent 'Abdullah ibn Rawaha al-Ansari to the people of good faith, and he estimated the value of the fruits when they began to ripen, then made his assessment. They disputed it, and 'Abdullah ibn Rawaha al-Ansari said to them: "I have made my assessment and have not wronged you. If you wish, take what I have assessed; if you wish, hand it over to us and take half of it." So the Jews said: "This is the truth by which the heavens and the earth stand." And may Allah's prayers be upon him.

<!-- page 121 -->
If the fruits are held for trade, then zakat is due on them. But what is purchased from them and consumed, and certain portions set aside for personal use, carry no zakat.

- Mentioning Zakat al-Fitr:

Allah Almighty said: "Successful indeed is the one who purifies himself, and mentions the name of his Lord, and prays." [Al-A'la: 14–15]

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers and blessings be upon him, said: "He has succeeded who purifies himself" — meaning he who gives Zakat al-Fitr — "and mentions the name of his Lord" — meaning the Eid prayer. He said that Zakat al-Fitr is obligatory upon the rich and the poor alike. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said that it is obligatory to give Zakat al-Fitr on behalf of every person — himself and all his dependants, young and old, free and enslaved — one sa' of food per person. And Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, said: A man pays Zakat al-Fitr on behalf of his Jewish and Christian slave. It is narrated from him, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that if a man's wife has slaves among her children, he pays on their behalf; and if they are not among her children, then the wife must pay on their behalf. And it is narrated that he, peace be upon him, used to pay Zakat al-Fitr on behalf of his father after his father's death — and this is a voluntary act of goodness, not an obligation. He said — peace be upon him — that one who cannot find food may give a dirham in its equivalent value at the time, and that it is preferable to pay Zakat al-Fitr before the Eid prayer.

This concludes the first part of the book of abbreviation. It is to be read together with the second part, which deals with the chapters on the validity of fasting, the effects narrated from the pure Imams — the righteous masters — compiled by Qadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad, may Allah be pleased with him — covering all the chapters of fasting, the chapters of Hajj, all the chapters of jihad, and concluding with the mention of those whom it is obligatory to fight from among the people of the Qibla, which forms the final section. In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful.

<!-- page 122 -->
Mentioning fasting and what is desired in it:

Allah Almighty said: "O you who believe, fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, so that you may become God-fearing — for a appointed number of days..." to His saying: "...so that you may complete the number and glorify Allah for having guided you, that perhaps you will be grateful." [Al-Baqarah: 183–185]

Allah Almighty has thus made fasting in the month of Ramadan obligatory every year for every person who is of legal responsibility. I have mentioned in the chapters on prayer what boys are commanded to do regarding prayer and fasting, and in what circumstances they are commanded to do so.

It is narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that when the month of Ramadan drew near, he would pray and say in his supplication: "O Allah, the blessed month of Ramadan has come — if we reach it, help us through it and accept it from us." And when it entered, he would say in his supplication: "O Allah, this is the month of Ramadan in which the Quran was revealed as guidance for the people, and signs of guidance and the Criterion. O Allah, bless us in the month of Ramadan, help us through it, and accept it from us, for You are capable of all things."

It is narrated from his father, peace be upon him, that he said: "Do not say 'Ramadan' alone; rather say as Allah Almighty said: 'the month of Ramadan.'" And from his forefathers, that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, said: "Sha'ban is my month and Ramadan is the month of Allah" — and may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family. All months belong to Allah Almighty, but the month of Ramadan is attributed to Him in honor. He also said: "My month" regarding Sha'ban is also from the perspective of distinction. And the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, used to fast and ascend — may Allah's prayers be upon him — upon the pulpit and say: "O people, Jibril came to me and said: 'O Muhammad, whoever reaches the month of Ramadan and is not forgiven, then dies — may Allah keep him far away.' Say Amin." So I said Amin. And he, may Allah's prayers and blessings be upon him and his family, preached on the last day of Sha'ban and said: "O people, a month has come upon you—"


<!-- page 123 -->
A great and blessed month — a month in which one night is better than a thousand months. Drawing near to God through any act of goodness in it is like performing an obligatory duty elsewhere, and performing an obligatory duty in it is like performing seventy obligatory duties elsewhere. It is the month of patience, and the reward of patience is Paradise. It is the month of consolation, and in it the sustenance of the believer is increased. Whoever provides food for a fasting person to break his fast with earns forgiveness of his sins and freedom of his neck from the Fire, and he receives the like of the fasting person's reward without any diminishment of that reward. Some of the companions said, "O Messenger of Allah, not all of us find what to give the fasting person to break his fast with." He said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, that Allah Almighty gives this reward even for breaking the fast on a taste of milk, a date, or a drink of water. Whoever satisfies a fasting person, Allah will give him water from the basin — a drink after which he will not thirst until he enters Paradise. The first third of the month is mercy, the middle is forgiveness, and the end is freedom from the Fire. Whoever is relieved of his property in it, Allah frees him from the Fire. Multiply in it four qualities: two with which your Lord will be pleased, and two from which you cannot be free. As for the two with which your Lord is pleased, they are the testimony that there is no god but Allah alone, with no partner, and asking Him for forgiveness. As for the two from which you cannot be free, ask Allah for Paradise and seek refuge with Him from the Fire. He said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, that for everything there is zakat, and the zakat of the bodies is fasting. The Prophet, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, also said: seek help through patience and prayer. He said patience is fasting, and Allah rewards the patient without reckoning. He said, may Allah bless him and his family, to Ali, peace be upon him: "O Ali, be steadfast in hardship, perform ablution for the Qur'an, and teach yourself patience." He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him and his family peace, that the most restful in the world are the least fortunate in the Hereafter, and the hungriest on the Day of Resurrection are those who were hungriest in the world. He said, may Allah's peace be upon him and his family, that Allah Almighty and all His angels send prayers of supplication upon those who are fasting. He said: "I have not commanded my angels to pray for anyone without their being answered."

<!-- page 124 -->
May Allah bless him and grant him peace. The fasting person sleeps in a state of worship and is praised, and he said, peace be upon him, that Allah Almighty says: "Fasting is for Me, and I will reward him for it." For the fasting person there are two joys: joy when he breaks his fast, and joy when he meets his Lord. And by Him in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, the breath of the mouth of the fasting believer is more pleasing to Allah than the fragrance of musk. He said, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, to whoever was invited to someone's food: "Break your fast with them, for whoever fasts eats your righteous food, and the angels have come upon you." This indicates the virtue of breaking the fast.

**Mentioning the beginning of the fast and entering it:**

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, said: "The pre-dawn meal is a blessing, so do not abandon it, even if one of you drinks only water, for Allah has angels who send prayers upon those who take the pre-dawn meal." The Commander of the Believers, Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: When Allah Almighty revealed, "Eat and drink until you see the white thread distinct from the black thread," some people took black and white threads and continued to eat and drink through the night until the white thread became distinct from the black thread. Then Allah Almighty clarified that it meant the whiteness of dawn. The fasting person's dawn — after which food and intercourse are forbidden — is the dawn whose light spreads across the horizons of the east. The first dawn does not forbid anything; it is the one that appears vertically, high up. He also said that the Sunnah is to hasten the breaking of the fast after sunset and to delay the pre-dawn meal. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, also said that an obligatory fast is not valid except with the conviction of an intention to fast. The Commander of the Believers, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family, said: Allah Almighty has made the crescent moon a marker of fasting times, of seasons for Hajj, and of the fixing of religious observances — a blessing of Allah Almighty upon His creation. Whoever fasts based on something other than the sighting of the crescent moon, and later comes to know that that day

<!-- page 125 -->
was indeed from the month of Ramadan, must make it up, because he fasted voluntarily. It is permissible to fast on a day about which one is doubtful as to whether it is from Ramadan, doing so voluntarily. If he later learns that it was indeed from the month of Ramadan, it suffices, because he fasted voluntarily. One should not fast on a day of doubt out of obligation, for Allah Almighty is not to be worshipped in any act of worship upon a basis of doubt.

**Mentioning what obliges breaking the fast and what one should break the fast with:**

Allah Almighty said: "It has been made lawful for you on the night of fasting to return to your wives… then complete the fast until the night" [al-Baqarah]. The fasting person is therefore forbidden to eat, drink, or engage in intercourse during the day of the fast. This is a matter of consensus among Muslims. It has been narrated from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, that he made expiation obligatory upon whoever breaks the fast or engages in intercourse during the day of the month of Ramadan deliberately. The expiation for deliberately breaking the fast or engaging in intercourse during the day of Ramadan is freeing a slave; if that is not possible, then fasting for two consecutive months; if that is not possible, then feeding sixty poor people, along with making up the day on which the fast was broken. If a person can neither give charity nor fast, then there is nothing required of him, for Allah does not burden a soul beyond what it can bear. It has been narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, regarding a man who caresses his wife during the day of the month of Ramadan, or kisses her, or initiates physical contact with her, that if he wishes he must make up the day and expiate; if he does not wish, there is nothing on him, though he should guard himself against such things, purify his fast, and protect it from what corrupts it, not exposing it to what would violate it. Whoever bends over his wife during the day of the month of Ramadan while she is asleep, or while she is averse, or while she is insane, or while she is a child, he must make up the day and expiate, since the act was undertaken at great cost. There is nothing upon the woman in the situations we have mentioned. They said, may peace be upon them, that whoever is in a state of major ritual impurity on the night of the month of Ramadan should not deliberately

<!-- page 126 -->
sleep until he has performed the ritual bath. If he does sleep and then engages in intercourse, he must make up that day. If he had a dream and woke, then fell back asleep, and his eyes overcame him or he was overwhelmed and did not attend to it until dawn, he should perform the ritual bath, complete his fast, and nothing is required of him. Whoever ate, drank, or engaged in intercourse during the day of the month of Ramadan forgetfully should complete his fast and need not make it up nor expiate, but should give thanks in it when he remembers it. However, if he then swallows food or drink deliberately after having remembered, or exceeds the limit in the case of intercourse, he must make up the day and expiate. They said regarding one who vomits deliberately during the day of the month of Ramadan that he must spend a day in its place on account of his negligence in fasting. If something from that vomit returns to his throat, he must make up the day and expiate. If his body vomited involuntarily and he could not prevent it from overtaking him, there is nothing on him. Whoever stayed awake during the last part of the night and ate or drank while inside his house without going out or looking to see whether dawn had come, and it turned out that dawn had already arrived when he was eating or drinking, he must fast that day and make it up. However, if he went out, looked at the dawn, and did not see it, then returned and ate or drank, and dawn came while he did not do so deliberately or negligently and his gaze was sound, there is nothing on him. If there is doubt about the dawn and he is among the people of knowledge of it, he may eat and drink until he is certain of its emergence. If he is doubtful and has no knowledge of the dawn, he should stop until the matter is certain or until those who know it and who observe it confirm it. If he looks at the sun and believes it has set, then eats or drinks during the month of Ramadan, and it then appears that the sun had not yet set, he should leave off eating and drinking until it sets, and nothing is required of him. If the fasting person uses kohl and it reaches his throat, he must make up that day. If he does not know that it reaches him and then becomes aware that its taste is reaching his throat and he does so deliberately, he must make up the day and expiate. If he is covered with kohl and its taste has not reached his throat, there is nothing on him. They have permitted the use of the siwak for the fasting person unless it is a wet stick; if he uses that and its taste reaches his

<!-- page 127 -->
throat deliberately, or he exceeds that, he must make up the day and expiate. If he does not do so deliberately, he must make up the day only. If he uses a dry stick, there is nothing on him. They have also permitted the fasting person to chew food, to taste it with his tongue, so long as nothing of it reaches his throat, just as it is permissible for him to rinse his mouth with water. As for cupping, if he fears it may lead to vomiting he must make up a day, even if he knows that cupping may expose him to that — he should not use it unless he has no choice, in which case it is obligatory. They have also permitted the fasting person to use pleasant fragrances, oils, and good smells, as long as none of that reaches his throat. Likewise, he should not pour anything into his ear that could reach his throat.

**Mentioning fasting during travel:**

Allah Almighty said: "Fasting is prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may fear God — for a fixed number of days… and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days" [al-Baqarah]. Allah Almighty thus prescribed the fast of the month of Ramadan upon the resident who is legally responsible, and made it obligatory upon the traveler for a number of other days afterward. It was thus obligatory upon the traveler to fast while at home; if he then traveled during the month of Ramadan, Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, narrated that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, set out during the battle of Mecca in the month of Ramadan and broke his fast, ordering those with him to break their fast. He was then informed that some of his companions continued to fast, and he said: "Do you not have a good example in the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him?" He stood on his feet on the road where they could see him, took a cup of water and drank it while they watched. When they saw this, they broke their fast. Some people persisted in fasting, and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, called them the disobedient. Breaking the fast during the day of the month of Ramadan while traveling is therefore permissible according to the Book of Allah Almighty

<!-- page 128 -->
and the Sunnah of His Messenger, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family. Whoever refrains from eating and drinking while traveling is not considered to be fasting, and he must make up the number of days he spent traveling, as enjoined by Allah Almighty. Whoever stopped eating and drinking while traveling, without intending it as fasting and without considering it as such, and then spent those days at home, there is nothing required of him. It has been narrated from many of the Imams, may Allah's prayers be upon them and his family, that they would do this. I have mentioned in the chapters on prayer the book in which breaking the fast and shortening the prayer are obligatory: wherever it is necessary to shorten the prayers, it is obligatory to break the fast. It is disliked to travel deliberately in the month of Ramadan for no purpose, and if a traveler leaves his home during the month of Ramadan before the end of the day he must make up that day. If he leaves after the passing of the day, he should not break his fast on that day and need not make it up. If he arrives home before the passing of the day and has not tasted anything, he should complete his fast for that day and need not make it up. If he arrives after the passing of the day, he must make it up.

**Mentioning the permissibility for the ill of what confronts them:**

Allah Almighty said: "Fasting is prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may fear God — for a fixed number of days… and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days." As was clarified in the chapter preceding this one, it is not apparent that Allah Almighty intends by this only those who travel during the month of Ramadan; it is equally obligatory upon those who are sick in it. Abu Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, was asked about the extent of sickness that entitles the fasting person to break the fast. He said: this is entrusted to the sick person himself — if fasting harms him, there is no objection to breaking the fast; and if it does not harm him, he should fast.


<!-- page 129 -->
Let him break his fast. Whatever harms a sick person through fasting is grounds for breaking the fast, and the Commander of the Believers, 'Ali ibn Abi Talib, may Allah's prayers be upon him, ruled regarding the sick person who cannot fast during the month of Ramadan that he must feed one poor person for every day half a sa' of food. He, may Allah's prayers be upon him, further specified that the sick person who may despair of recovering from his illness — such as one afflicted with leprosy or a chronic ailment — must feed the poor, just as the elderly sheikh who is unable to fast must also feed the poor, since he too may despair of ever being able to make up the missed days. However, one who knows that he may recover from his illness and regain the ability to fast must make up the missed days when he is older and able to fast. As for the pregnant woman and the breastfeeding woman, they are treated in the same manner as a sick person: if either is unable to fast, she breaks her fast, even if she persists in her condition until another month of Ramadan arrives. If she is then able to fast, she fasts and also feeds the poor for the days she broke her fast prior to that Ramadan; if she is still unable to fast, she breaks that Ramadan's fast as well and makes up the first missed fast days through feeding the poor and the second through fasting. All of this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them. They, may Allah's prayers be upon them, loved that whoever dies with obligatory fasting owed should have it made up on his behalf. They also said, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that a woman who menstruates during the month of Ramadan and breaks her fast is obligated to make up what she missed. Whoever owes make-up fasting for days missed in the month of Ramadan may spend those days however he loves — consecutively or separately — though Allah Almighty has commanded. They, may Allah's prayers be upon them, disliked spending the make-up days of Ramadan during Dhu al-Hijjah, since that is a month of asceticism, and they preferred to hasten the make-up of missed Ramadan days as close as possible to the month of Ramadan itself, though nothing is incumbent on one who delays that make-up as long as it falls before the next Ramadan comes. If the next Ramadan does arrive and the make-up has not yet been performed, then feeding the poor is additionally required, as we have mentioned before.

<!-- page 130 -->
**On Breaking the Fast from Fasting:**

Allah Almighty said: *"Then complete the fast until the night."* [Al-Baqarah: 187]

The night begins with the setting of the disc — that is, the disappearance of the sun below the horizon at sunset, with no barrier obstructing it. If there is a barrier, then the sign of the setting of the disc is the darkening of the eastern horizon and the arrival of night from that direction.

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that they loved hastening the breaking of the fast and hated delaying it. They instructed that one break the fast after the Maghrib prayer as promptly as possible if one is able to do so and has food available, and that one should not remain fasting unnecessarily. They said: if food is prepared and the sun has set while a man is present for prayer, and the food of the fasting person is set before him, he should not rush to the food; rather, he should first rise and pray, then eat — so that he may pray on time. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, would say when breaking his fast: "O Allah, for You we have fasted, and upon Your provision we have broken our fast; accept from us the thirst of the day, for the veins are full and the reward remains, God willing."

They also said, may peace be upon them, that if the crescent moon of Ramadan is sighted during the daytime — whether at the beginning of the day or at its end — the people should not break their fast until night, for that day is counted as a day of the month of Ramadan. Similarly, if the crescent is sighted at the end of the month during the daytime, the same ruling applies. Likewise, if the crescent of the new month is seen during the daytime and had not been sighted the night before, then that day should not be fasted, since the day of the month of Ramadan begins only when the crescent is sighted on the night of that day.

**On the Mention of Laylat al-Qadr:**

Allah Almighty said: *"Indeed, We sent it down on the Night of Power"* — to the end of the surah. He, Blessed and Exalted, also said: *"Indeed, We sent it down on a blessed night"* and *"By the clear Book."*

<!-- page 131 -->
*"Blessed are We who were warners; in it every wise matter is distinguished — a command from Us."* Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said concerning the words of Allah Almighty — *"The angels descend and the Spirit descends in it by permission of their Lord"* — that the angels and the scribes descend to the lower heaven by the permission of their Lord, and they write down what is in the Sunnah and what is to befall the servants. The matter has a station in which Allah exercises His will: He gives what He wills and delays what He wills, and He confirms what He wills in the Mother of the Book. Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said concerning the words of Allah Almighty — *"in it every wise matter is distinguished"* — that on that night the command of the Sunnah and all that it contains is written.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, was asked about Laylat al-Qadr and said: "Seek it in the last ten days of the month of Ramadan." He also said, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family, that on Laylat al-Qadr the delegation writes down every year all the wise matters that are to be distinguished. Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, gave a sign of the Night of Power: the wind blows on that night, and if it is cold it turns warm, and if it is hot it turns cool. He also said that the Commander of the Believers, may Allah's prayers be upon him, related that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, would awaken his family during the last ten days of Ramadan, tighten his waistband, and pass the nights in prayer standing. Ja'far ibn Muhammad 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, explained that "tightening his waistband" means that he withdrew from his wives.

**On Mentioning the Sunnah and Supererogatory Fasting, from Majlis al-Bayan:**

Fasting is divided into four categories: obligatory fasting, Sunnah fasting, forbidden fasting, and voluntary fasting. The obligatory category has seven divisions: fasting the month of Ramadan every year; fasting two consecutive months for one who deliberately breaks a fast on one day and cannot find a slave to manumit; fasting two consecutive months for one who kills a believer by mistake when he cannot find a slave to manumit while paying blood money to the deceased's family; fasting two consecutive months

<!-- page 132 -->
in the expiation of zihar for one who cannot find a slave to manumit; fasting three days in expiation for a violated oath for one who cannot find food, clothing, or a slave to manumit; fasting for the one who combines 'Umrah with Hajj in a tamattu' pilgrimage if he cannot find a gift offering — which is ten days; and fasting by vow. As for Sunnah fasting, it has twelve divisions: the fast of i'tikaf; the fast of Sha'ban; fasting every Thursday of every month; fasting the three white days; fasting on Friday and Thursday — or Saturday — without singling out Friday alone for fasting; fasting in the manner of Dawud, peace be upon him, which is one day of fasting followed by one day of breaking the fast, and which is the most rigorous form of fasting; fasting on the day of 'Arafah for one who is not a pilgrim; fasting for a boy when he reaches nine years of age; fasting as an expiation for hunting; and fasting as an expiation for shaving the head.

As for forbidden fasting, it has ten divisions: the first is the fast of Eid al-Fitr; the second is the fast of Eid al-Nahr; the third, fourth, and fifth are the fasts of the three days of Tashriq; the sixth is the fast of wisal, which is fasting two or more consecutive days without breaking the fast; the seventh is fasting in perpetuity; the eighth is fasting while menstruating; the ninth is fasting by a vow made in sin; and the tenth is fasting in doubt, since it is not permissible to fast except with certainty. As for voluntary fasting, it has twelve divisions — in each of which a person has a choice: if he wishes he may fast, and if he wishes he may break his fast. These are: fasting in Rajab; fasting the days of Shawwal after Eid al-Fitr, during which fasting one month is equivalent to fasting Ramadan; fasting nine days of Dhu al-Hijjah, which are the ten days; fasting on the day of 'Ashura' by refraining from food and drink without holding the belief that such fasting is superior, since that resembles grief more than devotion; fasting the month of Muharram, for it is one of the sacred months; fasting for the sick and the traveler, since they have the option of fasting or breaking the fast; fasting on Monday; and fasting the ear — which includes matters such as: a woman may not fast voluntarily except with the permission of her husband; a slave may not fast voluntarily except with the permission of his master; and a guest may not fast voluntarily except with the permission of his host. Fasting also includes refraining from the words that Allah Almighty has mentioned, as He has narrated from Maryam when He said: *"Eat and drink and be gladdened; and if you see any human being, say"*

<!-- page 133 -->
*"'I have vowed to the Most Merciful a fast, so I will not speak today.'"* It was in forgetfulness, and He instructed her to eat, drink, and speak — because, as this indicates, the words that were forbidden were some words only, while others remain permissible, just as it is permissible to speak in prayer with the supplications of Allah, as I have mentioned in the chapters on prayer.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, did not bind himself to a prayer that was not prescribed, yet the Imams from his progeny, may Allah's prayers be upon them, bound themselves to certain prayers and commanded their Shi'a to observe them as a matter of encouragement and persuasion, not because they constitute a binding obligation that cannot be left. Similarly, prayer is obligatory, and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, bound himself to fasting two months a year beyond the month of Ramadan. Likewise, the Imams from his progeny, may Allah's prayers be upon them, bound themselves to certain fasts and encouraged their followers to observe them as a matter of exhortation and encouragement, not as something that must not be abandoned. As for the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, it has been narrated from the Imams that he, may Allah's prayers be upon him, would fast until it was said of him that he does not break his fast, and then he would break his fast until it was said of him that he does not fast. He used to fast Muharram, Rajab, and Sha'ban; he used to fast one day and break the fast the next; he used to fast Monday and Thursday; and he used to fast on the white nights, which are the thirteenth day of the month and the two days after it. These nights are called the white nights because the moon appears in them from the beginning of the night until its end, as though they are aglow with moonlight. He, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family, further prescribed that if one fasts Sha'ban and three additional days — namely a Thursday each month: the first Thursday of the month, a Thursday at the end of it which is the last Thursday, and a Wednesday in the middle closest to the midpoint — then those days amount to two months, equivalent in reward to the obligatory fast. For this reason he, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his family, was eager to observe it. It has also been narrated from Abu 'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, that he fasted Rajab, Sha'ban, and the month of Ramadan, connecting them together. He said, may peace be upon him, that Noah, peace be upon him, boarded

<!-- page 134 -->
the ship on the first day of Rajab and ordered those with him from the jinn and humans to fast. He, may peace be upon him, said: fasting one day of Rajab keeps the fire at a distance of a year's journey; fasting two days keeps the fire two years away; and with each additional day of fasting the fire recedes further, until when he fasts seven days, the seven gates of Hell are closed before him; if he fasts eight days, the eight gates of Paradise are opened to him; if he fasts ten days, his request is granted; if he fasts fifteen days, he is told to begin anew; and whoever increases beyond that, Allah increases his reward.

Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's prayers be upon him, was asked about fasting on the day of 'Ashura', and he said: that practice predates the obligatory fast of the month of Ramadan. Abu 'Abdullah Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, also said: the Ark came to rest on Mount Judi on the day of 'Ashura', and so Noah, peace be upon him, ordered those with him to fast that day. He also said: it is the day on which Allah Almighty accepted the repentance of Adam and Eve; it is the day the sea was parted for the Children of Israel and Pharaoh was drowned; it is the day on which Ibrahim, peace be upon him, was born; it is the day on which Allah Almighty accepted the repentance of the people of Yunus, peace be upon him; it is the day on which 'Isa ibn Maryam, peace be upon them, was born; and it is the day on which Allah Almighty honored al-Husayn, may Allah's blessings be upon him, with martyrdom. It is also the day on which the Qa'im from his progeny shall rise. He said — Imam al-Mu'izz li-Din Allah, may Allah's blessings and peace be upon him — that the day of 'Ashura' is a day of the greatness of Allah, His Messenger, and His guardians, by virtue of the merit and proof contained within it. Yet the enemies of Allah's guardians — from the Umayyads and the progeny of Marwan — upon receiving that day in violation of the prohibitions of Allah and the religion of Allah, and after killing the sons of the Messenger of Allah, may peace be upon him, made it a day of pleasure and humiliation rather than a day of forgiveness and righteous works in accordance with the Sunnah of Allah and the Sunnah of His Prophet. They persisted in the prohibitions of Allah and His sins. It has been narrated from the Imams, may Allah's prayers be upon them, that they loved fasting on that day for whoever knew of its virtue, except for one who feared that fasting would weaken him from standing in prayer. They also disliked singling out Friday for fasting unless one fasted the day before or after it as well.


<!-- page 135 -->
He said, "Whoever fasts on Friday, it is as if he fasted for the period between the two Fridays," meaning that if a day arrives and he does not single it out for fasting. He, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, said he would not accept from whoever had an obligatory fast outstanding that they perform supererogatory fasting until the obligatory fast is fulfilled. They are permitted, however: whoever fasts voluntarily may break his fast, unless the sun has already set without his having made it up, in which case he breaks his fast after sunset and must make up that day. He forbade fasting on the day of al-Fitr, the day of al-Adha, and the three days of al-Tashreeq. As for the fast of al-wasal — whereby the fasting person continues for two days or more without breaking the fast — this is forbidden.

**On I'tikaf:**

I'tikaf is the practice of remaining and residing in a mosque. Allah Almighty said: "And do not engage in relations with them while you are in retreat in the mosques" [al-Baqarah: 187], referring to women. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, used to observe i'tikaf in the last ten days of Ramadan, obligating himself to remain in the mosque day and night. He used to say that i'tikaf in those ten days is equivalent in reward to two Hajj and two Umrahs. He would remain standing through those nights, may Allah bless him and his family, and would bathe every night between the two evening prayers.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, observed i'tikaf in the first ten days of Ramadan, then in the middle ten days, and then in the final ten days. 'Ali, upon whom be the prayers of Allah, said that the one who undertakes i'tikaf must remain in the mosque day and night, occupying himself with prayer, recitation of the Quran, and the remembrance of Allah Almighty. He must not preoccupy himself with conversation except in what is necessary, must not conduct trade — neither selling nor buying — must not recite poetry, attend a funeral, visit the sick, enter a house, seclude himself with a woman, speak in whispers, touch anyone, nor cease speaking with people except for what is beneficial.

It was narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may the prayers of Allah be upon him, that he said: "There is no i'tikaf except with fasting, and

<!-- page 136 -->
no i'tikaf except in a mosque where the congregational prayer is held. The one in i'tikaf does not pray in his house, does not receive women visitors, does not sell, does not buy, does not leave the mosque except for something necessary, and does not sit anywhere until he returns." The same rules apply to a woman in i'tikaf, except that if she menstruates she must leave the mosque. The minimum duration of i'tikaf is three days.

**On Hajj and What Is Stated Concerning It:**

Allah Almighty said: "And proclaim the Hajj among the people; they will come to you on foot and on every lean camel; they will come from every distant mountain pass" [al-Hajj: 27]. It was narrated from Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may Allah's prayers be upon him, from his father, that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said: When Allah Almighty commanded Ibrahim, peace be upon him, to call the people to Hajj, he ascended Mount Safa, placed his finger in his ear, and called out as Allah Almighty had commanded him. His call was heard by those in the east and in the west — even those yet unborn in the loins of their fathers — and they responded to him. Whoever fulfills Hajj once has responded once, whoever fulfills it twice has responded twice, and whoever fulfills it more times has responded accordingly.

Ja'far ibn Muhammad, may the prayers of Allah be upon him, said: "From this was the Talbiyah made."
